#2959. How Al Horford's ‘strange blessing' made him a 3-point pioneer, 20-year NBA vet

How Al Horford's 'strange blessing' made him a 3-point pioneer, 20-year NBA vet originally appeared on NBC Sports Bay Area

Programming note: Al Horford's interview with Bonta Hill and Dalton Johnson on the "Dubs Talk" podcast debuts Friday morning, and can be found HERE.

Running diagonally across the left collarbone of Warriors jerseys throughout the 2026-27 NBA season is a yellow "Nellie" band honoring the late coaching great Don Nelson, who died at 86 years old on Aug. 9.

Invisible further down the left side over the chest of Warriors jerseys is a badge of honor only Al Horford can see. He carries the rarity of making it to 20 years in the NBA deep inside his heart. This season, Horford became the 13th player in NBA history to reach the milestone, and at 40 years old, he is the oldest ever to play in a Warriors jersey.

Mike Budenholzer coached Horford his final three seasons with the Atlanta Hawks from 2013 to 2016. Those were Horford's seventh, eighth and ninth NBA seasons. The thought of Horford ever reaching 20 seasons is instantly met with a "no" from Budenholzer.

But then Budenholzer begins immediately with "Well …" and the same praise that everybody around the league has for Horford follows.

"In some ways I can because he takes such great care of himself," Budenholzer says. "He is so meticulous with his body and his nutrition and diet and such a pro. But, I mean … 20 years. Somebody just a couple weeks ago told me he's getting to 20. I was like, holy s–t."

"But I didn't know there's only 13 guys that have done it. That is super cool, and I'm just happy for him. He's just one of the all-time great guys."

Former Florida Gators teammate Corey Brewer isn't surprised by Horford's longevity. Brewer and Horford won back-to-back national championships together in college, and were both top picks in the 2007 draft. Horford is one of three players remaining from their draft class, along with Kevin Durant and Mike Conley, and Brewer last played in 2020 when he suited up in five games for the Sacramento Kings.

"I can believe it," Brewer said. "That man takes care of himself, and like I said, Al's always doing the right thing. He would be one the guy [to play this long]."

Their college coach isn't surprised, either.

Billy Donovan watched Horford apply his lessons into his own talent and grow offensively over three years at Florida. Up close as Horford's opposing head coach in Oklahoma City and Chicago, Donovan has witnessed his evolution as a pioneer in basketball's big man revolution behind the 3-point line.

He'd be the first to jokingly give Horford trouble for making threes against him, and Donovan, now in his first season as the lead assistant coach for the San Antonio Spurs, is first in line to still praise him to this day.

"I am not one bit surprised that Al individually has had a Hall of Fame career, and I'm not surprised that collectively on a team he's impacted winning at such a high level, because he did it in college," Donovan said in an exclusive interview an hour before the Warriors' blowout loss to the Spurs on Nov. 13.

Lasting this long in the NBA is most special to Horford because of how much change the game has undergone.

Horford is an undersized center at 6-foot-8, and has always been a presence as a rim protector. He moved faster than most big men early in his career, outrebounded them and hit mid-range jumpers over them. Horford found early success making the playoffs as a rookie, and was named to his first two All-Star teams in his third and fourth seasons with the Hawks.

Between his All-Star seasons in 2010 and 2011, Horford averaged 14.7 points, 9.6 rebounds, 2.9 assists and 1.1 blocks per game, and shot 55.4 percent from the field. Horford also shot a total of five 3-pointers in those two seasons, and made two.

Offense was played within 15 feet for Horford when he first became a pro. It was all paint, and mid-range at most. Shooting threes wasn't even a thought, until it had to be.

Bringing Budenholzer to Atlanta put an even bigger emphasis on the Hawks shooting threes. Much more so for big men like Horford. Through his first six seasons, Horford attempted 18 recorded threes. He took 11 in his first 29 games trying to adjust under Budenholzer in the 2013-14 season, before his first long-term injury sidelined him the rest of the year.

The injury derailed the Hawks' hopes. They were the No. 3 seed in the Eastern Conference when Horford sustained a torn pectoral in a double-overtime win where he scored 25 points to go with eight rebounds, four assists, two blocked shots and one made three on his only attempt.

Without Horford, the Hawks went 22-31 and finished as the No. 8 seed in the East. Without the injury, Horford might never make it to Year 20 as someone who changed his game for the betterment of the team, and extended his own career.

"As I started to be challenged as a player, like, hey, the league is changing, I had a GM with the Hawks, Rick Sund, and after my first major injury, I tore my pectoral muscle, he was like, 'Listen, you're a physical player. You want to play down low, but you're not gonna make it playing like that. These guys are bigger than you; they're stronger. You have to start expanding your game. You have to start shooting the ball.'

"It was hard for me to understand," Horford remembers on the latest episode of the Dubs Talk podcast. "But I was like, I'm gonna start trying it, practicing it, and it felt really unnatural shooting the three ball."

Horford began shooting from the corners and progressed from there. Sund didn't draft Horford. He became the Hawks' general manager before Horford's second season, and their conversations about expanding his game really began when Sund transitioned into a senior advisor role in Atlanta.

Sund remembers talking Horford into taking threes from the corner after a practice as a point to prove he had the ability to make the shots. Horford already was such a great all-around player to Sund, but he wanted him to find any advantage to not be left behind and prolong his career with a combination of skills and high IQ.

"You can quote me on this: He's certainly Hall of Fame worthy," Sund says. "There's no question in my mind."

But Sund makes sure to credit Budenholzer as a "progressive" and "innovative" coach who was willing to collaborate, and came into Horford's life at just the right time.

"I think he saw where Al could really kind of blossom or be really good in that type of system," Budenholzer says of his conversations with Sund. "All of us together, putting our heads together, that was what was cool about Atlanta – a lot of great teamwork and at all different levels from front office to coaches to players, a lot of good synergy and a lot of good things happened."

As Horford rested and rehabbed his torn pec the rest of the 2013-14 season, he watched the game grow from a new perspective and connected on a higher level alongside Budenholzer, who then was a first-time head coach at 44 years old. Despite the loss of Horford, the Hawks kept growing as a team and buying into a unique offensive style.

All of a sudden, Horford was watching power forward Paul Millsap shoot 212 threes. Pero Antic, a 6-foot-10 center from Macedonia, played two NBA seasons. They coincided with Budenholzer's first two years in Atlanta, and Antic took 171 threes his first season and 173 his second.

The Hawks spread out 7-foot-2 center Roy Hibbert, who finished second in Defensive Player of the Year voting, and pushed the No. 1-seeded Indiana Pacers to seven games. Horford saw the game from a different lens, beginning the next phase of his career being influenced by Budenholzer.

"I've always wondered if there was a strange blessing, particularly in just buy-in and really kind of appreciating what we were trying to do and what we could accomplish, that there was maybe some strange blessing for him to be injured that year, and for him to watch and see it," Budenholzer now says.

"He's so good, he's such a good player. We weren't going to do anything without him, but I think it was fun for him to watch. That team was just fun from Jump Street. First year, second year, third. It was, but him watching it, I think there was some strange blessing. I think it helped his buy-in, I guess, is a long way of saying it."

Horford recognizes Sund as the first person to get through to him on shooting threes, Budenholzer as the person who instilled confidence and encouraged him to let it fly, and Kenny Atkinson as the person who kept him mentally strong in transforming his game.

Atkinson was an assistant coach all three years Budenholzer coached Horford with the Hawks. They worked closely together, and as the Hawks pushed Horford to shoot more threes, Atkinson sent Horford articles about how Chris Bosh overcame mental obstacles going from an elite mid-range shooter to a dangerous threat from downtown.

The care that Atkinson showed Horford still resonates. So does looking at Budenholzer on the sidelines after taking a three and getting the nod of approval.

"One minute to go, tight game, it wasn't probably the best shot. I was taking it," Horford says. "He really instilled that trust in me, and that's how my confidence started to grow. From that point, I was like, I can actually do this.

"A lot of guys I saw that were coming up with me, they wanted to stay with the way that they were successful at the mid-range, and then you could see that three, four years later, then they started trying to do it, and by that point it was a little late. So, for me, I was very fortunate that I was able to make that transition early."

From a player perspective, nobody was better for Horford than Kyle Korver. The two became best friends during their four years as teammates in Atlanta. Korver is a master of his craft shooting the 3-pointer, and he set the tone for the Hawks by showing up early in the morning, caring in the weight room and paying attention to detail in his shot.

Korver noticed Horford's willingness to step out and shoot threes. Horford can still recall a day Korver, in passing, told him he liked how his 3-point shot was looking, which was gospel to his ears. Budenholzer says it also clicked for Korver, who made 2,450 threes in his 17-year career at a 42.9 percent clip, that others around him shooting threes can make life easier for himself.

"There's the Steph Currys of the world, and then I think there's probably four or five of them, and Kyle's in that group," Budenholzer says. "Al, just being around Kyle and watching it, and them talking – it was fun.

"They'd come up with little ideas and little wrinkles, different angles of screens, just like Draymond and Steph do. I think that it's always great when the players start taking ownership and they start figuring things out. And I think Kyle and Al were very much participants in that."

Horford was named to his third All-Star team in Budenholzer's second season as the Hawks' head coach. The Hawks won a franchise-record 60 games and Budenholzer won Coach of the Year. That season planted the seeds of a new Horford, nearly a decade into his career.

He went 11-of-36 on threes in the 2014-15 season after a combined 10-of-29 in the first seven years of his career, which Horford says were mostly just heaves. But the Hawks were swept by LeBron James and the Cleveland Cavaliers in the conference finals. Year 9 marked Horford's real transformation. He could have never guessed it would be his last with Budenholzer as his coach.

In the Hawks' 10th game of the 2015-16 season, Horford made four of his six threes in a 26-point performance to beat the New Orleans Pelicans by eight points. That night gave him 36 3-point attempts on the season, the same amount he finished the previous year with.

The 3-point shot was no longer an afterthought for Horford. It was now a main part of his game, and has changed his play in the second decade of an illustrious career. Horford again was an All-Star, played all 82 games and took 256 threes in the 2015-16 season, which now is the sixth-most of his career.

"It was pretty remarkable making that jump," Horford says.

Bosh in the same season shot 222 threes in 53 games. It was the last season of his Hall of Fame career because of problems with blood clots.

Dirk Nowitzki was the league's MVP the season before Horford was drafted third overall, and he shot 2.2 threes a game as a 7-foot marvel in 2006-07. Less than a full decade later, Horford took 3.1 threes per game and made more than Bosh ever did. Everything stemmed from a slogan Budenholzer lives by.

"There was a mantra that we were gonna let the motherf–ker fly," Budenholzer said.

Serving as an assistant to Gregg Popovich for 17 seasons highly influenced Budenholzer as a head coach. The two-time Coach of the Year calls himself "a big believer in confidence." He tries to give his players a roadmap that starts with a lot of instruction and structure, but then gives them lots of freedom and a ton of confidence to play.

"I think a good shot in the first couple minutes is the same as a good shot in the last couple minutes," Budenholzer says. "I'm not saying that's right. I'm probably in the minority, but I've kind of lived my life that way. You're open, you shoot it.

"And I think basketball and offenses really stall out and get hard when you pass up open shots, and whether it's the beginning of the game or the end of the game, you're open, you got to shoot."

All these years later, Budenholzer understands why Horford struggled with the change at first, yet was so coachable. It speaks to who he is.

"He's so unselfish, so that was probably one of the hard things," Budenholzer says. "And as a team, we played very unselfish too."

"So there were times where he's like, 'Is that OK?' And I'm like, 'No, you're open, shoot it.' And everybody's got to have kind of a similar balance of just you shoot it, but we still play unselfish. We still move the ball. We still move without the ball. You know, the kind of fundamental stuff. But yeah, it was fun watching him look over at me to see if it was OK."

Horford's final season playing for Budenholzer was his first of attempting at least 200 threes in 10 of his next 11 seasons. The one season he missed the mark, Horford shot 152 threes as a member of the Oklahoma City Thunder in just 28 games of the 2020-21 season. Horford attempted a career-high 5.4 threes per game in that span and made 36.8 percent of them.

He even made it to 200 3-point attempts in only 45 games with the Warriors last season at 39 years old.

Horford's four consecutive threes in the fourth quarter of the Warriors' play-in tournament win over the LA Clippers were a testament to how the big man has evolved. A decade after Budenholzer became his head coach in Atlanta, Horford made 35 threes in the playoffs for the Boston Celtics in their 2024 championship run, including eight in the NBA Finals over five games.

This last January, Horford joined Nowitzki and James as the three players in history to have made 1,000 threes, blocked 1,000 shots and grabbed 9,000 rebounds for a career.

"Once I started to understand that it wasn't only shooting, what it was doing to the rest of the floor and how it was opening our offense, then it even gave me more confidence," Horford says.

But the Hawks were swept again by James' Cavs one round earlier in the conference semifinals. Not everyone saw eye-to-eye internally after that, and Horford signed a four-year contract to sign with the Celtics in the offseason for his first stint in Boston. Budenholzer only lasted two more seasons in Atlanta.

The Hawks ranked second, seventh and seventh again in 3-point attempts per game during Budenholzer's three years as Horford's head coach. They dropped to 16th the year after when the Hawks tried to replace Horford with Dwight Howard.

"Atlanta made a big mistake in letting him go," Sund says. "I can't believe we just let him go to Boston."

Now, Horford has taken on a mentorship role for big men across the NBA. Players began coming to him asking how he made the transition as basketball went through its 3-point boom. His advice has been to "read the room." Horford feels he could have shot more sometimes, but his awareness of his teammates around him gives him the ability to know when it's worth it or not.

He has seen and felt how basketball ebbs and flows. Horford played for a championship team that shot more threes than anyone, and he points out that the Thunder (10th in attempts) and New York Knicks (12th in attempts) weren't recent long-distance juggernauts. The 40-year-old isn't blind to the amount of threes teams are taking, including the Warriors, but believes balance always solves itself.

As Horford explains, "The way that we look at things is quality shots, good shots. If it's a three, if it's a good shot, take it. We don't want to just shoot a three just to shoot them." They're here to stay, and with that comes new challenges.

"The new thing, it's really going to be those long rebounds, those offensive rebounds, and that changes the complexity of the game," Horford explains. "That changes transition. There's a lot of things that kind of go hand in hand with that."

Horford played Nowitzki in his NBA debut, and Rasheed Wallace his second game, two giants among floor-spacing big men. In his fifth career game, Kevin Garnett went to work against him in the paint and from everywhere inside the 3-point line for 27 points on 12-of-16 shooting.

The most taxing part of Horford's career is now, not then, and it's because of how the game is played rather than solely his age.

Defenses could get set much easier at the start of Horford's career. Now a player starts at the 3-point line, runs in for the missed shot, doesn't get it and has to run back to try and catch a guy that has already taken off. Imagine doing so at 6-8, 260 pounds and with 20 NBA seasons of wear and tear.

"It's more demanding physically. It's a whole 'nother component," Horford says. "And that's why it's gonna be interesting to see. I think the teams that have more depth are gonna probably thrive a little more because everybody, they're sending them. These teams, that's how we're playing. We're playing fast, and it's really just playing fast, no concepts, and that's kind of how it is."

Learning defense is Horford's first non-negotiable for the younger generation. Next, without a doubt, is developing an outside jump shot.

Choosing between the two can be difficult for a five-time All-Star who has made an All-Defensive team and made over 100 3-pointers three times, in Years 16, 17 and 18 of his career.

So, is swatting a shot or swishing a three more satisfying to Horford?

"I mean, I'm a shooter now," he says. "I like shooting the three. I like shooting the three, but there is a really good feeling when you get to block a shot. I'm still a defender, I think, at the core.

"When I get to block a shot, it feels good."

Horford has enjoyed the satisfaction of blocking a shot 300 more times than he has making a three. It's obvious which of the two had a bigger part in extending his playing days to two decades, and it all started with the strange blessing of an injury that let him sit by Bud for a season.

"Yeah, I love that guy," Budenholzer says. "He and I text occasionally. I'm not the best texter. The great thing I will say, he's always been one of the best guys after a game coming down to my locker room. Two minutes, five minutes, 15 minutes – just catching up, talking.

"He's got a very, very special place in my heart, my life, and hopefully, I do the same for him. He's the best."

#2955. Top Wall Street analysts are bullish on these 3 stocks for the long haul

The ongoing earnings season, Middle East tensions and buzz ahead of the U.S. midterm elections have been keeping investors busy. Further, worries about elevated artificial intelligence spending are weighing on investor sentiment.

Investors looking beyond the short-term noise and focusing on long-term returns can track top Wall Street analysts to pick the right stocks, backed by strong research.

Here are three stocks favored by some of Wall Street's top pros, according to TipRanks, a platform that ranks analysts based on their past performance.

This week's first stock pick is Datadog, an AI-powered observability and security platform. The company will announce its results for the third quarter on Nov. 4. Datadog is seeing strong demand for its offerings, thanks to cloud migration and rapid AI adoption. TipRanks' KPIs tool for DDOG shows the growth in its customer base over the past few years amid increasing market penetration and growing adoption of its products.

Heading into Q3 FY26 earnings, Bank of America analyst Koji Ikeda reiterated a buy rating on Datadog stock and raised his price target to $335 from $305. The analyst noted that industry checks and data indicate that infrastructure software demand is strengthening, with AI increasingly driving usage and growth.

Ikeda called Datadog and Atlassian his top picks, saying that companies that can show that AI is a tailwind to growth could see their valuation rerate, even at premium multiples. He added that the total addressable market for observability is growing faster than anticipated, driven by accelerated growth of enterprise AI and the rapid ramp of consumer AI agents.

The five-star analyst believes that focus will be on revenue growth, which he estimates to be 28.7% for Q3 FY26 versus the Street's consensus of 29.1%. Ikeda thinks that the market is underestimating the growth prospects of DDOG's security products. Meanwhile, he cautioned investors about concentration risk related to large customers.

"We think results will be supportive of our long-term thesis that Datadog is a strong share gainer in the rapidly expanding observability opportunity," said Ikeda.

Ikeda ranks No. 673 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been successful 55% of the time, delivering an average return of 10.9%. See Datadog Statistics on TipRanks.

The next pick, semiconductor giant Nvidia, is also benefiting from AI-driven demand for its products. After attending Nvidia's GTC Berlin conference, Morgan Stanley analyst Joseph Moore reaffirmed a buy rating on NVDA stock with a price target of $300, calling it a top pick in the semiconductor space.

The five-star analyst said that the conference supported his thesis that Nvidia continues to effectively navigate bottlenecks in the supply chain, land/power/shell, and funding. In fact, Moore is "convinced that all 3 aspects are opportunities for NVDA."

While Nvidia's GTC Berlin offered no major updates, Moore noted the conference reflected that the company continues to add to its significant advantages heading into 2027. The analyst said that Vera Rubin is gaining momentum, though details on next year's Rubin Ultra were lighter than expected.

With demand outpacing supply, Moore said that focus is on closing the gap. In this regard, he highlighted the supply chain deals on substrate, packaging and testing. While the analyst continues to view TSMC 3 nm wafer supply as the biggest challenge, he expects it to ease over time.

Moore ranks No. 142 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 59% of the time, delivering an average return of 23.4%. See Nvidia Ownership Structure on TipRanks.

Aerospace company SpaceX recently announced solid third-quarter results, with its AI revenue surging to $7.56 billion from $813 million in the prior-year quarter.

Impressed by the results, Needham analyst Ryan Koontz reiterated a buy rating on SPCX stock with a price target of $250. Koontz highlighted that SpaceX's Q3 results significantly exceeded expectations, proving that the company's vertically integrated business model is driving massive scale.

Furthermore, Koontz emphasized the 195.2% sequential growth in SpaceX's AI revenue, fueled by wholesale spot compute deals. He noted management's increasing focus on its AI strategy, given that it is optimistic about adding 10 gigawatts of incremental capacity during 2027.

"With increasing confidence on Starship re-use, we meaningfully increase our estimates," said Koontz.

Koontz ranks No. 357 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 42% of the time, delivering an average return of 23.7%. See SpaceX Technical Analysis on TipRanks.

#2951. Why Al Horford returned to Warriors after injury-riddled first season with team

Why Al Horford returned to Warriors after injury-riddled first season with team originally appeared on NBC Sports Bay Area

Al Horford had more left in the tank ahead of his 20th NBA season.

Horford surprised some fans by re-signing with the Warriors on a two-year, $13.9 million contract that includes a player option for the 2027-28 NBA season.

After all, Horford seemingly contemplated retiring from the NBA following an injury-riddled 2025-26 season that saw the 40-year-old miss 37 games.

Speaking to NBC Sports Bay Area's Bonta Hill and Dalton Johnson on "Dubs Talk," Horford explained why he ultimately decided to return to Golden State.

"I feel like last year wasn't what we are about as the Warriors," Horford said. "I felt like Steph [Curry] couldn't do what he could do. He had injuries. Coach [Steve Kerr] was dealing with a lot, playing different guys all over the place. I was like, 'I want to give it another shot with the group that we have, and I think if we are healthy at the right time and we're playing the right way, I want to see what that looks like.' So for me, after we lost to [the] Phoenix [Suns], that was what was going through my mind, and I was ready to hit start again, so that's where that commitment comes from, and I want to see it through."

Horford mentioned he had "high expectations" for the Warriors when he originally arrived from the Boston Celtics, but pointed to season-ending injuries to Jimmy Butler and Moses Moody as incidents that changed the team's dynamic.

Butler and Moody's presumed return around the All-Star break leaves the Warriors in what Horford believes will be a "good position" by season's end.

Horford's original decision to leave Boston for Golden State was similarly unexpected, partly because of the Celtics' history with the Warriors during Horford's seven seasons with them.

The five-time All-Star selection was a part of the 2021-22 Celtics team that lost to the Warriors in the NBA Finals in six games before finally winning his first championship in the 2023-24 NBA season against the Dallas Mavericks.

Horford added that before signing with Golden State on his initial two-year, $11.65 million deal last offseason, he did not even look at the Warriors as an "option."

"I was a Celtic through and through, and just committed to it," Horford said. "When I decided that I wasn't going to go back to Boston, and I'm looking at the possibilities, I'm like, 'Really?' I had to have some long conversations with my wife, and we were kind of really talking it through and things like that.

"But I've always respected and been intrigued with the Warrior way and the way that they play here and the things that they do. Playing with Steph, it really puzzled me, and a guy like Draymond [Green] and how he impacts the game. So that got my attention and then looking at how Jimmy came in and had an impact. So I feel like it kind of all came together."

Horford is averaging 3.8 points and 3.6 rebounds in 16.9 minutes per game for the 8-8 Warriors as a backup center, and figures to be an important veteran presence as the season progresses.

#2942. Troubling stats highlight Warriors' struggles during disappointing road trip

Troubling stats highlight Warriors' struggles during disappointing road trip originally appeared on NBC Sports Bay Area

Without a nucleus, a team can't function properly.

Warriors superstar Steph Curry is at the center of everything the Warriors are trying to accomplish this season. The four-time NBA champion is currently sidelined with a right oblique abdominal tear, and Golden State's supporting cast has yet to show any fight against adversity.

Golden State's offense has clearly been stagnant since their best player was injured, and signs don't point to a resolution anytime soon.

The first quarter woes continued throughout the road trip. The Warriors only led in one of the five games, which was early on against the Dallas Mavericks.

The start of the Warriors season has been extremely streaky. They lost the first three out of the gate, then went on an incredible eight-game win streak. Since Curry went down against the Houston Rockets, they haven't won another game.

The defense has a lot of work to do, but the offense is the biggest concern. Unable to break 100 points in four straight games is unacceptable in today's NBA.

Curry will be reevaluated on Thanksgiving, but more help could arrive sooner than that, as guard Brandon Williams, sidelined with a hamstring injury, will be reevaluated this weekend.

The hope is to get Williams back on the court next week, but as long as Curry is out, wins will be hard to come by for Golden State.

Rookie Yaxel Lendeborg has his work cut out for him early into his NBA career. Trying to keep a historic franchise afloat while waiting for Curry to return is a tough task for a 24-year-old.

One positive is that the Warriors return home on Sunday after a disastrous road trip. The bad news is that they'll be facing the 11-4 Boston Celtics.

#2941. AI can help find holiday deals — but it's not great at spotting scams, experts say

Artificial intelligence shopping tools can help consumers compare prices and find gift ideas, but experts say shoppers still need to verify unfamiliar retailers and sellers before buying.

Scammers can use AI to make fake stores, product photos and reviews look convincing. It can also make creating and deploying websites easier, said Leyla Bilge, global head of scam research at cybersecurity company Norton.

"The biggest impact right now we're seeing in AI is on coding," Bilge said. Scammers can register "thousands and thousands of fake e-shops online," she said, which increases the chances shoppers will encounter one.

A Norton survey of 1,000 U.S. adults found that 16% of respondents had been scammed while shopping online during the holidays. Of those victims, 88% lost money, with an average loss of $394. The survey was conducted from an opt-in online panel between Sept. 10 and 21 by Dynata on behalf of Norton's parent company, Gen.

Here's a look at more stories on how to manage, grow and protect your money for the years ahead.

Scams can have impacts beyond financial losses. In a survey of 3,110 U.S. adults released in June, the Global Anti-Scam Alliance found that 82% of respondents had encountered a scam in the past year. More than half, 59%, said the scam affected their mental well-being.

More than half of GASA survey respondents said they had experienced repeat scams.

If you put your address or phone number in an AI tool that turns out to be fraudulent, "you might get reached out to again with a much more sophisticated, much more targeted and personalized attack and scam," Bilge said.

Among respondents who had used an AI shopping assistant or agent, 56% said it had recommended or completed a purchase from a retailer they didn't recognize, the Norton survey found.

An agent focused on finding the lowest price may steer a shopper toward an unfamiliar seller, so experts say shoppers shouldn't treat its recommendation as vetted.

"You absolutely need to check with the retailer directly," said Matt Schulz, chief consumer finance analyst at LendingTree and author of "Ask Questions, Save Money, Make More."

Bilge said many shoppers make purchases on their phones through social media, sometimes with a single click, leaving them little time or incentive to assess where the link goes. Then, some still buy even if they're unsure of the site's legitimacy.

The Norton survey also found that 35% of Americans had bought from a website or seller they thought might be a scam because the deal seemed too good to pass up.

Before buying from a seller or retailer, consumer experts recommend these steps:

While AI can narrow gift options and find the lowest prices, experts say shoppers should verify the store before handing over payment details — and, when possible, pay by credit card. Keep order details and receipts, too.

The FTC says cardholders can dispute charges in certain cases, such as when merchandise never arrives.

— CNBC's Stephanie Dhue contributed reporting to this article.

#2940. Highlights: Area 51 helps San Antonio bounce back with a win over the Clippers

The San Antonio Spurs needed to bounce back after a disappointing loss to the Detroit Pistons on Tuesday night. A back-and-forth contest with the struggling Los Angeles Clippers wasn't exactly the dominant response some might have wanted, but the Spurs pulled out a 124-112 win.

The Spurs needed leading performances from their stars to outlast the Clippers. As they have so many times before, San Antonio relied on Area 51, the duo of Stephon Castle and Victor Wembanyama.

Castle stuffed the stat sheet with 21 points, 12 assists, 6 rebounds, and 4 steals (and 8 turnovers, but that's another story). He was slinging the ball all over the floor, including on this full-court pass that led to a transition "shunk" by Wembanyama.

Yes, you read that right. Shunk. A shot and a dunk.

Wembanyama told reporters after the game that he has a blister that has been hurting his hand on dunks. So when he had the chance, he dunked the ball without touching the rim. So the shunk was born.

Castle's ability to draw two defenders is one of his best skills. Here, he jumps Kris Dunn's pass to Brook Lopez and streaks to the other end, drawing two defenders and kicks it to Julian Champagnie for a wide-open three.

Defense to offense was the name of the game for both teams on Thursday. The Spurs scored 19 points in transition and 25 points off turnovers.

Castle didn't just get it done on the break. He had a balanced game in the half-court, orchestrating the offense for the Spurs with Dylan Harper sitting out. His connection with Wembanyama was on full display on this lob, as he once again drew the attention of multiple players, freeing up the big man for an easy lob.

Wembanyama got to the rim more often than he did against Detroit. The big man had 31 points and 11 rebounds, scoring 22 of those points inside the arc.

Wembanyama added 3 assists, including this sweet wraparound pass to Keldon Johnson. KJ was a key contributor off the bench, adding 10 points, 5 rebounds, 3 assists, and 2 steals.

Perhaps the best play of the night came from another bench player, Harrison Barnes. The veteran got out in transition and rose, using all 6 feet 8 inches of his frame to slam the ball home over the springy shot-blocking Yanic Konan Niederhauser. The "unc" has turned back the clock a few times this season. San Antonio will need him as they wait for Harper and Tobias Harris to heal up and rejoin the lineup.

The defense, particularly down the stretch, helped the Spurs get back into the game. Devin Vassell was a key part of their defensive effort, racking up 3 steals in the win. This is just an all-out effort play. Vassell gets a difficult strip on Darius Garland, grabs the 50/50 ball, and dunks it home in transition.

#2939. One questionable play call may have crushed Rams' Super Bowl chances

The Los Angeles Rams' Super Bowl hopes took a significant hit on Friday when Sean McVay told reporters that Puka Nacua underwent surgery on his MCL and will miss a couple of months. The injury will likely cause Nacua to miss the rest of the season, and while McVay wouldn't rule him out for the playoffs, he's "planning on not having him" the rest of the way.

The worst part is the injury might've been completely avoidable.

It occurred on the Rams' final offensive snap against the Washington Commanders in Week 9. They were leading 20-13 with 1:44 left in the fourth quarter and Washington was out of timeouts.

McVay called a jet sweep to Nacua, his 15th touch of the game, which went for 5 yards on third-and-11. McVay knew the Rams probably weren't going to pick up the first down on that play – only seven of Nacua's 40 career carries have gained at least 11 yards – but it was a way to kill the clock and get the ball into his best player's hands.

Nacua had already touched the ball 14 times before that, taking a beating throughout the game. Even though he's their more reliable offensive player, Nacua didn't need to be the guy touching the ball on that final play. A simple handoff to Kyren Williams or Blake Corum would've done the job.

But McVay tried to get creative and catch the defense off guard by giving it to Nacua in the backfield. It didn't work, and that one virtually meaningless play may have derailed the Rams' season.

Sure, you could argue that the game wasn't over, and the Commanders even made things interesting on the ensuing drive by getting to the Rams' 31-yard line. But any play to keep the clock running would've done the job on third-and-11. It's not as if it was third-and-short where the Rams were likely to move the chains and end the game.

McVay even admitted a day after the game that he was "kicking" himself for that play call and has questioned the decision "over and over," knowing the consequences of losing Nacua. That was almost two weeks ago, before the Rams knew Nacua needed surgery.

"I'm kicking myself about those things," he said. "… I'd be lying to you if I said I haven't questioned the decision making on that last play over and over in my head of what could have been another option that you felt like could have gotten a conversion and maybe end of the game that would have led to him not taking a violent hit to his right knee. It's stuff that I feel tremendous responsibility for, and we have to be able to figure out how to protect these guys while also putting them in positions that help our team and allow them to be able to shine."

Now that he's likely out for the year, it makes the call even worse.

Among the Rams' pass catchers, Nacua has been the most trustworthy and consistent all year. It was already going to be tough for them to make a run to the Super Bowl with him, but the odds are stacked against them now that he's out a couple of months.

Davante Adams will miss at least one more game, as will Terrance Ferguson. Maybe the passing game will kick into gear when those guys return. But for another week, it'll be Konata Mumpfield, CJ Daniels, Tutu Atwell and Xavier Smith leading the receiving corps.

Injuries happen in the NFL, but there are times when coaches can keep their players out of harm's way. That final play against the Commanders was one of those times.

#2938. Intuit to sell Mailchimp to Bending Spoons for $3.68 billion

Tax software maker Intuit said Thursday that it has agreed to sell marketing subsidiary Mailchimp to Italian technology consolidator Bending Spoons for $3.68 billion in cash and stock. The deal is set to close in early 2027, according to a statement.

The sale is a costly retreat for Intuit, which paid $12 billion for Mailchimp in 2021 in a push beyond its core tax and accounting businesses. Five years later, the software maker is unloading the email-marketing company for less than one-third of that price after Mailchimp became a drag on its overall revenue growth.

The deal is the latest instance of Bending Spoons picking up a once promising technology asset. The company bought productivity software startup Airtable in September for 10% of what it was once worth.

The Bending Spoons portfolio also contains AOL, Brightcove, Eventbrite, Evernote, Meetup, Vimeo, and WeTransfer.

In August Intuit started running Mailchimp as a distinct operating segment. The move was meant to give investors more transparency as the company tried to manage the unit for profitability, Sandeep Aujla, Intuit's finance chief, said at a September company briefing.

Also on Thursday, Intuit announced stronger-than-expected earnings and revenue for its fiscal first quarter, which ended on Oct. 31. The company reported $2.91 in adjusted earnings per share on $4.35 billion in revenue. Analysts polled by LSEG were looking for $2.70 per share and $4.31 billion in revenue.

While Intuit's revenue increased 12% year over year, Mailchimp revenue declined 1% to $313 million. Analysts polled by StreetAccount had expected $314.4 million.

Management called for $22.88 to $23.12 in adjusted earnings per share for the 2027 fiscal year, with $23.081 billion to $23.365 billion in revenue. Analysts polled by LSEG had predicted $23.62 in adjusted earnings per share on $23.43 billion in revenue. Intuit shares tumbled 15% in extended trading.

The company said Mailchimp should produce $1.256 billion to $1.266 billion in fiscal 2027 revenue, which would be down 1% to flat year over year. In August Aujla said Intuit anticipated Mailchimp's higher effective prices offsetting increased client churn. Mailchimp was founded in 2001. Rivals include Klaviyo, which went public in 2023.

Berkshire Hathaway invested $600 million in Bending Spoons when the acquirer went public in July, and in October Bending Spoons said Berkshire invested another $330 million.

#2937. Kushner says Russia 'comfortable' with idea of NATO-like security guarantees for Ukraine

U.S. envoy Jared Kushner has said Russia may accept NATO-like security guarantees for Ukraine, adding that President Donald Trump wants to bring an end to Russia's full-scale invasion "with Europe by his side."

His comments come shortly after Trump, Russian President Vladimir Putin and Chinese President Xi Jinping held historic trilateral talks on the sidelines of the Asia-Pacific Economic Cooperation forum, a meeting that did not appear to result in any concessions on Ukraine or European security.

"I think we've got the Russians comfortable with that concept where any security guarantee for Ukraine would be based on Article 5," Kushner said alongside European leaders at a news conference in Germany on Thursday, referring to the part of NATO's treaty that enshrines the principle of collective defense.

"I think that that comes with at least what the Russians are saying, which is their intention is, when this war is over, to not have another war in the future," he added. Kushner, who is Trump's son-in-law, was at the conference in Berlin alongside fellow U.S. envoy Steve Witkoff.

They have led the Trump administration's efforts to end Russia's nearly five-year-long war with Ukraine.

Article 5 defines an attack against one NATO member as an attack against all members. The alliance calls it a "unique and enduring principle that binds its members together, committing them to protect each other and setting a spirit of solidarity."

Ukraine has long held ambitions to join the alliance. Russia has previously said that if Kyiv joined NATO, it would cause problems for many years.

It was not immediately clear what form potential security guarantees would take, or whether Russia or Ukraine would accept them. Russia's Foreign Ministry, Ukraine's Foreign Ministry and NATO were not immediately available to comment when contacted by CNBC on Friday morning.

"From America's perspective, giving an Article 5-like security guarantee is a really, really big deal and I think that that is something that President Trump is open to, but obviously it has to be in America's interest to do that," Kushner said.

Russia launched a wave of missile and drone attacks on Ukraine's energy and transport infrastructure overnight, damaging three bridges in Kyiv and killing at least three people, authorities said.

Kyiv mayor Vitali Klitschko said via Telegram on Friday that Kyiv's Podilskyi, Darnytskyi and Southern crossings had been damaged by missile strikes, with experts currently assessing the damage.

Russia's Defense Ministry said it had carried out a "massive" strike on several Ukrainian cities, targeting enterprises of the country's defense industry and warehouses used in the production and storage of missiles and drones.

Putin vowed on Thursday that Russia would respond to Ukraine's attacks on the Kerch Bridge, adding it would be "much more painful." The bridge, which links Russia to occupied Crimea and is one of Putin's signature projects, was damaged by repeated Ukrainian attacks this week.

Ukrainian President Volodymyr Zelenskyy on Tuesday described it as an "unprecedented operation" several weeks in the making, with Ukraine's defense forces, security services and weapons manufacturers involved.

#2934. Top Wall Street analysts like these 3 dividend stocks for consistent income

Democrats' midterm election win has put the artificial intelligence trade in question, as voters clearly expressed opposition to the rapid buildout of data centers. The stock market could remain volatile amid AI trade concerns, macro uncertainties, and geopolitical tensions.

In this scenario, investors can add dividend-paying stocks to their portfolios to earn steady income. However, the extensive universe of dividend stocks can make selection challenging. Top Wall Street analysts' recommendations can help investors shortlist attractive dividend stocks, as these experts assign ratings after in-depth financial analysis.

Here are three dividend-paying stocks that are highlighted by Wall Street's top pros, as tracked by TipRanks, a platform that ranks analysts based on their past performance.

Viper Energy (VNOM) is a subsidiary of Diamondback and owns and acquires mineral and royalty interests, primarily in the Permian Basin. The company reported upbeat third-quarter earnings, supported by recent acquisitions and continued development activity from Diamondback and third-party operators. Viper announced a base cash dividend of 50 cents per share for Q3 2026, payable on Nov. 19. Based on the base and variable dividends announced over the past 12 months, VNOM's dividend yield stands at 5.7%.

Following the Q3 results, TD Cowen analyst Aaron Bilkoski reiterated a buy rating on Viper Energy stock with a price target of $61. The analyst noted that the company delivered solid Q3 results, with production, including oil and natural gas liquids, exceeding both TD Cowen and consensus estimates.

The 5-star analyst highlighted that the key update was the announcement of two royalty acquisitions for $809 million in cash, including deferred consideration, and $82 million of newly issued shares.

Given the recently announced base dividend hike, Bilkoski expects Viper to direct incremental free cash flow toward buybacks. While the acquisitions increase leverage, the analyst believes that Viper has enough capacity for opportunistic buybacks.

Bilkoski noted that Viper has historically delivered one of the highest production per share growth profiles in his royalty coverage, but still trades at a notable discount to the royalty peer average and the company's historical FCF yield range. "In our view, its above-average growth warrants a higher valuation," said the analyst.

Bilkoski ranks No. 525 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 57% of the time, delivering an average return of 12.80%. See Viper Energy Stock Buybacks on TipRanks.

Exxon Mobil (XOM) reported robust Q3 2026 profits, as its U.S. refineries boosted production to capitalize on the high diesel fuel prices sparked by the conflict in the Middle East. The oil and gas giant announced a quarterly dividend of $1.07 per share, payable on Dec. 10, reflecting a 3.9% increase. This marks Exxon's 44th consecutive year of dividend increases. At an annualized dividend of $4.28 per share, XOM offers a yield of 2.53%.

Impressed by the Q3 print, TD Cowen analyst Jason Gabelman reiterated a buy rating on Exxon stock and raised his price target to $193 from $180 to reflect higher downstream earnings, with the expectation of increased mid-cycle margins.

Gabelman noted that the strength in Exxon's refining business continues to offset Middle East-related headwinds. He added that the company increased its ownership in LNG projects, which should reduce its Middle East concentration over time from 75% to 50% and lower the likelihood of diversification-focused mergers and acquisitions. Beyond LNG, Gabelman expects Exxon's growth in the 2030s to come from Guyana, Kashagan, Permian, and possibly Venezuela.

Gabelman noted that Exxon announced a dividend hike of nearly 4% and plans to maintain buybacks at $20 billion for 2027. "The decision to maintain reduces the pro-cyclicality nature of the buyback," said the analyst.

Gabelman ranks No. 347 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been successful 66% of the time, delivering an average return of 16.90%. See Exxon Mobil Ownership Structure on TipRanks.

Williams (WMB), which owns and operates energy infrastructure to deliver natural gas, reported strong third-quarter results. The company announced a quarterly dividend of $0.525 per share, or $2.10 per share annualized, payable on Dec. 28. WMB offers a dividend yield of 3.09%.

In reaction to the Q3 2026 results, CIBC analyst Robert Catellier reaffirmed a buy rating on Williams stock with a price target of $82. The analyst noted that Q3 results were essentially in line with expectations and reflected continued strength in execution across WMB's infrastructure portfolio.

Catellier said that Williams' Q3 performance was driven by higher transmission, storage, and power generation revenues, as well as contributions from the Momentum Midstream acquisition.

He noted that Williams maintained its full-year EBITDA guidance of $8.3 billion to $8.5 billion. In fact, results are tracking toward the higher end of the guidance range, backed by contributions from recently completed projects and the Momentum Midstream deal. WMB continues to target long-term EBITDA compound annual growth rate of at least 11% through 2030.

Catellier expects WMB's long-term targets to be supported by incremental transmission and storage opportunities, including Transco's Southeast Supply Enhancement II project and an 84 MMcf/d expansion of the Garden Connector project.

Catellier ranks No. 667 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been successful 66% of the time, delivering an average return of 9.60%. See Williams Technical Analysis on TipRanks.

#2930. Some FHA borrowers pay mortgage insurance for a decade or more. There's an industry push to change the rules

Homeowners with conventional loans who pay mortgage insurance typically can shed that expense — meant to protect a lender against loss if the borrower defaults — once they've built sufficient equity in their home. But that's not how it works for those with a Federal Housing Administration loan.

A fresh push to change the rules governing mortgage insurance for FHA loans came this week, after the Department of Housing and Urban Development released a report to Congress on Monday showing that the FHA's mortgage insurance fund remains financially strong. Its capital ratio — a measure of its financial cushion against potential losses — was 10.29% as of Sept. 30, down from 11.47% a year earlier, but still more than five times the 2% minimum required by Congress.

"The program's financial strength provides an opportunity for targeted changes that improve affordability for borrowers while preserving the long-term stability" of FHA's insurance fund, said Bob Broeksmit, president and CEO of the Mortgage Bankers Association, in a statement released after the report was issued. The trade group was one of a handful of organizations that put out statements calling for changes.

The push, which revives a longstanding mortgage industry request, also comes several weeks after President Donald Trump issued an executive order that included calling for the launch of a federal pilot program that would cancel mortgage insurance on certain conventional loans once borrowers meet equity requirements.

The order would apply generally to future loans backed by Fannie Mae and Freddie Mac — two government-sponsored enterprises that purchase qualifying mortgages from lenders and resell them as bundled securities. It wouldn't apply to FHA loans.

FHA mortgages are issued by private lenders but insured by the federal government, which protects lenders against losses if a borrower defaults. The loans are designed to make homeownership more accessible, particularly for borrowers with limited savings for a down payment or with lower credit scores.

Affordability challenges persist in the housing market, with elevated home prices and mortgage rates putting homeownership out of reach for many prospective buyers. Existing homeowners are also facing rising costs alongside their mortgages, including property taxes and homeowners insurance amid overall high inflation.

About 83% of new FHA purchase loans went to first-time homebuyers in the most recent fiscal year, according to the FHA report to Congress. Altogether, the FHA insured more than 8.4 million mortgages as of Sept. 30.

The median age of first-time buyers is 40, according to a new report from the National Association of Realtors.

FHA loans also require borrowers to pay mortgage insurance regardless of the size of their down payment. Under current rules established in 2013, FHA borrowers with a down payment that's less than 10% of the loan must pay mortgage insurance premiums for the duration of the loan. Those who put 10% or more down pay that insurance for 11 years.

Borrowers generally pay both an upfront mortgage insurance premium and an annual premium, which is typically divided into monthly payments. The upfront premium is usually 1.75% of the base loan amount and can be financed into the mortgage. For many borrowers with 30-year FHA loans, the annual premium is 0.55% of the outstanding loan balance.

Conventional mortgages generally don't require mortgage insurance unless the loan is for more than 80% of the home's value. In 2022, the premium typically ranged from 0.58% to 1.86% annually, according to Fannie Mae.

If those borrowers have to pay mortgage insurance, they can typically request its removal once they've built 20% equity in the home, or it is automatically canceled when their loan drops to 78% of the original value.

The mortgage industry has floated two different proposals.

One would reduce the rate that applies to FHA mortgage insurance. For example, the Mortgage Bankers Association recently suggested a rate reduction of 20 basis points, which would lower the standard 0.55% rate to 0.35% for new borrowers.

For example: On a $350,000 mortgage, a 0.55% rate generally translates into $1,925 annually, or about $160 monthly, at the start of the loan. As the principal owed slowly goes down over time through a borrower's monthly payments, so does that mortgage insurance amount.

At 0.35%, the annual premium would be $1,225 at the start of the loan, or $102 monthly — a difference of $58 per month.

The FHA last cut that rate in 2023, when it was reduced from 0.85%. At the time, HUD estimated that affected borrowers would save an average of $800 annually. However, in the FHA's new report to Congress, the agency noted that the 2023 cut has reduced the fund's revenue, contributing to a decline in its capital ratio.

The other industry proposal would implement auto-cancellation of premiums once the loan balance fell to 78% of the home's original value at purchase — in other words, the same as for Fannie- and Freddie-backed mortgages.

"Cancellation prevents the squeeze for borrowers who have paid down their loans and shouldn't be paying for coverage they have outgrown," said Michael Farrell, president of the National Association of Mortgage Brokers, a trade group that generally has supported the cancellation proposal.

"NAMB believes that cancellation at 78% protects the [FHA insurance fund], because premiums can restart if the [capital] ratio ever falls to 2%," Farrell said.

FHA mortgages received that treatment from 2001 through 2013, but the agency eliminated it to shore up its insurance fund after financial reserves deteriorated over time following the housing market crash, which led to the 2008 financial crisis.

Whether the proposals will go anywhere this time is uncertain. HUD, which oversees the FHA, did not respond to a CNBC email seeking more information.

#2928. White House replaces cyber director Sean Cairncross amid widespread tech outages

White House National Cyber Director Sean Cairncross is leaving his post, with Pentagon CIO Kirsten Davies set to serve as acting director after a week of high-profile U.S. technology outages.

The role, which advises the president on cybersecurity and coordinates U.S. cyber policy across federal agencies, has been handed to Davies, the Pentagon's chief information officer, on an interim basis. A permanent national cyber director must first be nominated by the president and then confirmed by the Senate.

Axios first reported Cairncross' exit, citing two White House officials who said President Donald Trump's chief of staff, Stephen Miller, pushed for his departure.

The White House declined to comment. However, a source familiar with the White House's thinking told CNBC that the President fully supported firing Sean Cairncross from his position because he lacked the strong cyber credentials to perform the job. Davies has the credentials to excel in the position and has been Senate-confirmed, the person added.

The Office of the National Cyber Director did not immediately respond to a CNBC request for comment.

It comes after a series of technology outages, including a nationwide aviation ground stop, a worldwide Microsoft Outlook outage and a reported blackout of the National Security Agency's classified communications network.

The White House has insisted that there are no indications of a coordinated cyberattack, state-sponsored activity or malign actor behind recent tech outages.

Davies addressed her responsibilities in the new position and thanked Miller and Trump for their confidence late Thursday.

"Our nation stands in the crossroad of analog, digital, and quantum technologies, our American way of living increasingly interconnected. Our opportunity is to dominate the cyber landscape for generations to come … but only if we modernize and drive resilience into the foundations our great nation runs on," she wrote on X.

Secretary of Defense Pete Hegseth and Secretary of State Marco Rubio both congratulated Davies on her appointment.

#2926. Bitcoin sell-off: Crypto is failing the 'digital gold' test again as it sinks with stocks

Bitcoin's latest sell-off is reviving doubts that it can behave as the "digital gold" market hedge that crypto backers have long envisioned.

Coming into 2026, experts said that showing bitcoin could serve as a market hedge rather than a risk-on asset that moves up and down with growth stocks was an important test for the cryptocurrency.

Bitcoin was trading under $62,000 on Friday morning, down from an all-time high of more than $126,000 in early October last year. Its downward spiral in recent months and weeks has been precipitated by a confluence of factors. The heavy midterm elections losses for the Republicans, the Binance hack, and a world on edge about the security of all things technology — FAA ground stop, the outage of the National Security Agency's highly classified internal communications system, several online banking outages — have not helped the mood when it comes to putting trust in the digital world.

But the market itself may be the biggest factor. Investors are currently in sell everything mode, including tech stocks and gold — the Dow Jones Industrial Average and S&P 500 tumbled into correction territory on Friday, according to CNBC data. Bitcoin's recent price action has also tracked the move in the biggest tech IPOs, SpaceX and Anthropic. Taken together, the cryptocurrency is failing the test again when it comes to showing its worth as a market hedge during times of investor anxiety.

In the past month alone, bitcoin had dropped roughly $20,000, as of Friday morning, according to CNBC data.

Investors and financial management firms aren't viewing it as safe an investment as in the past, said Ryan Kirkley, co-founder and chief executive of Global Settlement Network, a Miami-based fintech that provides infrastructure for currency settlement, including crypto. "There's a bit more caution than there was a year ago."

What is behind bitcoin's fall, and what is in store for its future? Here is what experts say.

There have been many theories about how bitcoin would react based on different market and economic conditions. But historically, bitcoin has done best in low-rate environments and when lending opportunities are plentiful, said Juan Xavier Sanchez, head of wealth strategy at Activest Wealth Management in Aventura, Florida.

When rates are down and treasuries aren't paying good yields, people tend to be more risk-seeking for higher returns. "Bitcoin for many investors is in that bucket," Sanchez said.

Experts have noted that gold, which is now down over 35% from its early 2026 peak, is less attractive when cash accounts are paying 5%. Investments like gold and bitcoin offer no yield.

Bitcoin's rising correlation with the Nasdaq-100 since late September suggests the two are increasingly moving in tandem, according to data from Fairlead Strategies, a technical research firm. That's consistent with an environment of higher volatility in which correlations across risk assets tend to increase. However, the correlation remains below its early-2026 highs, the data show.

Bitcoin has certain characteristics of digital gold, in that it has a limited supply, no single owner and no CEO or board that can decide to issue more, said Anthony Georgiades, founder and general partner at Innovating Capital in New York, which invests in early-stage crypto, blockchain, digital infrastructure and deep tech companies.

But it's also been caught up in monetary policy since its inception. The last decade or so of monetary policy has been extremely rewarding to speculative risk-on assets, Georgiades said. However, in periods of volatility, those types of assets have experienced the most significant drawdowns and have been the most heavily punished, Georgiades said. That means that a shock on one side of the board that might be completely independent of bitcoin could trigger a large volatility event "given how intertwined digital assets have become in the overall financial system," he said.

Notably, bitcoin hasn't acted much like digital gold in more recent years, said Abe Sheikh, chief investment officer of Cordoba Advisory Partners in New York. In fact, it has fallen substantially during periods when the stock market has been falling. For example, from October 6 through November 18, the S&P 500 fell about 6.3%, and bitcoin fell around 27%. From January 21, 2025 through April 8, 2025, the S&P dropped around 17.4%, while bitcoin slid 27.8%, he said.

Democrats gaining control over both houses is viewed as a negative for crypto from a market perspective. The crypto industry spent more than $200 million to support crypto-friendly candidates, but its role had less of an impact on overall results than in the last election cycle. However, bitcoin's downward trend was already in the works, said Cyrus Amini, chief investment officer at Hyphen Wealth Management in Lafayette, California.

In September, the Clarity Act failed in the U.S. Senate, and while its outlook is now dim, according to many political and market experts, some form of crypto legislation is still considered a priority for many Democrats and Republicans, while the Trump-appointed heads of the Commodity Future Trading Commission and Securities and Exchange Commission continue to favor crypto in rulemaking.

This fall, investors also started to question the security of encryption technology, leading to more concerns over the security of their bitcoin holdings, said Kirkley of Global Settlement Network. The Binance hack in October muddied the waters further. And the recent stock market jitters and economic concerns piled on doubts, Kirkley said. The last few weeks have been especially rough with data security concerns from multiple fronts, he added.

Wall Street's embrace of bitcoin has been a big positive for the cryptocurrency. Kirkley noted that 20% of bitcoin's market price is coming from institutional investors, and that's a conservative estimate.

In addition, new research from digital assets company Galaxy shows that a record 40.5% of all 13F-reported spot bitcoin exchange-traded product holdings, and 11.2% of all the bitcoin in the funds, came from advisory and wealth planning firms, such as RIAs and brokerage platforms, as of the third quarter 13F filings with the SEC, which are required of firms with over $100 million in assets to report their holdings.

Much of this investment is through bitcoin spot ETFs like the iShares Bitcoin Trust ETF (IBIT) that track its price. These ETFs, first approved by the SEC in January 2024, were supposed to provide a reliable new source of institutional demand. But ETF ownership also makes bitcoin especially easy to sell.

Outflows from several of these funds have been substantial in recent months. IBIT, the largest of its peers, had six-month net outflows of $1.65 billion through Nov. 18 and one-month outflows of $1.37 billion, according to ETF Database. The second biggest, Fidelity Wise Origin Bitcoin Fund (FBTC) had one-month net outflows of $322.65 million and six-month net outflows of $771.86 million, according to ETF Database. Grayscale Bitcoin Mini Trust ETF (BTC) had one-month net outflows of $66.35 million, though it had inflows of $590.04 million over six months.

As of September 2026, public companies collectively hold close to 1.29 million bitcoins, which is more than 6% of the 21 million coins in existence, according to bitcoin.com.

Most public companies invest their corporate treasury portfolios in low-risk, highly liquid instruments such as money market funds, Treasury bonds and Treasury bills. That said, there are some well-known companies that have invested in bitcoin. SpaceX, for example, purchased 18,712 bitcoins for a total cost of $661 million, at an average price of $35,324 per coin. The company began accumulating bitcoin in early 2021 and held its position unchanged from mid-2024 until its IPO filing.

Digital Asset Treasury companies, known as DATs or DATCOs, have also played a role in bitcoin's volatility. DATs are publicly listed firms whose primary business strategy involves holding significant quantities of digital assets, such as bitcoin or ethereum, on their corporate balance sheets to drive shareholder value. Companies in this space include Strategy, Strive, and Twenty One Capital.

"DATs have exacerbated the problem," said Sheikh of Cordoba Advisory Partners. When bitcoin is rising, these companies borrow money in the debt market or raise capital to buy more bitcoin, which magnifies the upswings. They have also sold bitcoin to make their bond coupon payments. "It leads to enormous volatility on the upside and downside," Sheikh said.

As of mid-November, Strategy, the largest corporate holder of bitcoin, holds 850,849 BTC, according to a regulatory filing. That represents roughly 4% of bitcoin's total supply. The company acquired these holdings at a total cost of approximately $64.2 billion, with an average purchase price of roughly $75,452 per coin, according to the filing.

Bitcoin has been trending lower since the peak in October 2025. A trading range has taken hold and the bottom of the range is just below 60,000, said Katie Stockton, founder and managing partner of Fairlead Strategies. "It appears to be trying to find a footing," she said.

Oversold conditions on the heels of recent lows in June and July, in addition to long-term momentum, suggest bitcoin can support an upturn, she said. But that depends on whether the crypto can hold at a current range around $60,000, she said. "At the end of the day, bitcoin is exceptionally volatile. It's still an area that people might go to sell when they think that risk is higher in other areas."

By mid-morning Friday, the price of bitcoin had slipped below $60,000, its lowest mark since July. It was on pace for its seventh-straight weekly loss for the first time since May 2022, according to CNBC data.

#2925. Neemias Queta is flourishing

"I feel like his growth and his role has gone kind of under the radar," Joe Mazzulla said of Neemias Queta after the Celtics' 131-94 win over the Mavericks on Sunday night.

Coming into last season, we did not know what to expect from Neemias Queta as he entered his first season as the Celtics starting center. He had some good moments in his first two years as a Celtic, but nothing to show he was going to be the long-term center.

There were growing pains at the start. The Celtics lost games early on due to their lack off rebounding but since that point, Queta has surpassed even his biggest fans' expectations.

With Queta as the closest defender this year, teams are shooing 41.3% on shots less than 6 feet away from the rim. That is the best mark in the NBA among players who have faced 4 or more shots around the rim per game. The average mark is 63%.

According to databallr, teams shoot 20% worse in general when Queta is on the court.

Databallr has a stat called DDPM which "estimates how much a player improves expected team defense per 100 possessions. It rolls together rim deterrence, contests, rebounding, disruption, foul discipline, and context-adjusted defensive information." Neemias Queta's DDPM is +2.3, which ranks in the 98th percentile of the NBA.

That shows in Queta's on/off numbers as well. With Queta on the floor this year, the Celtics are giving up 102.5 points per 100 possessions (compared to 112.1 with him on the bench) and have a net rating of +23.8 when he is on the floor (compared to +0.2 with him off of it).

Perhaps there is no better example of Neemy's impact than at the end of the Raptors game when he blocked 2 shots at the rim late in the game to seal a Celtics win. One of them came up one with 17 seconds left and the other with the Celtics up 5 with 4 seconds left.

One more block for Neemias Queta to end the game. Celtics pull out the win on the 2nd night of a back to back after being down as many as 22 points! https://t.co/YS3DB6PeDQ

— Celtics Clips (@celticsclipss) November 12, 2026

In what was a five-point win for the Celtics — Queta was a +27. His impact on both ends of the court cannot be overstated.

Queta is not a good defensive center; he is one of the best defenders in the NBA. The way he impacts the rim allows for the Celtics to play more aggressive on defense, which is something they wanted to do this season.

He is anchoring the 2nd best defense in the NBA right now. The Celtics defensive rating is 106.0, just 2nd in the NBA behind the Minnesota Timberwolves, at 104.0.

He has started to take and make corner threes this year. He doesn't shoot them a lot; he has only shot 6 to start the season but he has made 4 of them. He isn't taking contested threes or any threes above the break so we can't call him a three-point threat or a stretch big, but he has made the ones he takes so I can't complain about them.

The advanced numbers say his offense has been average. Databallr's ODPM has Queta at 0.0, in the 67th percentile and the Celtics only score 1.00 points per possession with Queta as the roll man in the pick and roll.

However, the on/off numbers say Boston is significantly better on offense with Queta on the court. The Celtics score 126.4 points with Queta on the floor and 112.1 with Queta off the floor. If we take Jayson Tatum off the court, the Celtics are scoring 131.4 points per 100 possessions with Queta on the court. Granted that is just 74 minutes, but it shows that these numbers are not skewed thanks to most of Queta's minutes happening with Tatum on the floor.

One reason for this is that he has the most screen assist points in the NBA per 36 minutes, with 15 points per 36 minutes. He has also been setting a ton of flare screens, which are off ball screens set and a player moves away from the ball. His screening creates a ton of opportunities for Boston in the half court.

The Celtics have gotten a lot of praise for their player development. They might not have a bigger win than Queta. Turning him into one of the best defensive centers in the NBA is not something many (including the Sacramento Kings who waived him) saw coming. Having Queta as a security blanket at the rim has been a big deal for the Celtics this year as their defense has become one of the best units in the NBA.

#2924. Bitcoin's regulation bull case is played out. What could drive crypto gains moving forward

Crypto investors are hunting for the next positive catalyst after U.S. crypto regulation — a longtime market driver — fizzled in 2026.

Bitcoin traded around $61,500 on Friday, a more than 50% decline from its all-time high above $126,000 reached in October 2025. The broader digital asset market has struggled after the Clarity Act, a crypto market structure bill, failed to advance in the Senate in September.

Investors now see Wall Street's embrace of blockchain technology — particularly tokenization — as the next trend to give bitcoin a boost.

"Every single traditional firm is now looking at digital assets," BitGo CEO Mike Belshe told CNBC last week in an exclusive interview from the fintech-focused Cantor Convergence conference in Miami Beach. That includes BlackRock, the world's largest asset manager.

He pointed to statements from the asset management giant's CEO, Larry Fink, who said the next major evolution in global finance is the creation of digital representations of every stock, bond, fund and real-world asset onto a shared blockchain ledger.

"I think the biggest catalyst we're seeing right now is actually very much in motion and it's happening, which is capital markets are moving onchain," eToro CEO Yoni Assia told CNBC in an exclusive interview from the conference. He pointed to the SpaceX IPO as an example, which had derivatives trading on eToro before the IPO.

"First day of trading, we distributed it to our retail clients, and it started trading 24/7, and that's all because capital markets are now copying crypto markets and are sitting on the same rails," Assia said. "Crypto markets have always been 24/7, they never sleep, and now what we're seeing is that capital markets are copying crypto markets using the same technology so stocks are moving onchain, they're being tokenized."

Assia said that supportive regulation in the U.S. from agencies, including the Securities and Exchange Commission and the Commodity Futures Trading Commission, is also helping adoption of crypto.

He pointed to the CFTC earlier this month clearing the way for crypto-style perpetual futures tied to individual stocks to trade on regulated U.S. exchanges for the first time. Assia also highlighted the SEC's move to issue an order in September, creating a regulatory pathway for certain trading venues to trade tokenized representations of publicly traded U.S. stocks.

"Blockchain technology is now starting to power what's happening in capital markets," Assia told CNBC. "That is the one of the biggest changes we're going to see in capital markets for many, many years."

"We're talking about hundreds of trillions of dollars that are going to be tokenized and move onchain and at the same time, I do believe that will support prices of bitcoin and other blockchains as well," he continued.

The industry is also watching whether inflation will ease and geopolitical tensions will subside in the new year, which could lower broader macro risk currently hampering the industry.

Last month, Strategy's Michael Saylor said he thinks bitcoin's next bull market is already underway.

ARK Invest Chief Investment Officer Cathie Wood told CNBC the investment management firm's $1.5 million bull case price target for bitcoin by 2030 still stands. She also said that it wouldn't surprise her if bitcoin reached its all-time high again before the end of 2027.

"Our macro forecast has inflation breaking down considerably," Wood told CNBC in an exclusive interview at Cantor Convergence. "I think the elections have increased our confidence there because the war [in Iran], one way or the other, is going to end."

She added that "bitcoin has weathered so much bad news," including the $6.3 billion Binance hack and the "distraction" of AI which, "many people would say is sucking the oxygen away from bitcoin."

The maturation of the artificial intelligence trade could provide an opportunity for bitcoin to find new inflows.

Wood said that ARK Invest believes bitcoin has found its low and stressed that cybersecurity is "top of mind right now," which could provide yet another opportunity for crypto markets to rebound.

"I think if the Bitcoin and crypto world coalesces and makes investors feel that they've got their arms around security and actually understand that there might be more security issues in the traditional financial system, I think we're going to see a wave of support," Wood said.
