#2922. SpaceX’s subsidiary Cursor tells lawmakers it's considering phasing out Chinese AI models

SpaceX's artificial-intelligence coding platform, Cursor, is considering moving away from using of Chinese AI models due to "geopolitical risk," CNBC has learned.

Cursor, which SpaceX acquired at an implied $60 billion equity value in August, had built its coding model Composer 2 on top of Chinese lab Moonshot AI's Kimi K2.5.

It's among the U.S. firms that have adopted Chinese models, whose usage has spiked across the globe this year, worrying lawmakers in Washington. The battle for AI supremacy between the U.S. and China has reached new heights in 2026 as Chinese models have improved.

But now Cursor plans to "explore phasing out PRC [People's Republic of China]-developed open-weight models because of geopolitical risk," according to a report produced by the House Committee on Homeland Security and the House Select Committee on China, shared exclusively with CNBC ahead of its publication on Friday.

The report, which follows a months-long investigation into the adoption of Chinese AI models into the American tech stack, said Cursor "informed the Committees" of those plans. Airbnb and DoorDash also engaged with the investigation about their use of Chinese AI models.

SpaceX announced it entered into a formal agreement to buy Cursor in June; the deal was completed in August. SpaceX is developing the Grok family of models, which Cursor has been involved in training.

Cursor, SpaceX and DoorDash did not respond to a CNBC request for comment. Airbnb declined to comment. The U.S. Commerce Department's Bureau of Industry and Security did not immediately respond to a request for comment.

The report found that cost, customization and near-frontier performance of Chinese AI models were driving adoption, with the U.S. lacking a competitive open-weight alternative at "price and performance the market demands."

"Beijing is now seeking to turn those technological gains into global market share and long-term dependence," the report says. China's embassy in the U.K. did not respond to a request for comment. China's Foreign Ministry did not immediately respond.

It also sets out recommendations to address the issues it has identified.

Those include launching a "national American open-weight initiative" that combines public computing power, shared evaluation infrastructure and "lawful access" to American frontier models for training.

The report also says an order, made public in October, that banned DeepSeek from federal civilian agency systems, should be built on, by prohibiting the use of "PRC-developed AI models" in federal information systems and in performance of federal contracts.

Separately, Washington is also concerned about Chinese companies accessing Nvidia chips remotely, via overseas data centers, and gaining ground by using "distillation," where new models use older, more established ones to help train them.

The U.S. Commerce Department's Bureau of Industry and Security, or BIS, announced on Monday an interim final rule designed to prevent Chinese companies' remote access to AI chips.

#2921. Open Thread: How the 24-second clock became the standard for the NBA.

As I've stated on many an occasion, I am a math geek. So you can imagine my pleasure in fiding this post:

The 24-second clock that is used in the NBA came about through a mathematical means.

Apparently before the adoption of the 24-second clock, teams simply dribbled out games for much longer than what qualifies as garbage time these days. One of the team owners came up with the equations that the game, which was forty-eight minutes, or 2280 seconds, divided by the average number of shots teams took in a game, or 120 shots, was equal to 24 seconds per shot. Thus eliciting a more timely and interesting game.

I have not personally verified the validity, but now I have plans during the holiday break.

#2920. U.S.-China trade, Taiwan and tech tensions remain unresolved as Trump returns from Asia

SHENZHEN, China — Even as Asia-Pacific Economic Cooperation leaders gathered for high-profile photos at the summit's close, key issues from Nvidia to Taiwan remained unaddressed.

"The intrigue is around not what was said, but what remains unresolved," said Gary Dvorchak, managing director at Blueshirt.

"Taiwan remains a major source of strategic uncertainty. North Korea was notably absent from the trilateral nuclear declaration," he said. "Southeast Asia, which is increasingly central to global manufacturing and investment, was overshadowed by major-power diplomacy."

President Donald Trump did not meet with North Korea's leader Kim Jong Un, despite speculation.

Taiwan, one of the 21 member economies of APEC, sent Acer Group founder and honorary chairman Stan Shih to Shenzhen. But the self-governed island, which Beijing claims is part of its territory, did not feature in any significant way during the event. Taiwan is set to hold key elections for Taipei mayor and other posts on Nov. 28.

Instead, the highlight of this week's APEC summit in Shenzhen was a trilateral meeting Thursday between Trump, Chinese President Xi Jinping and Russian President Vladimir Putin. The three leaders, meeting together for the first time, reaffirmed interests in reducing nuclear risks and plans to increase oil production.

"The optics are impressive, but there's not much happening," Ian Bremmer, president and founder of Eurasia Group, said Friday of the trilateral on CNBC's "Squawk Box Asia."

Resolving the years-long Ukraine war would require involvement from President Volodymyr Zelenskyy and European allies, Bremmer said.

Ahead of the trilateral, Trump and Xi also held their third bilateral summit of the year. Xi greeted Trump at the airport late Wednesday, reciprocating the hospitality shown in September when the Chinese leader visited Washington.

But little was shared publicly on whether the two countries agreed to reduce tariffs further, allow rare earths to keep flowing beyond a January deadline, or address tech competition.

Recent reports tying Jensen Huang to an alleged Chinese spy led to the executive's resignation as Nvidia CEO. It also raised questions in Congress about the effectiveness of U.S. efforts to restrict China's access to advanced chips used to train artificial intelligence.

Trump indicated to Fox News that Nvidia came up briefly in his talks with Xi. U.S. officials last week said Trump did not plan to raise the matter, while noting Tesla and SpaceX CEO Elon Musk would accompany the U.S. president.

No other executive was named. Other American business leaders, including Visa CEO Ryan McInerney, who also chairs the U.S.-China Business Council, joined the APEC CEO Summit and was seen at a welcome banquet Wednesday evening.

However, Musk did not appear with Trump at major APEC-related events. CNBC was present for the opening portions of a banquet Wednesday and the Economic Leaders' Meeting Thursday and did not see Musk.

In fact, Musk flew to Shanghai Thursday morning to visit Tesla's factory there, a company executive said on Chinese social media. Musk also showed up in a social media video with Ren Hongbin, chairman of the China Council for the Promotion of International Trade.

Musk addressed Wednesday's DealBook Summit in New York via video conference, having previously said on X that he would be joining from China.

Tesla and the White House did not immediately respond to CNBC's requests for comment.

"We still don't have a durable resolution of the U.S.-China trade dispute," Dvorchak said, adding: "Business needs certainty, not just ceremony."

Trump visited Shenzhen after briefly traveling to the Philippines on Tuesday for a meeting with the leaders of Australia, India and Japan as part of the Quad grouping happening on the sidelines of the ASEAN Summit. Trump also met the prime minister of Thailand, Anutin Charnvirakul.

Topics of discussion included U.S. cooperation with the region, trade and critical minerals, U.S. Ambassador to ASEAN Kevin Kim told reporters Wednesday. He declined to comment on whether China's influence in the region was addressed.

Kim emphasized it was the second year in a row that Trump visited Southeast Asia, and noted the region "will be at the heart of SI [artificial intelligence] going forward," making it important that American technology is "getting into the right hands" through a regulatory framework the president is building.

Speaking to reporters a day later in Shenzhen, Japanese Prime Minister Sanae Takaichi also highlighted the ASEAN and Quad events over APEC. "The major outcome of this diplomatic week was the Quad Summit," she said, noting via official English translation how the four leaders expressed "strong opposition to the unilateral approach to change the status quo by force or coercion ... [as well as] arbitrary export control."

Takaichi, the first Japanese leader in seven years to visit China, received a cool reception upon her arrival in Shenzhen for the APEC meeting.

Tensions between Japan and China have escalated since Takaichi drew Beijing's ire in Nov. 2025 when she said that a Chinese attack on Taiwan could draw a Japanese military response.

Takaichi said Thursday that Japan's position on Taiwan remained unchanged as stated in the 1972 Japan-China Communiqué.

The communique said Japan "understands and respects" the People's Republic of China's position on Taiwan, but does not explicitly acknowledge Taiwan as part of the PRC.

— CNBC's Anniek Bao and Lim Hui Jie contributed to this report.

#2918. Tokenization platform Securitize gains 10% postmarket on Q3 results, move into asset-backed finance

Tokenization platform Securitize climbed about 10% postmarket after reporting third quarter growth alongside increased operating expenses, and announcing an expansion into asset-backed finance through a strategic acquisition.

Miami-based Securitize, which made its public market debut in July, operates in the relatively new corner of the crypto equity universe involved in institutional tokenization infrastructure. Tokenized assets on the platform grew 9% in the third quarter, to $4.8 billion, from the same period a year ago. Aggregate transaction volume rose 20% from a year ago, to $4.3 billion. Revenue of $11.5 million was up 28% from last year.

The growth came as bullish sentiment around tokenization gained traction throughout the third quarter, ahead of the Security and Exchange Commission formalizing the long awaited Innovation Exemption on Sept. 17, which created a temporary pathway for trading of certain tokenized stocks and strengthened the regulatory outlook.

Profitability is still meaningfully below year-ago levels, due in part to costs associated with going public over the summer, as well as the scaling up of the tokenization business at an active time for the entire sector, Securitize CEO Carlos Domingo told CNBC in an exclusive interview.

Securitize reinforced "certain areas of the company like finance, compliance, legal [and] cybersecurity," which increased corporate costs "compared to a year ago when we were privately held," Domingo said. "We're in in a very good position. The adjusted EBITDA … is improving significantly from previous quarters."

Securitize reported an adjusted EBITDA loss of $2 million, compared with positive adjusted EBITDA of $3.6 million a year ago, better than a $5.5 million loss in the second quarter. The company posted a 183% increase in operating costs and expenses.

Also on Monday, the Securitize board authorized a $50 million share buyback and announced the acquisition of River One, an institutional asset-backed financing platform developing technology to connect stablecoin capital with traditional lending markets. Terms were not disclosed.

Securitize sees an opportunity to modernize the $9 trillion market for asset backed finance by using stablecoins to finance real world assets, generate yield and tokenize investment positions and credit products, Domingo said.The acquisition also marks an expansion beyond Securitize's core niche markets at a time when tokenization's competitive landscape is growing, with traditional asset managers like BlackRock and Franklin Templeton, and established financial market infrastructure providers like DTCC and Broadridge, entering the industry.

"We have been very active in tokenized funds, treasuries [and] yield … And we've been obviously recently very active in tokenized stocks, so this adds another piece of the puzzle in the emerging tokenization space for us to capture revenue growth."

#2916. The Phoenix Suns’ core four are getting better, with more runway to grow

The Phoenix Suns are 7-7 overall after a 1-5 start to the season. We were hoping for a much quicker start to the season than last year, but the difficulty of opponents, combined with the addition of Miles Bridges and Oso Ighodaro's injury, meant the Suns were not at their best to start the season.

The Suns are now 6-2 since Ighodaro was inserted into the starting lineup. Devin Booker is back to playing at a near All-NBA level this season. Jordan Ott is learning the right rotations. Dillon Brooks looks like he is in full villain mode on both ends of the floor after an up-and-down week in November that culminated in a 1-point performance in a loss to the Lakers. His move in the rotation to be the go-to scorer with the second unit to begin the second and fourth quarters appears to have unlocked him.

Miles Bridges is starting to find his lanes and facilitate an offense that needs to keep moving. His defense and rebounding are getting better as well.

Even Jalen Green, who finds himself in trade chatter, in the last two games, has had inspiring second halves to show what Brian Gregory's vision of this team is supposed to look like. Green's 18-point, seven-assist and seven-rebound night was a glimpse into who the Suns need him to be more consistently as one of the best athletes and downhill threats on this team. Combine that with the energy he played with against the Nuggets in that second half, where he had multiple steals, and maybe, just maybe, this backcourt pairing might work between him and Booker. The fit will never be perfect, but if Green can play this way more consistently, it will quiet a lot of the noise around him for the time being.

Regardless of the level of opponent, the Suns offense was crisp and flowing all night long against the Kings. The ball movement, spacing, and unselfishness jumped off the television screen. All four of the Suns starters had over five assists against the Kings; Ighodaro only had two. But that level of effort and commitment to making each other better on both ends of the floor started against the Nuggets in a win Monday night.

In back-to-back games, the core four have all scored in double figures, and the only occurrence of someone scoring below 15 points was Bridges against the Kings; he scored 13 points. Against the Nuggets, the core four combined for 67 points, and against the Kings they combined for 80 points.

The numbers also back it up as well. Before the Suns played the Magic on November 5, the core four had a -59 over the first seven games of the season. Since then, it is only a -2 in eight games, and the group continues to play better. The eye test also shows that they are playing better as well.

This group still has a long way to go, and it still has yet to put it together against a high-quality opponent like the Thunder or Spurs, but the good news is the next seven games on the schedule are: at Utah Jazz, at New Orleans Pelicans, at Houston Rockets, home vs Dallas Mavericks, at Utah Jazz, home vs Los Angeles Clippers, and home vs Sacramento. The Suns should realistically finish this stretch 5-2 or better.

However, it is even deeper than the record over the next seven games. The Suns need to continue to prove that how they have played the last two games is who they are. They are still building winning habits and are still working through defensive and rebounding lapses that have plagued them all season.

Does the 3-point shooting efficiency continue for Booker? Can Green play with the defensive intensity and playmaking? Can Brooks sustain the energy to be the emotional leader night in and night out? Does Miles Bridges continue to do the little things like rebounding and defending regardless of his shot attempts? And as a group, can this core four show up and put teams away without sleepwalking through quarters that give the lesser teams life?

This team still has flaws that might be too difficult to overcome, but if the Suns answer yes to most of these questions over the next two weeks, then they will be right back up into the top eight in the Western Conference when the calendar turns to December.

#2914. Fundora vs. Zapotoczna and Jones vs. Pettus live results, round-by-round updates, ring walks

Uncrowned has Gabriela Fundora vs. Jasmina Zapotoczna and Amari Jones vs. DeAundre Pettus live results, round-by-round updates, highlights, ring walks and start time for the Fundora vs. Zapotoczna and Jones vs. Pettus fight card on Friday night at Fantasy Springs Casino in Indio, California. Gabriela Fundora defends her undisputed flyweight title against Jasmina Zapotoczna in the night's co-feature, while Amari Jones faces DeAundre Pettus in a middleweight contest in the main event.

Fundora (18-0, 10 KOs), Uncrowned's No. 2 pound-for-pound boxer, defeated Gabriela Celeste Alaniz in November 2024 to unify the flyweight belts. She has since made three successful defenses of her undisputed championship, all by stoppage, including a sixth-round knockout of Viviana Ruiz Corredor in March.

Zapotoczna (10-1) has one career defeat, a unanimous decision loss to Shannon Ryan in February 2024. The Polishwoman, now based in the UK, won a split decision over Chloe Watson to capture the European title in March 2025 and then retained it against Justine Lallemand in November 2025.

Jones (17-0, 15 KOs) is the mandatory challenger for the IBF middleweight title. He captured the position by knocking out former champion Vincenzo Gualtieri this past May. Jones now returns in a stay-busy fight before his shot at Aaron McKenna materializes in 2027.

Pettus (14-4, 7 KOs) has never been stopped in his four defeats. He has won two fights since a 2025 loss to Jordan Panthen, including a decision win over Scott Sigmon this past December.

The Jones vs. Pettus prelims begin at 5 p.m. ET and can be watched here on Uncrowned.

The main card starts at 8 p.m. ET on DAZN, with main event ring walks expected around 11 p.m. ET.

#2913. Bitcoin suffered identical sell-off pattern ahead of Anthropic, SpaceX IPOs — what happens next?

Bitcoin has suffered nearly identical sell-offs ahead of two of the biggest public offerings this year, leaving investors wondering if competing demand for investor capital could be weighing on the cryptocurrency and perhaps delaying its recovery.

The price of the bitcoin dropped 22.7% in the 30 days before Anthropic's IPO and fell 22.3% in the month leading up to SpaceX's June 12 listing. Ether declined 25.3% in the 30 days preceding Anthropic's debut, and 28% in the same window ahead of SpaceX.

"Liquidity events can elevate correlations across asset classes quickly," Matthew Sheffield, chief investment officer at the ether treasury firm Sharplink, told CNBC. "The first 15 min of [Thursday's] Anthropic IPO put that on full display. Tech stocks, value stocks, oil, gold, crypto all sold off as traders sold assets to buy shares in the secondary market."

"Stores of value function as such over long horizons, but in the short term are still liquidity driven," Sheffield said. "We saw this in early Covid when the market fled to cash. Stock, bonds, commodities ... it didn't matter, you were being sold for cash."

While investors appear to be rotating capital out of crypto to make room for history-making IPO investments, that can't be said with precise certainty. Crypto has also been under pressure from ETF outflows, leverage, macro uncertainty and broader risk-off sentiment.

Still, the post-IPO price action may offer a data point in understanding where bitcoin's price goes next. With Anthropic now public, investors are watching to see whether bitcoin and ether find a floor, and if institutional demand returns.

"[After] the SpaceX IPO, bitcoin didn't truly find its final bottom until after the IPO was completed, and it didn't actually reverse the downtrend until 70 days later," said Matt Cole, CEO and chairman of Strive Asset Management, a bitcoin treasury firm. "My view is that [for] bitcoin, the most likely scenario is that it chops around here."

The expiration of IPO allocation restrictions can influence the timing of capital flows into digital assets, Sheffield said.

"Post SpaceX we saw the wealth effect in full force," Sheffield said. "Many brokers make traders wait 15-30 days before they are allowed to flip shares they received as allocations. … If you went long ETH versus SpaceX on the day of the IPO, on day 31 was when you would have become profitable."

Bitcoin's next critical test is between $60,000 and $62,000, according to Nicolai Sondergaard, a research analyst at Nansen. On Friday, it was sitting at about $62,000.

As of Friday, bitcoin was hovering at the upper end of a critical $60,000 to $62,000 support range. Its next move could determine whether it can stabilize or if it will face another leg lower and revisit its July low of $57,737.35.

Throughout November, bitcoin has been struggling from a combination of dollar strength, institutional selling, and ongoing leveraged bitcoin liquidations. The declines began in late October, as concerns over artificial intelligence valuations and spending fueled a broad tech sell-off. They were compounded by the $6.3 billion Binance hack and weakening institutional ETF demand.

Bitcoin is in the early stages of forming a bottom, according to Cole. Retail fear is elevated amid recent outages, quantum concerns and other short-term uncertainties, but bitcoin should continue to benefit from macro forces like rising debt and weakening fiat currencies, which should drive price higher, he said.

"Capital markets are open, … retail fears are high, and when you combine those things it's much more likely to be a dip-buying opportunity across the board, including bitcoin, than the start of a great financial crisis," said Cole. "It'll likely age as a volatile time to consider allocating to risk, not the opposite."

"The winter for bitcoin is basically at the end, but after winter does not come summer, it comes early spring," he added. "Anytime anyone's been around early spring, what you notice is it gets really cold still, it still rains – it's not necessarily great weather, and I think that's the environment we're in for bitcoin."

#2912. Knicks 102, Timberwolves 99: “3 straight on the road, what’s up…”

LaMelo Ball hit one of his patented three-pointers from the left elbow early in the third quarter against the New York Knicks last night and as the crowd cheered, he proceeded to jog back on defense. Of course, the only not totally broken Ball brother couldn't let the moment pass without completely indulging himself into a thoroughly scripted "ice in the veins"-gesture that feels as original these days as a two-handed set shot. Just a moment later Josh Hart rained on the parade, running directly by Vanilla Ball's D and finishing an easy backdoor cut with a simple layup. And just like that the Knicks were up 14 again, ignoring the protocol of how cutting a deficit to 12 in a game where your team got handsomely dominated up to that point needs to be celebrated.

It feels good to root for a professional team, doesn't it? It will allow you to say things like Real Clydes Wear Plaid shared in the Postgame Thread and which I unapologetically stole for the headline of this recap. The Knicks won their third game in a row at Minnesota last night and they probably made it a lot harder than it needed to be. Their opponents certainly had something to do with it, no matter how ill-advised some individual emotions might have been. The Timberwolves can play tremendous defense, are as long as those trees in their logo and have an aggression you could certainly link to that name-giving animal of them. They got a good albeit cranky coach, a proven system, years of winning experience. Yet in the end they also got LaMelo Ball now.

We get that he is a talented, at times iconic passer; we also get that he has found his footing fairly well on the frozen tundra near the Great Lakes so far. But we really can't get convinced that you can ever really win big with a "talent" like that. His play can remind one of the game young kids play before they really get a hang of Dr. Naismith's rules. They also take two steps before every dribble, then four after they pick the ball up. They also couldn't care less about a repetitive shooting motion or dribbling the ball without palming it every time. With the kids it's okay and fun, we let them discover themselves as players and excuse it. But Lamborghini LaMelo should be held to a different standard, he is supposed to be a professional NBA player after all.

Luckily for the Knicks, they got plenty of real pros on their roster, guys who truly care about winning and treat their profession like it means something – not only to them but to others. It certainly seemed that way when they played most of the game last night with a level of calmness and concentration that has been a hallmark of theirs ever since they went on that beautiful championship run last year. But despite all their dominance over that two-month stretch in the playoffs, you can't ignore that they are a team with plenty of cracks in their armor, many of which showed themselves last night as well. It's the kind of weakness that are often the reason for the Bockers falling behind big only to have the opportunity to stage their beloved monumental comebacks later on.

Yesterday the Knicks offense completely bogged down late when Minnesota went to more switching on D (raise your hand if you've seen that before), neutralizing all the beautiful team basketball that helped the Gotham Gang run up an almost 20-point lead earlier. Not even Karl-Anthony Towns, once again mesmerizing for most of the night against a traditional five at his old stomping grounds, couldn't regain his first-half form. Maybe he rode the pine a little long, Mr. Brown? Well, you can't play him all 48, we get it. A lineup that included Jose Alvarado, Andre Drummond and Josh Hart was still an interesting choice, to say the least. For all the individual chops it's quite a load for Jalen Brunson to carry that group offensively, it almost feels like him heaving big AD up K2 or some other majestic mountain. It might get you to the summit against certain defenses but considering some defenses are as long and physical as the Wolf Pack, it can also get cold rather quickly. Especially when Brunson still hasn't found his touch inside the paint yet.

That New York could still pull it out in the end is a testament to timely plays and a gritty, opportunistic defense that at no point in the game relented to the physically imposing Wolves coming at them. When LaMelo Ball couldn't find someone open downtown, the canines' only plan was to ram their oversized combo forwards ahead. It worked here or there but never consistently. Watch out, there's an extended arm coming at you – boom, another offensive foul! It's an interesting yet understandable choice by the league to make the off arm a point of emphasis this year (sorry, SGA). Why didn't they do the same for palming the basketball or taking more steps than a dressage horse? Your guess is as good as mine.

For the moment, though, let us not worry about it – there is a win to be celebrated. For all we know though, our guys are probably watching some film right now and preparing for their next opponent (Portland on Saturday). Maybe they are also speeding race cars through some busy pedestrian zone or practice their one-legged step-back fadeaway jumper that has netted them less than 42 percent shooting for their career?

Nah, we leave that to the guy with ice in his veins.

#2911. Data center construction costs soar 13% in 2026

A version of this article first appeared in the CNBC Property Play newsletter with Diana Olick. Property Play covers new and evolving opportunities for the real estate investor, from individuals to venture capitalists, private equity funds, family offices, institutional investors and large public companies. Sign up to receive future editions, straight to your inbox.

The demand for large-scale data centers in the U.S. this year, driven by the need for computing power for artificial intelligence, grew well beyond the supply of powered land and the labor to build these structures. It also pushed timelines and risk for contractors.

All that resulted in costs rising 13% year-over-year to more than twice the global rate, according to a new report from Turner & Townsend, a global project management company. The gain this year was about three times the last ten-year average.

The report looked at over 500 live or recently completed projects in 62 global markets. It also included a survey of 400 experts in the global data center industry.

While there was some stabilization in cost inflation this year, it was not enough to offset the huge growth in not just demand but speed of construction and delivery.

"More aggressive schedules tie to contractors in particular and subcontractors baking in more risk allowances in their pricing," said Paul Barry, North America data center lead for Turner & Townsend. "So whether that's acceleration allowances, whether that's more overtime and labor attraction allowances, even just higher fees for taking on the risk of delivering at a faster pace than what they would traditionally take on."

All of those things, combined with the spike in demand and the strain on the supply chain, translate into higher costs, he added.

Capital expenditure on global data centers is projected to rise from about $800 billion this year to $1.8 trillion per year in 2050, according to a recent report from PwC. The U.S. is expected to capture almost half of all global investment in AI infrastructure, which includes both data centers and chips.

Power is, of course, key to all data centers, and 56% of those surveyed by Turner & Townsend globally said it was a major obstacle to delivery. Just over half said utility connections typically take between one and two years, and almost a third reported timeframes of over two years. Costs for power vary regionally — from $15.01 per watt in Silicon Valley to $10.51 per watt in Dallas, based on a typical 60-megawatt design.As with every type of construction in the U.S., labor continues to be a significant headwind. Just over one quarter of respondents said their contractor capacity, that is, the ability to manage construction backlogs, was sufficient.

Going forward, Barry said he expects costs to moderate somewhat, with cost increases in the 6% to 10% range in the next year. A lot of the major projects have already started or are starting now, so cost risk has been baked into those contracts.

"If this demand continues, then there'll be more continued flow of project starts and so on, but I think we've hit a big surge in 2026, especially of new big major projects that have been procured," said Barry.

#2910. A wave of 'pent-up quits' could boost turnover in the job market, expert says: 'The labor market is a bit like a coiled spring'

Frustrated workers could trigger a wave of turnover, creating a shift in the sluggish job market, according to experts at the job boards Indeed and Glassdoor.

That sluggishness, or what labor economists call a low-hire, low-fire economy, means that fewer people are getting hired, and fewer workers are leaving their jobs — even as a growing share of job holders report frustration with their career opportunities. In 2026, 21% of workers report feeling dissatisfied with their career prospects, according to Glassdoor reviewer data. Dissatisfied workers rated their jobs at a one or two out of five stars.

A stagnant job market can discourage even the unhappiest of workers from quitting, but hiring could soon ramp up. Indeed's Job Posting Index, or JPI, which tracks the level of job postings on Indeed relative to a pre-pandemic baseline, saw year-over-year growth for the first time in September 2026 since 2022. The upward trend suggests that the job market is "heating up," Glassdoor chief economist Daniel Zhao tells CNBC Make It.

As more opportunities open up, job holders might regain the confidence to exit, turning into "a wave of quits and turnover," Zhao says. Turnover could also help those who are out of a job secure employment as roles that were once filled become vacant.

"The labor market is a bit like a coiled spring right now," Zhao said at a press event Tuesday in New York City. "There's a lot of pent-up potential energy, and it's unclear exactly which way that that spring is going to release that energy."

In a healthy job market, employee dissatisfaction can forecast turnover rates. When employee dissatisfaction reached 17% in April 2022, per Glassdoor reviews, quit rates also reached their highest levels at 3%, seasonally adjusted, according to data from Bureau of Labor Statistics Job Openings and Labor Turnover Survey data published by the St. Louis Federal Reserve.

However, when workers feel anxious about their future job prospects, turnover can stall. Though workers feel dissatisfied with their jobs at higher rates in September 2026 than they did in April 2022, only 2.1% of workers quit their job in the month, according to the BLS JOLTS.

"There aren't those opportunities to climb the career ranks internally or externally," Zhao says of the job market. "There's no outlet for the frustration or dissatisfaction that [workers] might be feeling."

Employee turnover was one focus of Indeed and Glassdoor's 2027 Hiring and Worklife Trends Report, published Wednesday. Their research, which assumed a consistent relationship between worker dissatisfaction and resignations based on historical data, found that the quit rate is 0.22% lower than what employee sentiment would typically suggest. While the figure seems small, it is equivalent to an additional 349,000 quits per month.

"Workers are very hesitant to quit, especially without something lined up, because hiring is very sluggish, and long-term unemployment is looming over them," Zhao tells CNBC Make It.

While unemployment levels are stable, people are remaining jobless for longer, according to data from the BLS. Those who face long-term unemployment, job seekers who have been out of work for 27 weeks or more, accounted for 27.6% of all unemployed people in October 2026.

Even if more jobs open, turnover across sectors could vary. Industries that are highly exposed to artificial intelligence like software development, engineering and banking previously dragged Indeed's JPI down, meaning employers in these sectors posted fewer jobs. Indeed's data shows that postings in these sectors have since seen growth, and could result in what experts call "pent-up quits," which occur when dissatisfied workers leave their jobs once market conditions improve. Job posts on Indeed for employers in industries with less AI exposure like healthcare have softened, according to experts.

However, business and professional services, which includes banking, shed 27,000 jobs in October 2026, according to data from ADP Research.

"As we look ahead to 2027, that increase in demand might not necessarily translate into more hires, and it also might not be enough hires to give workers the confidence that they can actually quit their jobs and move on to something better," Zhao says.

Even as the job market appears to be trending in a positive direction, further economic pressure could continue to place a strain on employers and their hiring plans.

"Nobody was really expecting 2026 to come with the geopolitical challenges that have happened and the the impact that has had on energy prices and inflation," Zhao says. "And so there's always the outside chance that something happens in 2027 that derails the job market from the path that it's on."

#2908. Bucks vs. Trail Blazers Player Grades: Ament drops career high, Rollins returns with a vengeance

On yet another night when the Milwaukee Bucks matched up against a high-profile former player (or two), they couldn't get it done, losing to the Portland Trail Blazers 124-114. The Blazers gave the Bucks chances early, looking like they were the ones on the second night of a back-to-back, not the Bucks. Unfortunately, the Bucks failed to capitalise on those chances and fell at the mercy of two former star Milwaukee point guards, Damian Lillard and Jrue Holiday, who taught the young Bucks a lesson down the stretch. Read our full summary of the game here and catch a six-minute audio recap on the Bucks+ podcast, Bucks In Six Minutes, below.

31 minutes, 19 points, 4 rebounds, 3 assists, 1 steal, 4 turnovers, 7/13 FG, 4/7 3P, 1/3 FT, -16

Herro's counting stats look fine, but it felt like he played a lot worse. Herro started the game on autopilot, and his turnovers all felt like pick-sixes, especially the one in the fourth quarter where he got ripped trying to bring the ball up against Holiday. I generally don't lean too much into single-game plus/minus, but this one feels dead on. All things being equal—which they may not have been—Herro should not have closed the game.

21 minutes, 15 points, 3 rebounds, 6 assists, 4 steals, 3 turnovers, 5/11 FG, 1/5 3P, 4/6 FT, +5

You can basically take everything I said about Herro and flip it for Rollins. His impact on the game was monumental, and most of it came on defence. Even more impressive, he did this while coming off an illness. Rollins deserved more minutes than he got (assuming he wasn't on a minutes restriction).

22 minutes, 9 points, 1 rebounds, 2 assists, 1 steal, 1 turnover, 3/11 FG, 1/4 3P, -5

Jaquez was bad. He contributed virtually nothing on offence, and with Avdija out, there wasn't really a defensive matchup for him. I especially hate the possessions where Jaquez dribbles aimlessly without gaining any advantage on his defender; it feels like they always end in a turnover.

25 minutes, 11 points, 5 rebounds, 2 steals, 4/8 FG, 2/4 3P, -5

Ware was… decent? Like, the three-ball is coming back, and he continues to show stuff defensively, but you know what I loved? That monster offensive rebound and putback he had in the fourth quarter. That showed a level of emotion and care factor we have rarely seen from Ware. Inject it into my veins!

28 minutes, 14 points, 3 rebounds, 3 assists, 2 steals, 3 turnovers, 5/13 FG, 2/5 3P, -11

A bit of a rookie game for Burries, with a bunch of good stuff and some not-so-good stuff. He's got that floater going left on lock, so that's good. His defence felt really impactful, and the play where he prevented Scott Henderson from even crossing half court was stupendous. I will say that Burries' release is going to have to speed up in the future; guys are getting to his shots starting from far away. All in all, I thought the rook played better than what his stat line would suggest.

22 minutes, 18 points, 3 rebounds, 2 assists, 1 steal, 2 turnovers, 6/11 FG, 3/6 3P, 3/5 FT, -6

A third really solid game in a row from Jakucionis, who should have closed over Herro. At this point, it's basically become a theme that he comes into the game, makes stuff happen, and, most importantly, brings energy. Now, some silly stuff is going to come with that, but you take the good with the bad, because there is undoubtedly more good. Finally, love that he's not even hesitating on his three-ball right now.

35 minutes, 18 points, 9 rebounds, 0 turnovers, 6/11 FG, 2/5 3P, 4/4 FT, -7

On balance, this was Ament's best game of his young NBA career, and it showed in the number of minutes he played. I truly think his shot has a high chance of going in every time he shoots, and that's a great sign for the future. I also love that Ament is attacking rebounds more and more, especially on the offensive glass; his game is becoming more multifaceted with each passing week. Plus, no turnovers! My only negative—and it is a big one—is that his off-ball defence remains a wreck; overhelping was a major issue last night.

27 minutes, 7 points, 3 rebounds, 3 assists, 1 block, 0 turnovers, 3/8 FG, 1/5 3P, +8

I know it's become a bit of a cliché to say that "good things happen when Nance is on the floor," but it's true! His line isn't impressive, but the offence just flows when the two-way big is on the court in a way it doesn't necessarily with other players. Just wait until that three-ball starts dropping. More please!

11 minutes, 3 points, 3 rebounds, 1/2 FG, 0

I considered leaving LeVert off, but I couldn't ignore his impact as a rebounder in the fourth. He obviously had that ridiculous and-one over Clingan, but LeVert grabbed like three O-boards in one possession; unfortunately, the Bucks couldn't pay it off with a bucket.

I've already alluded to Jenkins making what I believed was the wrong call closing with Herro over Jakucionis, but he also brought back Rollins—who was making a wildly positive impact—too late. Then there are the tough decisions that aren't entirely based on the now, like closing with Ament. You had to close with the rookie given the game he had played, but Lillard and Holiday kinda feasted on him late. That's the push-and-pull of coaching a rebuilding side. On the other hand, I loved the move to start Nance.

DNP-CD: AJ Green, Kam Jones, Bogoljub Markovic, Cormac Ryan

Inactive: Myles Turner, Kyle Kuzma, Ousmane Dieng

The veterans are not necessarily needed for this team to compete.

I found it ironic that Dame complained about a foul call where Ament hooked his arm and then drew a shooting foul. That's Dame's signature move! LOL.

Jrue was coaching up Brayden after the game. What a guy.

Speaking of sneaky foul-drawing, Jakucionis has the "drive hard at the hip, throw it up, and pray to the gods they call a foul" move on lock. What worries me is that, like it or not, those calls are generally reserved for guys with stature. Therefore, there will be—and have been—times when KJ does that, it doesn't get called, and he throws up a shot that is basically a turnover.

Gary Trent Jr.'s stint was absolutely diabolical. Exactly why I don't necessarily buy the idea that high steal numbers are a sign of a good defender.

Former Wisconsin Badger John Tonje was in the Blazers rotation.

Nate Ament spoke glowingly of Jrue Holiday in his postgame presser, highlighting his two-way impact.

#2907. What's ahead for AI financing after Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang departs

Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang's departure hasn't produced a broad shock to the credit market, but it is putting the company's role as a financier of artificial intelligence infrastructure under scrutiny.

Huang, who stepped down Thursday amid an espionage and adultery scandal, built Nvidia into an AI powerhouse in the 33 years since he co-founded the company. Chief financial officer Colette Kress has been named interim CEO as the company searches for a permanent chief executive.

Nvidia not only supplies its graphics processing units to hyperscalers and tech giants, it also provides financing to other companies to help the industry expand. That could all change.

"Although we expect the company to fulfill all of its previously announced commitments, we believe that going forward, it may be more difficult for the company to negotiate new multi-party investments among ecosystem partners at the same scale it has done up to this point," Goldman Sachs analyst James Schneider said in a note Thursday.

The news of Huang's resignation comes after investors were already concerned about the massive amount of debt being taken on to fund AI infrastructure.

Tech bond yields have risen and spreads have widened on credit default swaps, most notably in lower-rated names. Credit default swaps are contracts in which investors pay for protection in case the borrower can't repay its debt. Wider spreads indicate investors view the bonds as riskier.

On Friday, the yield on Nvidia's 4.5% bond due in 2031 sat at 5.717%, nearly 5% higher week to date. Bond yields move inversely to prices.

Tech stocks slumped Friday with the Nasdaq 100 closing down 1.2% as markets continued to digest the Nvidia news. Yields on hyperscaler bonds ticked slightly higher on Friday, signaling the broader concern about AI debt lingers.

It's the smaller AI companies that could ultimately see an impact if Nvidia makes any changes to its AI financing. The company had $36 billion in off-balance-sheet AI cloud agreements, or backstops, for neoclouds, according to its last quarterly financial filing in July.

Neoclouds CoreWeave, Nebius and IREN are all in Nvidia's portfolio. Their shares were down sharply Thursday.

CoreWeave, which has become the poster child for AI debt fears, saw its bond yields retreat after its quarterly earnings on Tuesday eased investor concerns. The yield on its 9.75% bond due in 2031 was 14.79% on Friday after climbing to 16.7% last week. The spread on its five-year CDS also dropped more than 17% week to date, although it remained above 1,000 — which is generally considered a sign of distress.

It remains unclear how Nvidia's support for those companies may change, said CreditSights analyst Andy Li.

"We don't know what the ultimate strategy for Nvidia is going to be coming out the other side," he said.

"However, I think directionally, just given the fact that Jensen was so crucial in selling the Nvidia story and being a very aggressive deal maker, it's likely that a lot of this creative deal-making and circular financing and equity and credit support is going to moderate," he added.

CoreWeave CEO Michael Intrator told CNBC Wednesday, before Huang's departure, that Nvidia "is not one person."

"There are thousands of people over there that interface with thousands of people at my company every day. They integrate on the technology. They integrate on the physical infrastructure. They integrate on the networking," he said in an interview with "Squawk on the Street." "They have been a great partner for us for years. They will continue to be a great partner for us."

Bob Lang, founder and chief strategist at Explosive Options, doesn't see any big changes in terms of Nvidia's relevance or its ability to finance for now. As CFO, Kress was involved in the financing deals, and the search for a permanent CEO is going to take some time, he noted.

The real issue is just how much money the company will want to lend for AI financing, rather than, say using its hundreds of billions in free cash flow for other purposes, like share buybacks, capex, or acquisitions.

"Big companies like Nvidia and Meta, they're holding all the cards," he said. "When they decide that the game is over, that's it — it's going to be curtains for some of these smaller companies."

— CNBC'S Kif Leswing and Isabel O'Brien contributed reporting.

#2905. Anthropic raises $93.5 billion total as underwriters scoop up ‘greenshoe’ overallotment

Anthropic underwriters have officially exercised their overallotment of shares in the historic initial public offering, bringing the total raised to $93.5 billion, according to a release Friday.

The artificial intelligence company raised $81.3 billion on Wednesday and netted an additional $12.2 billion through the overallotment, surpassing SpaceX, which held a record IPO in June.

Anthropic said it sold an aggregate of 1,127,000,000 shares of its Class A common stock at $83 per share in the offering. It sold 980 million shares on Wednesday and its underwriters had the option to purchase up to an additional 147 million shares by Friday as part of the overallotment, which is commonly referred to as the "greenshoe."

Shares of the Claude maker opened trading at $111.50 on the Nasdaq on Thursday, but slid throughout the day and closed at $97.17. The stock sank 12% in premarket trading on Friday during a broad tech sell-off.

In addition to the sale of its Class A common stock, Anthropic closed a concurrent sale of its Class N common stock after a group of tech companies, including Advanced Micro Devices, Google, Microsoft, Nvidia and others, agreed to participate in a $26 billion private placement at the IPO price. Anthropic said Friday that aggregate gross proceeds from its IPO, including the private placement, totaled $119.5 billion, before deducting offering expenses.

Anthropic dodged an unforgiving series of obstacles on its road to the Nasdaq on Thursday, as investors brooded over Jensen Huang's shocking ouster at Nvidia, voters' stinging rebuke of the tech during the midterms and whether rising interest rates would stymie industry plans for a multitrillion-dollar AI infrastructure buildout.

CEO Dario Amodei co-founded the company in 2021 after he and a handful of other researchers, including his sister Daniela, quit their jobs at OpenAI, now Anthropic's chief rival. Anthropic is best known for developing a family of AI models called Claude, which have captivated the corporate world and fueled the company's historic rise.

The siblings celebrated the IPO as a "milestone" in an interview with CNBC on Thursday, but said there's more work ahead for Anthropic.

"For us, it's like one stop on the journey," Daniela Amodei said. "We think about this as, 'Wow, this is an incredible moment for Anthropic for our mission, but we're going to wake up tomorrow and we're going to be the same company.'"

#2904. Yankees Mailbag: The DH question

Good afternoon everyone, it's time to dive back into the mailbag and answer some of your questions. Remember to send in your questions for our bi-weekly call by e-mail to pinstripealleyblog [at] gmail [dot] com.

jmack175 asks: With Judge getting older and needing to stay healthy, do you see a scenario where he gets more at bats at DH this season, and at some point when Stanton has his annual muscle strain from an intentional walk, Judge is fully transitioned to DH and Stanton is released? If that doesn't play out, how many more seasons do you see Judge playing RF?

Not right away, at least not with how the roster as constructed stands. The Yankees have their designated hitter spot locked up between Stanton, Ben Rice, and Luis García Jr. at the moment, though the team's future with Stanton is currently cloudy at best. For now, the team is going through with bringing Stanton to spring training and seeing what he's got left in the tank, but even removing him from the equation still leaves the position filled up with a hitter that the team wants in the lineup more often than not. There's also been some speculation that the Yankees could turn around and flip García after acquiring him at the deadline as part of their effort to find a quality third baseman to complete the infield, shifting Rice back to first base. Rice admittedly wasn't that good of a defender despite working with Paul Goldschmidt though, and the team certainly won't risk moving him back behind the plate at this point with how valuable he is to the team's offense.

Personally, I don't think it's worthwhile to lose García's bat unless there's no other avenue to upgrade third, and with the team's incredible starting pitcher depth as well as the prospects they could part with I think there are plenty of other routes they can consider. As for Judge, while it is concerning that the last two major injuries he's sustained have been on maximum-effort plays in the outfield (I'm still willing to consider the Dodger Stadium wall collision as more of a freak accident, but c'est la vie) he's still a tremendous outfielder when healthy and the team would do well to maximize the value he can bring by playing him out there as long as they can. Cody Bellinger provides excellent defense in left field, but we saw how much of a struggle the other two positions were for the team on a regular basis last year, and they may not be able to find a true center fielder this offseason to improve the defense with most options for the outfield being bat-first players. I don't know if Judge can still be Judge out there if he's giving 80-90 percent effort out there, but his cannon arm shouldn't be too affected by telling him to avoid a full-out dive for the ball and that change alone could be enough.

jws85 asks: Forget rumors of Seager, Bohm, Skubal, Jeffers, etc. Boone has one year left and to spend money on long term contracts to get the same old results — start the year on fire, sleep through the summer, make the playoffs, and get bounced early — makes no sense. Why hamstring a new manager with contracts that jam the roster, let the current roster with the addition of kids or one year cheap players get through 2027, and then go crazy for 2028.

We're making a lot of assumptions here, so let's break this down. Aaron Boone is on a lame-duck contract, yes, but there's no guarantee that means they'll move on from him at the end of next year — or replace him with someone fundamentally different. If the team can't accomplish more than another ALDS flameout three years in a row, I do think it's unlikely that Boone gets handed another contract, but why set yourself up for failure just to move on from Boone when he's at worst a minor part of the problem? The organization should be in win-now mode with their superstars getting up in age, and while they shouldn't take on a bloated contract for a player that'll only have one or two more positive years at best they should certainly be aiming to put together a World Series-caliber team instead of trusting that the kids can fill the gaps. They developed Rice and Cam Schlittler from unheralded prospect status into stardom, sure, but they made it to the majors by making the team unable to ignore them much like George Lombard Jr did this season.

Dax Kilby is a rising star in the organization but he's still years away from the majors, and after him the rest of their top 10 prospects are all pitchers. The best-case usage of the kids in this scenario is finding smart trades to fill out the lineup, as they simply can't all play. The only argument I'll make in favor of not adding too much to the payroll for 2027 is that the team likely won't be in the Tarik Skubal sweepstakes anyway and the other big ticket names don't impress me as much, but if they were I wouldn't hate it. Hand him a bag and the Yankees would instantly have the best rotation in baseball bar none, and still have depth to trade away to improve around the margins.

#2903. Victor Wembanyama has turned his NBA finals humiliation into a revenge tour for the ages

It wouldn't be exactly right to say Victor Wembanyama didn't miss a beat after a disastrous NBA finals in June that left him and his San Antonio Spurs defeated, memed and embarrassed. In the Spurs' first game this season, against an Oklahoma City Thunder team since decimated by injuries, Wemby went an appalling 1-for-13 from the field. Ten of his 12 points came via free throws. After getting blocked by Aday Mara, he leaned over his knees and muttered furiously to himself as if trying to exorcise an intrusive thought. It brought to mind an image from the NBA finals a few months earlier: Wemby on the floor after being fouled by the Knicks' Mitchell Robinson, crowing, "I'm in your head!" The Spurs went on to blow a 29-point lead, lose the game and the finals. Now Wemby seemed too preoccupied with the issues within his own dome to worry about wreaking havoc in anybody else's.

Related: Steph Curry's defense of Ayesha exposes the ugly double standards of sports' manosphere | Andrew Lawrence

Game two saw Wembanyama elect to lay the ball up instead of dunking it in the dying seconds of a close tilt with the Houston Rockets, which the springy Amen Thompson blocked spectacularly. Not a great start, and in a league whose narratives calcify long before sample sizes reach an adequate scale, it allowed for whispers that the Spurs were in trouble. But you could say Wembanyama has not missed a beat since then. After beginning the season 0-2, the Spurs are now 13-3, with 12 of those wins coming in a row before a loss to the Detroit Pistons on Tuesday.

San Antonio's basketball hasn't been as seamless as those numbers suggest – the starting lineup includes three guards, leaving the offense a bit clunky and wanting for three-point shooting. It also includes Wembanyama, which papers over pretty much every possible problem.

Unofficially crowned the best player in the world during last season's playoffs, Wembanyama has since distanced his closest challengers for that honor. Somebody has stolen Nikola Jokić's three-point shot and hidden it in a deep hole, signaling what may prove to be the start of his decline after six consecutive imperious, MVP-caliber seasons. Meanwhile, Shai Gilgeous-Alexander's efficiency has inevitably suffered as he's tasked with keeping the injury-ridden Thunder afloat.

Wembanyama, meanwhile, has got better, averaging 28.6 points, 11.9 rebounds and countless plays in which his mere presence causes the opposing offense to spontaneously combust through the first 16 games of the season. The Lakers' Luka Dončić is playing the best basketball of his career this season, averaging gaudy offensive numbers that, if sustained, would represent the most dominant scoring season in modern NBA history. That Dončić is not the runaway early favorite for MVP is solely because of Wembanyama's two-way excellence.

Wembanyama's unprecedented impact on the game of basketball is already well-documented, though the man is only 22. He is at once a void and a force field on defense, vacuuming up plenty of shots with his inflatable-man arms and deterring others simply by standing in the paint. Before the Pistons beat the Spurs on Tuesday, their players warmed up by shooting over teammates who held long foam blocks over their heads, practicing shots with sufficiently steep arcs to escape Wemby's treacherous fingertips, trying to learn how to play the game on a vertical axis.

Wembanyama racked up plenty of offensive highlights during last year's playoffs – a half-court buzzer-beater, a logo three-pointer to tie a vital game in the final minute when the rest of the team had gone cold – but his defensive foundation and paint presence are so strong that any three-pointers he can generate are a bonus.

Wembanyama was maligned during the finals for the lack of a "go-to move", which clogged up the Spurs' offense as the Knicks erased double-digit deficits late in games as if they were playing a G League team. The Spurs' game against the Pistons offered a clue to what he's developing: twice in the first half, Wembanyama threw the ball off the backboard, to himself, so he could collect it and dunk as everybody else watched from the floor. A player this physically anomalous and versatile will eventually have a bag so deep nobody will be able to see the bottom.

Despite Wembanyama's visually obvious gifts, there are still times when the magnitude of their manifestation on a game is shocking. In a blowout against the Sacramento Kings, he needed only 25 minutes to rack up 40 points, 11 rebounds and eight blocks. In a close game against the Dallas Mavericks, he produced 52 points, 24 rebounds and eight blocks in 34 minutes. On one possession, he blocked three consecutive Mavericks shots, an entire defense unto himself. The Spurs won the game by one point; during Wembanyama's minutes, they outscored the Mavs by 23. "I haven't been feeling great lately," Wembanyama said after the game. "I'm sick. I felt like I got my legs back. So, it should be more like this more often." Well then.

Even what felt like a fairly quiet Wembanyama performance against the Los Angeles Clippers on Thursday night (he only recorded one block, just before the end of the third quarter) dictated the shape of the game. The numbers are one thing – 32 points, 11 rebounds, no biggie – and watching him is another. He threw down fierce alley-oop dunks. He galloped through the paint, an Ent trampling helpless orcs, for easy floaters. Late in the fourth quarter, he juggled the ball to himself above the basket, reminiscent of a child batting a balloon across a room at a birthday party, before tapping it into the basket.

He found himself with so much space on one play that he rose as if to dunk the ball and dropped it gently through the net instead, flicking his wrist as if he'd just shot a three. On one of those jammed-up Spurs offensive possessions, the Clippers' Isaiah Jackson was guarding Wembanyama tightly as the shot clock wound down. Wemby took a long two-pointer with Jackson's hand in his face, a bizarre shot created from thin air, and drained it.

Wembanyama's highlights won't get old until 7ft 4in generational talents become commonplace, but equally impressive at this point in his career is his maturity. The finals last season were a nightmare on a few fronts: he enabled a legion of detractors with overly violent fouls and dubious flops, then validated their harshest critiques by underperforming in vital games. It'd be enough to ruin plenty of mortals. Wembanyama has emerged from the adversity intact and evolved, keeping the Spurs firmly on track for a shot at redemption.

#2901. Trump says he discussed Jensen Huang with Xi, apparently contradicting Bessent

President Donald Trump on Friday said he and Chinese leader Xi Jinping discussed their shared affinity for former Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang this week, appearing to contradict Treasury Secretary Scott Bessent who earlier in the day said the ousted leader was not mentioned in the Trump administration's latest talks with China in Shenzhen.

"I did discuss that with President Xi, and he said, 'Oh, brilliant, brilliant! He's a brilliant man.' He is," Trump said at a White House press conference.

Asked earlier Friday on CNBC's "Squawk on the Street" if Huang, a close ally of Trump's, had come up in those talks, Bessent said "no," without elaborating.

The Treasury Department did not immediately respond to CNBC's request for comment on the apparent discrepancy between Trump and Bessent.

Trump also reiterated that he would like to see Huang — who was replaced by interim CEO Colette Kress after resigning amid allegations of an extramarital affair with a Chinese spy — be restored as Nvidia's CEO.

"I would love to see Jensen be given a second chance," Trump said. "You know, we're a country of second chances, and I'd love to be able to see him. That's a very important company to us, and he put us in a position where we are literally 10 years advanced over other companies."

Nvidia's board is not considering bringing Huang back, a person familiar with the matter told CNBC earlier this week.

The president's comments came amid growing scrutiny of advanced U.S. chips, including those made by Nvidia, ending up in China either legally or by being diverted via third countries.

Both Trump's and Bessent's remarks are potentially an indication that the Trump administration may not focus on cracking down on China's efforts to acquire advanced chips.

Bessent suggested that U.S. policy allowing the sale of Nvidia's powerful H200 AI chips to China remains unchanged following the talks in Shenzhen this week.

"We've already agreed that the ... H200s are the threshold level of chips that could be sold to the Chinese, and, you know, that continues as planned," Bessent said.

"I wouldn't anticipate any change in the policy," he added after CNBC's Sara Eisen noted that Nvidia said it had stopped selling H200s to China.

The administration ignoring the subject of Huang's affair with a possible Chinese spy — whose Singapore-based company Megaspeed has been accused of acting as a cutout for Beijing — contrasts with the importance Trump has placed on beating China in the race to artificial intelligence dominance. It could suggest that despite his support for the U.S. AI industry, Trump is less concerned with addressing China's ongoing efforts to acquire U.S. semiconductors.

Trump has also defended Commerce Secretary Howard Lutnick, who has come under fire from multiple directions for reportedly slow-walking export restrictions on Chinese chip sales. "I understand both sides of it," Trump said on Monday when he also said he's like to see Huang back as CEO.

Bessent's appearance on CNBC came after Trump's return from a tour through Asia that saw him meet with Xi and Russian President Vladimir Putin in Shenzhen, China.

The meeting was historic as the first time the leaders of the three countries had met without other nations' counterparts. But the trilateral summit appeared to yield few deliverables on any number of the most pressing global issues related to the countries, including the Ukraine and Iran wars or the ongoing AI race.

Bessent told CNBC on Friday that there was a "robust discussion" between Trump and Xi on trade policy and AI.

They "agreed that humans should always be involved in any military decisions that SI makes, and the SI should not be allowed to be autonomous for major military actions," he said, abbreviating the term "super intelligence," Trump's attempted rebrand of AI.

In a CNBC appearance last week, Bessent lamented the "leakage" of U.S.-made AI chips to China and blamed the Commerce Department, led by Lutnick, for slow-walking export controls on suspected Chinese cutouts like Megaspeed.

Multiple Trump administration leaders have long sought to impose export restrictions on Megaspeed, but Commerce "has the pen" and "you will have to ask them about their internal processes," Bessent said in that interview.

On Friday, however, Bessent was much more tight-lipped.

Asked if there is a dispute between him and Lutnick over how aggressively the U.S. should be pursuing Chinese export controls, Bessent said, "I think we're all committed to following President Trump's instructions."

"I think we are going to see that the ... proper chip restrictions are in place, and the remote access has been curtailed," he added. Commerce recently took steps to close one of China's paths to accessing AI processors.

Correction: Treasury Secretary Scott Bessent spoke to CNBC's "Squawk on the Street." A previous version of this story misstated the name of the show.

#2900. Atlético play in Mineiro U15 and U17 finals this Saturday

Atlético's U-15 and U-17 teams take to the field this Saturday (21) at Toca da Raposa I for the first legs of the Campeonato Mineiro finals. The U-15s play at 8 a.m., while the U-17s kick off at 10 a.m.

The Crias reach the final unbeaten in 23 matches (14 in the first stage, seven in the eight-team stage and two in the knockout rounds), with 20 wins and three draws. Rômulo Rodrigues's side leads the attacking statistics with 70 goals scored. Forward Mauro is the competition's top scorer with 17 goals, followed by midfielder Moisés Caetano with 13.

The Crias also stand out at the back, boasting the best defensive record in the competition. They have conceded just seven goals in total. Standout performers include goalkeeper Luiz Octávio and the two players called up to the national team: centre-back Victão and right-back Pietro.

The clash with Cruzeiro will be the teams' third meeting in the competition. In their first encounter, on matchday 11 of the first stage, the Crias cruised to a 5–0 win at Cidade do Galo, with forward Maurinho scoring a hat-trick. The second match came on matchday three of the eight-team stage at Toca da Raposa I and ended in a 1–0 win for Atlético, thanks to a goal from Daniel Costa.

#2898. Jensen Huang's — and Nvidia's — legal exposure balloons

The still-unraveling scandal of former Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang's extramarital affairs and the tech giant's potential role in its advanced semiconductor chips ending up in China present a legal minefield for Huang and Nvidia.

Federal and state prosecutors and Nvidia employees may target Huang and the company in court, where some shareholders have already taken action.

That's on top of a rapidly growing pile of probes by Republicans who control Congress now and Democrats who will have majorities in both the House and Senate come January.

"It's not going to get any better for Jensen Huang; it's going to get far worse," said Salvatore Graziano, a New York securities litigation attorney at Bernstein, Litowitz, Berger & Grossmann. His firm is looking at shareholder lawsuit opportunities against Nvidia and Huang.

[Huang] "faces the Department of Justice, the Securities and Exchange Commission and private litigation," Graziano said. "The criminal exposure is very serious. The SEC could bar him from being an officer. And the potential liability [from shareholder] lawsuits can be very large."

Huang's and Nvidia's legal exposure stems from three issues that led up to his shock resignation Nov. 12.

There's his alleged affair with suspected Chinese spy Alice Huang, who worked for Megaspeed, a Singapore-based company under investigation for diverting advanced Nvidia semiconductor chips to China in violation of U.S. export rules. That, in turn, has raised questions about whether Huang and Nvidia were aware of any diversions.

There are Huang's two other alleged affairs with Nvidia employees.

And there is the sharp drop in the company's stock price in the wake of the scandal. Nvidia's share price has fallen 19% since Nov. 8, when Huang sent a company-wide apology acknowledging extramarital affairs.

Huang last week retained the law firm Paul, Weiss and its attorneys Melinda Haag and Walter Brown to represent him. Haag is a former U.S. attorney in San Francisco, and Brown is also a former federal prosecutor.

Huang's spokesman at Trident, a crisis communications firm, declined to comment when asked if Haag and Brown are representing him in any potential criminal case and other questions about his legal exposure.

Nvidia did not respond to a request for comment. Last week, Nvidia retained the law firm Wachtell, Lipton, Rosen & Katz to conduct an independent investigation into Huang allegedly being targeted by a spy, as U.S. intelligence reportedly has concluded.

The civil liability Huang and Nvidia could face is underscored by a hearing Graziano, the securities attorney, attended Tuesday in U.S. District Court in San Francisco. A judge at the hearing approved a $175 million settlement from Seagate Technology Holdings to resolve a shareholder lawsuit alleging it concealed sales of hard-disk drives to Huawei in China in violation of export bans.

The Seagate settlement came three years after the Commerce Department's Bureau of Industry and Security — which is investigating Megaspeed's business with Nvidia — imposed a $300 million penalty on Seagate for its sales to Huawei.

Graziano said his law firm is "actively looking at securities law liability for Nvidia, Jensen Huang and other senior executives in the company given all the recent developments regarding their [potential] knowledge and facilitation of illegal chip sales to Chinese entities."

There is already a pending class-action shareholder lawsuit in San Francisco federal court against Nvidia and Huang by the Rosen Law Firm, for allegedly misleading and false statements related to Megaspeed and Alice Huang.

"Several hundreds of investors have contacted us and expressed an interest to join the case — investors of all walks of life, from mom and pops to investors with very large losses," Rosen attorney Phil Kim told CNBC in an email.

Katie Sweeten, a former federal prosecutor in the DOJ's National Security Division, said the question of "who knew what and when, and what was [Huang's] role" is potentially the biggest risk for Nvidia and him.

"Now DOJ can leverage their criminal authorities," Sweeten, a partner at Scale LLP, told CNBC's Kelly Evans on "The Exchange."

"They can bring grand jury subpoenas, search warrants for emails, bank accounts, personal devices, computers, things like that," Sweeten said.

While it is "not illegal to, you know, sleep with a spy ... it is illegal to pass certain types of information to a counterintelligence officer," she said.

Sweeten also pointed to potential export control violations, which "could be very serious."

She noted the DOJ can indict a company as well as its officials if there is evidence of criminality. Export control violations carry a maximum possible criminal sentence of 20 years in prison and "really large fines," she said.

"You can't send the company to prison, but they can face fines, civil penalties, debarment — where you can't export items down the line," she said.

One example of that came in July 2025, when the DOJ obtained a guilty plea and an agreement to pay more than $140 million from Cadence Design Systems for violating the Export Control Reform Act by unlawfully exporting semiconductor design tools to China's National University of Defense Technology, a school led by China's Central Military Commission.

Another potential legal risk for Huang comes from his planned appearances before Congress.

Huang is scheduled to give a closed-door interview Dec. 1 to three House committees. Senate banking committee Chair Tim Scott, R-S.C., wants Huang's testimony before that panel to be public, and told CNBC Tuesday he hopes to have that happen "before Christmas."

Sweeten said Huang "has to be very careful" about what he says there because of the risk of being criminally charged with lying to Congress, and of saying something "that absolutely can become part of a criminal or civil investigation."

"I expect Huang to take the Fifth on most questions" from Congress, Jonathan Kanter, a former assistant attorney general in the DOJ's Antitrust Division, told CNBC, meaning Huang will likely use his constitutional right against giving testimony that could be used against him in a criminal case.

"With a Justice Department criminal investigation into Megaspeed already underway and more investigations possible, anything he tells Congress could be used against him," Kanter said. "His lawyers are likely to advise him against taking that risk."

Ben Heineman Jr., a former senior vice president and general counsel of General Electric, and a senior fellow at Harvard University, told CNBC that Nvidia's board will be scrutinized over its actions before it pushed Huang to resign.

Heineman cited the board's apparent lack of succession planning and what looks like weak oversight of Huang, including relating to personal conduct such as the two alleged affairs with Nvidia employees and with an executive of an Nvidia customer.

"When there is a sort of reasonable suspicion, they need to investigate it, and they don't appear to have done that," Heineman said.

Nvidia and Huang could face investigation from state attorneys general, including in California, where the company is headquartered.

On Tuesday, California Attorney General Rob Bonta told Bloomberg Television that the situation at Nvidia with Huang is "something that we're interested in." Bonta's office later told CNBC, "We are monitoring the situation."

Huang's alleged affairs with Nvidia employees also open the possibility of workplace litigation.

Ari Wilkenfeld, a Washington-area employment lawyer, said a consensual relationship between a chief executive and a subordinate "is not in and of itself illegal."

"The liability never comes from the existence of the relationship alone. It comes from whether it was genuinely welcome, what job benefits flowed from it, and what it did to everyone else in the building," Wilkenfeld said. "California law recognizes that so-called sexual favoritism can create a hostile environment for an entire workforce.

"The affair is Huang's problem," Wilkenfeld said. "What, if anything, Nvidia did once it knew is the company's problem."

#2897. Barcelona and Juventus could swap players in January

Juventus are interested in signing Alejandro Balde, while Barcelona have identified Andrea Cambiaso as a player they would like to add to their squad. This has raised the possibility of a swap deal between the two European giants, with both clubs potentially benefiting from an exchange during the next transfer window.

It would not be the first time Juventus and Barcelona have exchanged players. The two clubs previously completed a deal in the summer of 2020, when Miralem Pjanic moved to Barcelona and Arthur Melo joined Juventus. However, that arrangement failed to deliver the expected results for either side.

Both clubs could now explore another exchange as they look for solutions to their respective squad requirements and hope for a more successful outcome this time.

Cambiaso has been one of Juventus' most important players, but they could be willing to consider his departure because of his injury problems over recent seasons. He has also struggled to maintain his fitness during the current campaign, making his future an issue the Bianconeri may need to address.

The situation could create an opportunity for Barcelona, who are keen to strengthen their options with Cambiaso. The Italian is reportedly open to leaving Juventus, provided he receives an offer from a top club, meaning a move to the Spanish giants could appeal to him.

Meanwhile, Balde could also consider a departure if it offers him the opportunity to enjoy more regular playing time. Juventus would be an attractive destination if they can convince him that he would have an important role in their team.

According to Fichajes, there is a good possibility that Juventus and Barcelona could negotiate a player exchange in the next transfer window.

Barcelona would need to consider how to replace Balde if he leaves, while Juventus would have to assess whether losing Cambiaso makes sense for their defensive plans. Both decisions would depend on the clubs' respective requirements and the players' willingness to complete the moves.

Although nothing has been finalised, the possibility of another exchange between these European giants presents an interesting transfer scenario. The previous deal between them was unsuccessful, but both sides could be hoping for a better outcome this time.
