#2896. Forget Anthropic: In Europe, the IPO hype is about a blue lagoon

While investors are fixated on Anthropic's blockbuster stock market debut, a very different IPO is making waves in Europe: Iceland's Blue Lagoon, the operator of one of the world's most famous geothermal spas.

Blue Lagoon Corp. raised roughly 14.3 billion Icelandic kronur ($110 million) through the offering, valuing the Icelandic geothermal spa operator at about $787 million, according to the company's investor presentation.

The Blue Lagoon traces its origins to 1992, when Blue Lagoon Ltd. was founded to study the water in the Blue Lagoon, an open-air pool of warm, milky-blue water set in a black lava field in southwestern Iceland. It grew into a business spanning a spa, hotels and skincare made with ingredients from the lagoon.

Shares will trade under the ticker "BLUE" on one of Europe's smallest stock exchanges, Nasdaq Iceland Main Market, part of the Nasdaq Nordic group, which also operates exchanges in Sweden, Denmark and Finland.

"Wellness is the new luxury, and we know that the wellness economy is growing faster than the economy as a whole, so we have tailwind from that," CEO Sigríður Margrét Oddsdóttir told CNBC on Thursday.

"Now is the right time for us to list," she added. "We are a company that thinks long-term, we have a clear vision for the next decade, and being publicly listed gives investors the opportunity to participate in our journey."

The company's operations are centered on Iceland's Reykjanes Peninsula, where, as well as the Blue Lagoon geothermal spa, it runs the Silica Hotel, the Retreat luxury hotel and spa, and the Michelin-starred Moss restaurant.

It also operates the Kerlingarfjöll Highland Base and Hrauneyjar properties in Iceland's highlands, and sells Blue Lagoon-branded skincare products internationally.

The 34-year-old company has averaged 11% annual revenue growth over the past decade, and next year is expected to be a record, with revenue growing by more than half, Oddsdóttir said.

The company plans to use the raised capital to fund expanding its existing properties, acquiring businesses, and developing new destinations.

In total, nearly 4,000 subscriptions were received in the initial public offering, worth about 15.3 billion kronur, about 7% more than the total size of the offering, the company said, citing strong demand from smaller investors, including retail investors.

Iceland's relatively small stock market had just 27 companies on its main exchange at the end of 2025, according to Nasdaq. The market is dominated by banks, seafood companies and other domestically focused firms.

The global wellness industry reached $7.5 trillion in 2025, up 8.9% from the previous year, according to the Global Wellness Institute. Spending on thermal and mineral springs grew 10.4% over the same period, with the institute forecasting annual growth of nearly 10% for the sector through 2030.

Tourism accounted for nearly 9% of Iceland's GDP in 2025, and is growing, according to Statistics Iceland. The country welcomed around 2.3 million international visitors that year, with the U.S. as its largest source market.

#2894. Warsh wants Fed officials to talk less. Instead, they’re talking more

In his first six months as Federal Reserve chairman, Kevin Warsh has diligently avoided signaling where he sees monetary policy and interest rates headed. His colleagues have been only too happy to fill the void.

Fed officials have been rushing to the microphones lately, voicing sometimes conflicting opinions on how the central bank should be handling an inflation bug that the economy can't seem to shake.

Most agree that rates should be raised — and soon. Some are willing to hike at a measured pace as conditions play out, while others, such as Governor Christopher Waller and St. Louis Fed President Alberto Musalem, entertain the thought of more aggressive hikes in a shorter period of time.

Largely on the sidelines in the debate is Warsh, who has expressed disdain for that sort of forward guidance and argued that it handcuffs policymakers long before they actually have to make a decision. But that hasn't stopped most of the other 18 officials who sit in on Federal Open Market Committee meetings.

In fact, according to a Bank of America analysis, there has been more so-called Fedspeak in the past two weeks than in any similar period since the start of 2025.

"Warsh's refusal to guide markets might be encouraging the rest of the FOMC to speak more, not less," Bank of America economist Aditya Bhave said in a note Thursday.

"There is always a range of views across the 19 FOMC participants. Typically, the Chair would weigh in, indicating to markets whether the hawks or doves are in ascendence," he added. "Absent this leadership, however, both sides might feel incentivized to speak more often and more forcefully."

The increasingly vocal debate comes at a critical time for the Fed and has myriad policy implications.

Bhave noted "a deepening divide" on the FOMC. The Fed's decision not to increase its benchmark rate at its October meeting "increasingly looks like a mistake," and as a result "the hawks have been very vocal of late," he wrote. Leading the charge is Waller, who has raised the possibility of hiking by half a percentage point when the committee next meets in December.

Minutes from the October meeting hint at the tension, particularly when it comes to Warsh's refusal to provide guidance.

The summary related that some officials who were looking for a hike in October "could have supported no change in the federal funds rate provided that it was clearly understood by market participants that further tightening was likely" — a statement that points toward the forward guidance that Warsh has insisted the Fed avoid.

The minutes "revealed that some officials favoring a hike would have accepted a hold if the Fed had clearly communicated further tightening," market veteran Ed Yardeni wrote Thursday. "Warsh's communication philosophy may thus be limiting the Fed's flexibility precisely when it needs it most."

Cleveland Fed President Beth Hammack, who voted against the October pause in favor of a quarter-point hike, tiptoed around the forward guidance issue during a recent public appearance.

Prefacing some observations she made about where she sees policy headed, Hammack noted that, "Those are not promises. Those are not commitments. They're not forward guidance, which is a hot-button topic. But what they are are a conditional set of expectations of where I think the economy can go, based on what I'm seeing."

Hammack's comments illustrate a needle that Fed officials have been trying to thread: continuing to be transparent about their thinking without appearing locked into a future policy that changing economic conditions could render obsolete.

Speaking Thursday, Chicago Fed President Austan Goolsbee framed the argument around credibility.

"I am sympathetic to the view that [Warsh] has expressed that let's not be so, in in our language, forward guidance-oriented, saying here is what I think rates are going to be at the next meeting," Goolsbee said, calling such pronouncements "falsely precise."

However, he quickly separated forward guidance from transparency.

"I think central banks should be careful about doing anything that undermines credibility," Goolsbee said. "That said, I also think that it's pretty important [that] each person who sits around that table explain how they see the world. What do you see in business conditions? You don't want to surprise everybody."

Taking a slightly different tack, Philadelphia Fed President Anna Paulson, in a speech Thursday on policy communication, insisted that markets can figure out where policymakers are going without specific guidance so long as they understand how economic developments will influence decisions.

Paulson also defended the variety of voices coming from the Fed.

"But volume is not the same as clarity," she said. "The measure is not how much we say. It is whether we are understood." The mixture of voices "guards against any single communication becoming the only one that matters."

Financial markets have taken the plethora of Fed communications and interpreted it in a fairly hawkish way.

Traders are implying a 100% chance of at least a quarter percentage point hike in December, with a very small probability of a half-point move, according to the CME Group's FedWatch. From there, odds are high for a follow-up increase in January, then perhaps one more before the end of 2027.

While current inflation indicators are running above 3%, compared to the Fed's 2% target, market measures of longer-term inflation have declined.

Five-year forward expectations, as indicated through the Treasury market, are at their lowest since April. That suggests investors could be less concerned about inflation over the longer term, even as policymakers warn that current price pressures remain elevated.

Still, a sclerotic communications strategy from the Fed amid volatile economic and geopolitical currents could prove dangerous ahead.

"The result of this back-and-forth is undue rates volatility in already turbulent times," BofA's Bhave said.

For his part, Warsh has been advocating for a "family fight" atmosphere inside the Fed, arguing that competing viewpoints deserve a full airing as the committee works towards decisions.

"There are benefits to shaping the market's future expectations. Perhaps this is part of the 'family fight' that Warsh keeps referencing in his pressers," said Tom Graff, chief investment officer at advisory firm Facet. "That family fight seems to be happening out in public."

#2893. Is a bottom in for the Nasdaq-100? Charts to watch

(See the video above for Frank's complete chart breakdown.)

Is the Nasdaq-100's recent bounce a sign that the bottom is in? We're going to look at several charts to help answer that question.

First, let's remember how we got here. In September, the NDX broke out from a bullish cup-and-handle pattern, producing a 33,200-upside target. That breakout quickly failed, however, and gave way to a bearish pattern that broke down aggressively two weeks ago.

The decline brought the index relatively close to its 26,350 downside target before the current rebound began.

Zooming out, it's telling to see where the bounce originated. Three important forms of support converged in the 25,000-26,000 area:

This confluence of support helped encourage buying after a roughly 15% decline.

For perspective, we also see what larger drawdowns of 20%, 30%, 40%, and 50% would look like … a lot worse.

The following chart tracks NDX drawdowns from 52-week highs going back to just after the financial crisis. Only a handful of declines exceeded 20%, and three produced relatively quick reversals: late 2018, the Covid lows in 2020 and April 2025.

Historically, buying into declines of approximately 15%–25% has generally paid off, even when additional downside occurred first (yellow highlights).

The chart also highlights previous declines of 10%–16% in green, putting the current pullback into perspective.

The major exception was 2022, when the NDX eventually declined nearly 36% over approximately 10 months ... a much worse outcome, which many investors are concerned could happen again now.

That raises the key question: Are we experiencing another routine correction, or the early stages of a larger bear market?

One important clue will be how far this bounce progresses.

Any rally that fails to reclaim the previous highs could establish a significant lower high. Additional lower highs would provide stronger evidence of a developing, longer downtrend.

We saw this in March 2022, when that first sharp rebound effort failed to reach the previous high before a much more severe decline followed.

The bottom line: The current bounce is encouraging, particularly given where it originated and knowing how well buying prior 10-20% drawdowns has paid off.

From here, it's all about the follow through.  We don't need to see new highs right away, but any rally from here that's met with another wave of strong selling could reignite downside momentum.

Frank Cappelleri is the founder and president of CappThesis, an independent research firm that helps active investors through time-tested chart and statistical analysis. Prior to starting CappThesis, he spent 25 years on Wall Street as an equity sales trader, technical analyst, research sales specialist and desk strategist.

#2890. Fantasy Football Rankings: Players Hayden Winks is higher and lower on than consensus for Week 11

Fantasy football analyst Hayden Winks shares the players he's higher — and lower — on than Yahoo consensus for Week 11.

These rankings will be tinkered with all the way until kickoff, so please check back in on our weekly rankings pages and Sunday AM sit/start show for the absolute latest.

These blurbs are also in The Blueprint, which is my weekly rankings and analysis column. The RB version is out on Wednesdays, while the WR version and the QB/TE/K/DEF version are each out on Thursdays before Thursday Night Football.

Bo Nix has more games below 10.0 fantasy points (three) than he does above 20.0 (two) so far. It's largely the same issues as in previous seasons, but to his credit, things historically have looked way better in the second half of seasons. I haven't noticed that he's a worse athlete coming off the dislocated ankle. It's more of just decision-making inside the pocket, but hopefully Sean Payton got into his head during this bye week. Hopefully Jaylen Waddle was listening, too!

To be fair, Nix has had the third-hardest schedule to date and the easiest schedule moving forward. This week kicks that off. The Raiders have been horrific on defense in both phases. They've allowed the second-most fantasy points to QBs and the second-most rushing yards to all positions. That's difficult to do! Nix has been the QB17, QB23, QB8 and QB8 against the Raiders historically, but this defense's current 6-game streak of allowing a top-13 fantasy QB is more important. Denver is set up well as 10-point home favorites with a 25.25-point team total (eighth).

Tate (shoulder) has been limited in practice after missing the last handful of games. This assumes he's ready to roll, while Wan'Dale Robinson was placed on IR and Calvin Ridley was demoted. It'll be Tate, Elic Ayomanor and Chimere Dike in 3-WR sets moving forward. That's not much of a competition for Tate, who has lived up to his NFL draft status to me. He can win on breaking routes and has great hands. His skill set stands to benefit from the changes the Titans have made offensively with Tate banged up:

Over his past four games, Tate is the WR25 on WR22 usage and WR20 targets per game. In good matchups, he can be viewed as a boom-bust WR2 now. The Cowboys are a great matchup. They've allowed the sixth-most fantasy points to WRs, while struggling to defend Keenan Allen and Deebo Samuel Sr. last week as the latest example. Michael Wilson is the closest real-life comp to Tate, and he hung 128-1 on them as the WR4 three games ago. Tennessee owns a 23.0-point team total this week, easily their highest of the season.

Hampton set season highs in fantasy points (17.0 half PPR) and usage (15.9 expected) last week, in their first win of the year. Hampton handled 22 carries in a run-heavy game plan that continued because the scoreboard finally allowed for it. The Chargers are first in neutral run rate (61%), so don't be surprised if Hampton's fantasy performances tick up if they can stay more competitive after a brutal schedule. You can see the extra carries in the second half of last week here:

On tape, Hampton was used slightly differently last week. He handled nine pitches, which is the second-most by a player in a single game all year. Instead of being pointed to the perimeter, many of them got him going straight downhill, where his attributes are maximized. They also ran more man concepts instead of the wide zone stuff OC Mike McDaniel typically calls for. It's a meeting in the middle between player and coach, one that was long overdue.

This week, the Chargers are favored. I repeat, the Chargers are favored against the Jets. They don't have their best run-stopping DT right now either, which has helped opposing teams rack up 154 rushing yards per game this month (fourth). James Cook (RB7) and Kenneth Walker (RB1) just went nuclear against them, but even Ollie Gordon had 103 total yards and a score in Week 7, too. This is easily the best it's looked for Hampton since the games started this year.

Diggs is quietly the WR21 on WR24 usage this season and could hold the line with Terry McLaurin out for the season. He's not a full-time player, nor as quick as he once was, but his game ages gracefully as a crafty player with excellent hands and toughness. Diggs has been manufactured some targets on top of being their most relied-upon underneath and intermediate target.

This is an excellent spot for him with the Bengals expected to put a beating on the Commanders' defense. Game script should be on his side, and he needs these 3-WR sets to get onto the field. On top of that, the Bengals have a fantastic CB1 in D.J. Turner on the perimeter, but Diggs will avoid him and settle into where Cincy has been the worst at defending: the middle. Similar WRs like Chimere Dike (WR8) and Zay Flowers (WR3) have had great games against them recently, while traditional WR1s have struggled. The Commanders project for 23.75 points (11th), too.

Egbuka has been more good than great on tape this year. He battled a toe injury in training camp and then has picked up new injuries along the way. Baker Mayfield said he "looks great right now" after hinting that the injuries have prevented him from taking off earlier this year. For what it's worth, Egbuka is not on the injury report at all right now and that was not the case earlier this year.

Across the entire season, Egbuka is the WR33 on WR19 usage. Those are probably closer to his floor. We'll see if the Bucs going to more Tez Johnson forced Eguka to play outside more, but to put things simply, the Buccaneers are desperate for Egbuka to turn into an alpha. Everyone else doesn't have the skills to do that, and the pass game has been unwatchable. I can argue he is still frequently the designed first read on a bulk of the Bucs' dropbacks. Everyone has to just play better.

The Lions are too injured at all three levels to be nervous about the matchup. The Bucs are unlikely to be in full run the clock out mode like they've been in the past, and we'll see if CB1 D.J. Reed (hip) can suit up. There should be time for whoever is starting at QB to deliver the ball with Aidan Hutchinson out. A lowly 18.25-point team total holds expectations in check. We're chasing volume more than anything.

Lawrence has been in a groove as fantasy's QB8 (20.7 PPG), generating +0.29 EPA per dropback (QB3) with 21 passing touchdowns across nine starts. The only issue at times is not having a ton of volume due to game script, as the Jaguars easily lead the NFL in point differential. They're actually 31st all time in that metric through nine games.

This matchup is expected to be a bit closer, partially because the Giants defense has outkicked expectations. They've still allowed the seventh-most fantasy points to QBs, despite getting breakouts from rookie LB Arvell Reese and CB1 Deonte Banks. They're well coached with solid safety play. Where they've been lit up is against the scramble, as both Tyler Shough and Jayden Daniels have run like crazy. That's unlikely for Lawrence, so a more mediocre output wouldn't be too surprising. Jacksonville travels as 6-point road favorites with an implied team total of 26.25 points (seventh).

Gordon is now playing with fire after ripping off explosives as a new starter back in September. He's down to RB25 on RB13 usage this past month, thanks to 20 carries and just 1.5 receptions per game. His efficiency could crater without Malik Willis, as it did last week when he was the RB18 on RB10 usage. Trickier defenses than the Colts will give Kyle McCord more trouble.

The Bills are waking up on defense with a new DC. They've seen multiple true breakouts from their DL, and they're more sured up at S with Amani Hooker in the mix and moving Cole Bishop more into the box where he's at his best. Even while going through some pains, the Bills are still only allowing 93.6 rushing yards per game (25th). It's a bad matchup for Ollie with a team total of just 14.5 points.

Never get too high or too low on Jamo. He's a boom-bust WR22 just about every season now. This year, Williams is the WR25 on WR31 usage with stretches of WR1 and WR4 play.

This is a matchup to be more concerned with. The Bucs have two great CBs, and Zyon McCollum is just as good an athlete as Williams is. That's partially why the Bucs have allowed the fourth-fewest fantasy points to WRs on 15+ air-yard throws this year. Tampa Bay is a pass funnel defense, but a lot of that is in the Amon-Ra St. Brown and Sam LaPorta zone. It doesn't mean that Williams can't take it to the crib on low volume, but there have been better matchups per-game than this one. The Lions still have a 25.25-point team total (ninth).

Smith is expected to return after a pair of injuries have derailed his 2026 season. Since 2025, Smith is the WR21 on WR25 usage. While he doesn't have A.J. Brown to compete with, he also plays in a bottom-5 offense now. It's hard to calibrate all of the moving pieces, so Smith is a boom-bust WR3 until we have a better clue for how Joe Milton is going to fit in with Smith.

On top of that, the Steelers have an excellent DL that can spoil the entire game. Philly only has a 17.5-point team total, and I totally get it.

Christian Benford (groin) hasn't practiced, but the Bills added S Amani Hooker at the deadline and have a more menacing DL in 2026. It'll be almost entirely zone defense for Douglas to sort through with an inexperienced QB. It'll be a tough spot. To his credit, Douglas has been the WR22 on WR50 usage since returning from injury. He's a boom-bust WR3/4 with better odds of flaming out here.

#2887. A massive bearish trade just happened in the chip stocks

So much for buying the dip.As the S&P 500 entered correction territory and the Nasdaq-100 flirted with bear-market territory, down 19% from highs as of midday Friday, the single biggest options trade of the day was a near $100-million bet that the VanEck Semiconductor ETF (SMH) has a long way more to fall.Around 12:30 E.T., someone spent $93 million buying 20,000 380-strike puts expiring Sep. 17, 2027, according to trading data reviewed by SpotGamma and Volland, options-analytics platforms that both show the trade as a likely opening position, with volume exceeding existing open interest in the contract. The trade bets that the index will fall below $380, a nearly 8% decline from its current level. The SMH is off 40% from its recent all-time highs.

"That trade stands out big-time," wrote Jason DeLorenzo, founder and owner of Volland. "It is either a hedge or speculative, but either way it isn't a vote of confidence."The puts were bought for $46.50 a piece, an arguably expensive choice for a downside bet with volatility in the ETF trading at 70.

"You're paying a metric ton of Vega for that thing," added SpotGamma's Brent Kochuba, referring to the contract's sensitivity to changes in volatility of the underlying chip ETF. "Maybe they were a forced buyer, or maybe they're actually selling this, which can still be the case even though the price shows it happening closer to the ask."

The semiconductor index headed for a 9% drop this week alone.

#2886. LeBron Log No. 5: Popcorn in hand, a viral chorus and ‘basketball AI'

LeBron Log No. 5: Popcorn in hand, a viral chorus and 'basketball AI' originally appeared on NBC Sports Philadelphia

LeBron James spent Monday night in street clothes.

He still managed to argue a call with popcorn in his hand, practice his golf swing during a timeout and reject a report about himself before the Sixers left Brooklyn with a win.

That followed a Sunday afternoon in which James passed Jason Kidd on the NBA's career assists list and received an unusual description from Pacers head coach Rick Carlisle.

Welcome to the fifth edition of the LeBron Log.

James sat out the Sixers' 135-123 win over the Nets because of low back and sciatica management.

It was his fourth absence through 15 games. James has yet to play both ends of a back-to-back this season after logging nearly 33 minutes Sunday against Indiana.

He watched Monday's game from the end of the Sixers' bench in a yellow hat and an "Ohio Baby" sweatshirt. James snacked from a small bag before switching to popcorn.

When Joel Embiid believed he was fouled on a first-half drive, James stood and pleaded his case to official Jacyn Goble without putting down the box. He also joined conversations between teammates and officials during several timeouts.

James found another way to pass a third-quarter stoppage. As "Hot To Go" played inside Barclays Center, he practiced his golf swing near the bench.

His most pointed contribution came on Instagram.

An ESPN story by Dave McMenamin cited a Sixers source who said James had asked the coaching staff to remain patient while he worked toward finding his rhythm and conditioning by December.

James reposted ESPN's graphic and rejected the account.

"🧢! Those words never came out of my mouth!" he wrote.

The Sixers improved to 4-0 without James. They have beaten the Bucks, Hornets, Raptors and Nets in the games he has missed.

Brooklyn pulled within five points during the final five minutes Monday, putting the Sixers in another clutch game.

Philadelphia has reached 10 clutch wins more quickly than almost any team since the statistic began being tracked in 1996-97. The 2026-27 Sixers are the second team in that span with at least 10 such victories through their first 15 games.

The other was the 1997-98 Atlanta Hawks, who had 12.

James was also on the last team before Philadelphia to open a season with at least 10 consecutive clutch wins. The Lakers started 13-0 in those games last season.

James did not play in the Sixers' latest close finish, but he has now been part of the last two teams to start at least 10-0 under those circumstances.

Before Sunday's game, Carlisle explained why he tries to limit his play calls against James.

"LeBron James is one of the reasons we try not to have play calls, 'cause he knows all of them," Carlisle said. "He's a walking NBA basketball AI machine. That's what he is."

James followed with 15 points, 11 assists and seven rebounds in the Sixers' 131-124 win.

His pass to VJ Edgecombe for a layup with 1:19 remaining gave him 12,092 career assists and moved him past Kidd for third in NBA history. Only Chris Paul and John Stockton remain ahead of him.

Noah Levick wrote more about James' milestone and the anticipation behind his passes.

When told what Carlisle had called him, James appreciated the description.

"So, yeah, Rick is great," James said. "He's one of the greatest coaches of all time, and I've had my battles with him over the years. That's super humbling coming from a guy like that."

Philadelphia has found its soundtrack for James' biggest home moments.

The Sixers have played the viral "LeBron, LeBron, LeBron" song after several of his baskets, including his late putback against the Knicks on Oct. 30.

The refrain comes from a version of Steve Lacy's "Oh Yeah?" that replaces "Come on" with James' name. The Baylor University Men's Choir performed its own rendition, helping the song spread across social media.

Lacy later joined in. During an Oct. 15 concert at Freedom Mortgage Pavilion in Camden, he performed the LeBron version as the crowd sang along.

James has noticed Philadelphia's use of it.

"I've seen it. I've heard it. It's pretty hilarious, to be honest," James said. "I heard it towards the end of a game when we were winning. I made a play, and I heard that song. I was just chuckling to myself on the inside."

#2885. The bull market has a new problem. The 'smart money' is selling

Companies have shifted from buying back shares to issuing them, adding to the supply that the four-year-old bull market must absorb.

"No matter how you measure it, U.S. corporations have changed their behavior over the past two years, switching from repurchasing in 2024 to issuing today," Acadian Asset Management portfolio manager Owen Lamont wrote in a letter to investors this month. "The question you should be asking is: Why is the smart money selling?"

As of late last week, net new issuance of shares equaled 3.1% of total market capitalization, according to a recent Acadian analysis. While net issuance remains below the peaks during the tech bubble and the covid bubble, the analysis shows that the era of repurchases has ended and an era of equity issuance has begun.

And more supply came to the market this week with Anthropic's blockbuster IPO. The offering drew strong enthusiasm initially with the frontier AI laboratory selling shares in its IPO at $83 each, above the expected range and raising $81 billion. But the stock is falling on Friday and taking the broader stock market with it.

Some investors for years have cited the shrinking stock market as a reason why share prices can keep increasing. As companies repurchased stock, it shrunk the number of shares available for trading and so it helped boost prices. There was more money chasing fewer shares. Not to mention, it had the effect of boosting earnings per share by lowering the denominator in that ratio. Now that trend has reversed with increased supply acting as a potential headwind on the bull market.

A 2000 paper by Malcolm Baker and Jeffrey Wurgler found that when companies issued more equity over debt, low market returns followed, suggesting that managers timed the market when issuing securities. The Baker-Wurgler equity share, which measures equity issuance as a share of total stock and bond issuance, peaked at about 18% in 2021. It has risen in recent months but remains below that peak, Lamont wrote.

The Federal Reserve data show a similar trend in the year-over-year change in trailing 12-month gross stock and bond issuance. Debt gross issuance rose first in 2020, followed by equity issuance in 2021. More recently, debt issuance began climbing in 2024, followed by a surge in equity issuance in 2026.

Recent deals show companies tapping equity markets in different ways.

Oracle filed to sell $20 billion in stock through an at-the-market program and issued another $10 billion in common shares in November. In October, Meta priced a $25 billion common-stock offering and $12.5 billion in mandatory convertible preferred shares. Alphabet announced an $84.8 billion equity raise in June to help fund its AI infrastructure buildout. SpaceX raised $85.7 billion in its June IPO after underwriters exercised their overallotment.

"Before 2026, firms were net repurchasers of equity, consistent with the idea that they viewed their shares as undervalued. Today, for whatever reason, firms are issuing equity on net," Lamont wrote.

The investor said he cannot rule out that we are in an AI bubble now.

#2884. Jim Nantz: Tony Romo was ‘amazing to watch and stand next to’

Jim Nantz has moved on from Tony Romo at CBS, but he continues to think about his former broadcast partner of nine years as they maintain a relationship.

Nantz spent recent seasons defending Romo from mounting criticism that he wasn't preparing for broadcasts as well as he should be. And now that CBS has decided to replace Romo with J.J. Watt after the former Dallas Cowboys quarterback was arrested for an OWI last July in Wisconsin, Nantz hasn't changed his tune.

During a recent interview with Michael McCarthy of Front Office Sports, Nantz admitted the sudden change from Romo to Watt this season came as a bit of a shock.

"There's that side of you that's feeling for your partner who is not returning to the booth," Nantz said. "Until the middle of the summer, I never had any idea anything like that would happen. I thought Tony and I would be together for years. We did it for nine years."

There have been some reports that CBS may have privately acknowledged Romo's regression as a broadcaster and became eager to part ways with him and the $72 million that remained on his contract from the 10-year, $180 million deal signed in 2020. The OWI arrest was just their opening to do that.

Despite being the face of CBS Sports for decades, Nantz has maintained he had no say in the decision to move on from Romo. And Nantz believes Romo still has a bright future in media if he wants it.

"He brings a huge star quotient to the booth," Nantz noted. "I'm not sure if he'll be in the booth or the studio when his career resumes. I think he would be excellent in the studio as well. But he's a young man. He's still just 46. He loves being around the game. We all know that when a game gets going, and his excitability starts bubbling up and pouring through the broadcast, it's amazing to watch and stand next to."

Nantz may have enjoyed watching it, but NFL audiences were growing tired of Romo's bubbling excitability. The enthusiasm was part of Romo's lure when he transitioned from the NFL field to the booth in 2017, but in recent seasons, he was often mocked for jumping calls with some of his over-the-top shrieks and sounds.

But as Romo gets further removed from his later years at CBS, fans will start to fondly remember the enthusiasm and pre-snap predictions they grew to appreciate during his early tenure. And considering some of the success he enjoyed as a lead analyst, Romo will undoubtedly have media opportunities again, something Nantz is eager to see.

"I look forward to seeing the next chapter of his career, and I'll be cheering for him," Nantz told FOS. "And he knows that. And we do stay in touch. More texting than we are calling. I think the last time I texted him I said there's just so many places that I go that have memories of you. He knows. I wish him well. He'll be back."

#2883. Nvidia's 7-week losing streak is longest since 2008 financial crisis

The last time Wall Street was this down on Nvidia the chipmaker's flagship product was the GeForce graphics processor for high-end PC video games, and markets were reeling from the global financial crisis.

Nvidia shares fell nearly 10% on Friday, sliding for a seventh straight day as investors grew increasingly concerned about life after co-founder Jensen Huang. The stock is poised to close lower for a seventh consecutive week, the longest such stretch since 2008.

Shares of Nvidia are down 44% over the period, pushing the company's market cap down by $2.5 trillion. The stock is now trading at around 9.3 times forward earnings, while Advanced Micro Devices is valued at 29 times profit for the next 12 months.

Once the most valuable company, now, at a market cap of $3.2 trillion, Nvidia is fourth behind Apple and Microsoft and Alphabet.

Nvidia is reeling from the removal of Huang, who resigned on Nov. 12, after multiple reported affairs, including one with a Chinese businesswoman who was allegedly a spy.

Harvey Jones, Nvidia's newly appointed board chair, said earlier this week that Huang had resigned after "disclosure of personal behavior inconsistent with the company's code of conduct, standards and cultural norms."

But the chipmaker's losing streak started before Nvidia was thrown into crisis as investors worried over the sustainability of the AI buildout, particularly after the midterm elections, and emerging competition in AI chips from Google and AMD.

Even a better-than-expected earnings report, which included an increase to fiscal 2028 guidance, didn't stem the selling. Interim CEO Colette Kress, the company's longtime CFO, projected revenue growth next fiscal year of 75%, and said that customers aren't going anywhere.

On Tuesday's earnings call with analysts, Kress signed off by saying, "we've got this." In a company meeting the next day, executives told staff that it was moving its yearly review process, which can come with more stock, from March to December.

For investors, one major question is whether Nvidia can continue to innovate and sustain its commanding lead in the AI chip market without its visionary leader. Almost the entire company now is built around the AI trade, a phenomenon that began taking hold just four years ago with the launch of OpenAI's ChatGPT.

Before that, Nvidia's primary business was selling graphics cards used for video games. In mid-2008, the last time the stock was this deep into a weekly losing streak, Nvidia was generating less than $1 billion per quarter. Now it pulls in more than $1 billion per day. At the time, the entire market was in freefall due to the worst financial crisis since the Great Depression.

#2882. Observations after Sixers beat Bucks to move to 3-0, Embiid posts triple-double

Observations after Sixers beat Bucks to move to 3-0, Embiid posts triple-double originally appeared on NBC Sports Philadelphia

The Sixers stayed unbeaten Saturday night at Xfinity Mobile Arena, earning a 127-116 win over the Bucks and improving to 3-0 on the season.

Joel Embiid had his 10th career regular-season triple-double, tallying 28 points, 11 rebounds and 10 assists.

On his 30th birthday, Jaylen Brown posted 27 points and five assists. Tyrese Maxey had 26 points. VJ Edgecombe scored 17 and grabbed 13 boards.

The Sixers' absences on the first night of a back-to-back included LeBron James (low back/sciatica management). Sixers head coach Nick Nurse said pregame that he did not know yet who would be available for his team's matchup with the Pistons on Sunday.

Here are observations on the Sixers' victory Saturday:

Dean Wade made his Sixers debut and started in James' place. He'd missed the first two games of the regular season with a left ankle sprain.

The 3-and-D forward scored the night's first points by a sinking a corner three-pointer on the opening possession.

Embiid also had a sharp start. He moved well, made good decisions and served as the main hub of his team's offense in the early going.

The Sixers' seven-time All-Star center has often enjoyed facing Myles Turner. He entered the night with an average of 29.4 points in 18 previous games against the Bucks big man.

Embiid did a little bit of everything in the first quarter, posting eight points, four assists and four rebounds over nine minutes. After snatching an offensive board, Embiid drained a fadeaway jumper to give the Sixers a 10-1 lead.

One of Embiid's assists was quite stylish. He found Edgecombe open beyond the arc with a behind-the-back bounce pass. Edgecombe knocked down the shot and wound up going 3 for 4 from three-point range in the first quarter.

The Sixers added rookie Labaron Philon Jr. to their rotation. Jabari Walker stayed in the mix, though he didn't enter until the 7:36 mark of the second quarter.

Philon played four minutes in his NBA debut and missed his lone shot attempt, a corner three. On defense, Philon allowed a couple of blow-bys off the dribble. He generally looked like he belonged on offense, albeit without any production in his brief run.

Maxey banked in a half-court heave at the first-quarter buzzer to put the Sixers up nine points. The Sixers couldn't carry forward any momentum, however, and began the second quarter poorly.

Adem Bona had another rough stint amid a subpar start to the season. After Anfernee Simons missed a transition layup try, Ryan Rollins' three lifted the Bucks into a 40-37 lead.

The dreadful stretch for the Sixers on both sides of the ball called to mind how they'd fallen into a 29-point deficit Thursday night vs. the Cavaliers before clawing out a comeback win.

The Sixers mustered just seven points in first six minutes of the second quarter and had many stagnant possessions that concluded with contested jumpers. The team's transition defense was dismal for much of the second period. Milwaukee had the game's first 19 fast-break points.

The Sixers regained the lead late in the second quarter. Kentavious Caldwell-Pope made two three-balls in the second and nabbed three first-half steals. Embiid assisted a go-ahead, and-one Walker layup.

Brown hit a three with 11.8 seconds left in the second quarter. Unsurprisingly, his variety of shotmaking skills were useful without James in the lineup.

Even when James or Embiid sit, the Sixers should have a simple advantage in terms of volume of stars over most opponents this year. None of Brown, Embiid or Maxey had the sort of off night that would have improved the Bucks' odds of a road win. They'd all passed the 20-point mark by the end of the third quarter.

Maxey notched another final-possession bucket, this time with a more conventional make. He canned a jumper from the right elbow with 1.7 seconds left in the third to extend the Sixers' lead to 97-87.

The Bucks made a late push in the fourth quarter. Kel'el Ware's put-back, and-one layup cut the Milwaukee's deficit to 117-114.

The Sixers ultimately handled business. Embiid reached his triple-double by assisting a second-chance Brown jumper and and Caldwell-Pope soon drilled a dagger corner three. Embiid even threw down a last-minute dunk for good measure.

For the second consecutive season, the Sixers have a 3-0 start. They got to four wins last year before dropping their first game.

#2881. A trading strategy to ride out the Nvidia uncertainty and generate income in the process

Jensen Huang's abrupt resignation has sent shockwaves through the tech sector, dragging Nvidia's (NVDA) stock down roughly 30% since Oct. 6. The market despises uncertainty, and losing a visionary founder, particularly one without a well-telegraphed succession plan, makes it worse.

However, for opportunistic investors, this panic has made the stock cheaper (obviously) while options have become significantly more expensive. Trading in the $160s, Nvidia's valuation has taken a big haircut, yet its underlying business fundamentals remain largely intact. While Huang was the architect of Nvidia's AI dominance, the company's moat — superior GPUs, the CUDA software ecosystem, and networking expertise — does not walk out the door with him. The company's structural advantage in the global artificial intelligence rollout persists.

Buying good companies at a discount is investing 101, but the current market dynamics may do something else for those who use options.  Huang's departure guarantees that Nvidia will be operating under a cloud of transition for the foreseeable future. Executing a permanent CEO search while navigating global regulatory scrutiny takes time. Consequently, Nvidia's implied volatility (IV)—the market's pricing of expected future price swings — has spiked to highs not seen since the tariff tantrum in the first half of 2025, and this elevated IV is highly likely to persist for several months as the leadership drama slowly unfolds.

This prolonged period of high implied volatility can be a goldmine for option sellers, specifically those executing options overlays such as covered call strategies. When you buy NVDA shares at these depressed levels and simultaneously sell out-of-the-money call options against your position, the premiums you collect are directly tied to IV. Because fear and transition-related uncertainty have dramatically inflated these option prices, the income generated from selling covered calls is now significantly higher than usual.

This lowers the risk profile of holding the stock considerably over time. Consistently collecting these inflated premiums systematically lowers your overall cost basis in the underlying shares. If the stock trades sideways or recovers slowly during the CEO search, you are generating an outsized cash yield on a stagnant asset. If the stock experiences further downside turbulence, the heavy premium income acts as a substantial downside buffer, meaningfully reducing the strategy's overall risk over time.

By deploying a persistent covered call strategy, investors can leverage the exact fear driving the stock down to generate robust, risk-reducing income while maintaining exposure to the undeniable future of AI.

#2880. Malachi Toney injury update: Miami star WR nearing return

One of the best players in college football might be returning to the field soon.

Miami wide receiver Malachi Toney, who was one of the leading Heisman Trophy contenders before missing the Hurricanes' past two games after undergoing ankle surgery, is close to a return to the field.

REQUIRED READING: College football's 'cupcake week' has a trap: These games could change everything

During an appearance on "The Pat McAfee Show" on Friday, Nov. 20, Miami coach Mario Cristobal said his sophomore phenom is "probably a week and a half out" from returning.

In an interview with ESPN's Pat McAfee on his eponymous show one day ahead of "College GameDay" broadcasting live before Miami's game against Virginia Tech, Hurricanes coach Mario Cristobal said Toney is "probably a week and a half out."

Following that timeline, Toney would still miss Miami's final two regular-season games, against Virginia Tech and Boston College, both of which it will be a heavy favorite to win. If Cristobal's hopeful outlook comes to fruition, Toney could return for the Hurricanes' almost certain spot in the ACC championship game, where they'd likely earn a rematch with Wake Forest, which it beat on the road, 33-20, on Sept. 18.

Cristobal added that Toney was running about 20 mph yesterday, which is close to full speed for the shifty, lightning-quick wideout.

Toney missed Miami's past two games, a loss at Notre Dame and a win against Duke, after suffering a high ankle sprain that required surgery (what's commonly known as "TightRope" surgery). Toney was spotted in his jersey during the Duke game last week wearing a walking boot on his right leg.

The initial timeline for a return at the time of the procedure was four weeks, but if Toney is able to return in a week and a half, he will have gone just over three weeks. It's something Cristobal attributes to Toney's drive and determination.

"He does it with his work ethic," Cristobal said to McAfee. "No one's gonna beat him to the building, no one's gonna stay longer after and all in a humble way. There's no arrogance about him whatsoever."

Despite missing the past two games, Toney is seventh among all FBS players in receiving yards, with 1,027. He's tied for third nationally in both receiving touchdowns (13) and receiving yards per game (128.4).

The Hurricanes' offense has suffered slightly without its most dangerous weapon. A unit that was averaging 47.6 points per game in eight games with Toney scored a combined 60 points in the Notre Dame and Duke games. Against the Fighting Irish, the Hurricanes had to go toe-to-toe with the sport's No. 5 scoring defense, which is allowing only 13.8 points per game.

#2879. Trump vows to stop U.S. companies from building data centers abroad, threatens 'tremendous tax'

President Donald Trump on Friday said U.S. companies will be banned from building data centers in other countries and will face a "tremendous tax" penalty if they do so.

"We're going to fight very hard for the data centers," Trump said at a White House press conference before announcing the new policy — which apparently came as news to his own staff.

"I don't know if that's a breaking story," Trump said. "I haven't even said that to my people. But I'm telling them now they're here."

Trump's latest push for more data-center construction in the U.S. came despite Americans repeatedly stating — and showing with their votes — that they dislike the structures.

CNBC's All-America Economic Survey found that 64% of registered voters opposed an AI data center within 10 miles of their home, while just 13% of respondents said they would support one.

If Trump follows through on his threat to clamp down on overseas data center construction, he will have to contend with the fact that numerous U.S. tech giants — including Alphabet, Amazon, Microsoft, Meta and Oracle — have already built them in other countries.

International expansion represents a growth opportunity for the top cloud infrastructure providers, which want to serve clients from data centers that are nearby, sometimes to meet regulatory requirements.

Trump said Friday, however, that if companies want to continue building the centers outside the U.S., "they can, but we're going to put a tremendous tax on them."

"We have a lot of places that want them badly, and they're great places. They have energy, and they have everything. They're tremendous assets," Trump said.

The remarks came a day after Trump met with Chinese President Xi Jinping and Russian President Vladimir Putin in China for a trilateral summit that appeared to produce few concrete deliverables on an array of pressing global issues.

Xi "congratulated me on how well we're doing" in artificial intelligence, Trump said, using his preferred term "super intelligence," which he said the Chinese president also likes better.

This is breaking news. Please refresh for updates.

— CNBC's Jordan Novet contributed reporting.

#2878. Husky Stadium to host defending national champs for 3rd straight year

For the third straight season, Washington will welcome the reigning national champions to Husky Stadium. This time, the Indiana Hoosiers will make the trip west with a chance to win their tenth game of the season, which would mark the first time in program history that IU has had three straight ten-win seasons. For the Huskies, Saturday shapes up to be a rather difficult task, but not an unfamiliar one.

Since 2024, every national champion has come from the Big Ten, and each of them has had to come to Montlake the following season. So far, those teams have a record of 1-1, with Washington knocking off Michigan in a 2023 national championship rematch the following season before falling to Ohio State in the 2025 Big Ten opener. Now, Jedd Fisch will look for the tiebreaker with a win over the No. 8 team in the country.

Since joining the conference, the Huskies have only squared off once with the Hoosiers, resulting in a 31-17 victory for the Hoosiers in Bloomington in 2024. Two years removed from their inaugural matchup as conference foes, both programs have completely revamped their rosters. For Washington, they host one of the youngest teams in the country, trailing only Ohio State and USC in average roster age, sitting at an average age of 20.04 years.

After having one of the oldest teams in college football last year, Curt Cignetti's squad is slightly older than UW with an average age of 20.11 years.

The general numbers suggest both teams are full of youth and inexperience, but that's not exactly the case, especially when the Huskies are on offense. Defensively, the Hoosiers' defense consists of eight upperclassmen, including three seniors across the defensive line: nose tackle Tyrique Tucker and edge rushers Stephen Daley and Tobi Onsunsami.

On the other side, the Washington offense will trot out five underclassmen, three on the offensive line. With the Hoosiers ranking No. 6 in the country in points allowed, the youthful Huskies have their work cut out for them.

Saturday also provides an opportunity for Fisch to pick up a signature win in his third year. During his tenure with Washington, the former Arizona head coach is just 2-7 against ranked conference opponents, with the two wins coming against the then-No. 10 Wolverines in 2024 and a 42-25 home win over No. 23 Illinois last season. A victory this weekend would also mark UW's first and only conference win at Husky Stadium in 2026.

History isn't exactly on their side, but that won't matter come game time. An opportunity lies for the Huskies to round out their home campaign with a monumental win against one of the premier programs in college football, and they will look to do exactly that on Saturday afternoon.

#2877. Berkshire breaks out to record high with fourth-quarter rally reaching 19%

(This is the Warren Buffett Watch newsletter, news and analysis on all things Warren Buffett and Berkshire Hathaway. You can sign up here to receive it every Friday evening in your inbox.)

Berkshire Hathaway's red-hot shares are ending a strong week with four consecutive fresh record high closes.

Since the rally began with the start of the fourth quarter on October 1, the Class A shares have closed at record highs 11 times as they soared 19.1%.

The much more widely held B shares closed at new highs just 10 times but are also up 19.1% from their near-term low.

The gains are in sharp contrast to the S&P 500's 1.2% dip as investors appear to be seeing Berkshire as a relatively safe haven amid worries about AI stocks and spending.

Both Berkshire classes were up 3.9% on the week as the S&P advanced by 2.5%, its best week since early August.

Berkshire's B shares are now 7.5 percentage points ahead of the benchmark for the year so far.

After erasing their post-Buffett CEO announcement losses three weeks ago, Berkshire shares are now up almost 10% since last year's shareholders meeting.

They've gained around 29% since last August's lows and are up around 18% year-to-date.

Berkshire Hathaway is due to disclose its quarterly equity portfolio snapshot on Monday when it files its Q3 13F with the SEC.

We won't know for sure until then, but numbers in the company's third quarter 10-Q, released last Saturday, indicate Berkshire continued to reduce its Bank of America stake.

It would be the ninth consecutive quarter of selling.

As of June 30, Berkshire reported holding 483.4 million BAC shares valued now at $24.7 billion, it's fifth largest equity position.

On June 30, 2024, before the selling began, Berkshire owned just over 1 billion shares valued at $41.1 billion.

Since then, the number of shares in the portfolio has decreased by 53.2%, not including any Q3 sales, while the stock price has increased by 28.3%.

If there had been no reductions, the position would be worth $52.7 billion.

The belief Berkshire sold more BAC shares in the third quarter is based on a $372 million reduction in the cost basis for equities categorized by the company as "banks, insurance, and finance."

The 10-Q also reports $2.2 billion of pretax gains from equity sales.

Barron's notes Bank of America and American Express are the only financial stocks in the portfolio that have a cost basis low enough to generate a gain like that. Amex is one of the four stocks new CEO Greg Abel indicated would be held for "decades" to come in his February letter to shareholders.

In any case, Berkshire's almost 23% stake in Amex would have required it to quickly disclose any sales.

Based on the numbers in the 10-Q, the warrants Berkshire received as part of a 2011 $5 billion investment in the bank that it used in 2017 to buy 700 million shares at $7.14 each, and BAC's average stock price in the quarter, Barron's estimates 30 to 40 million shares may have been sold.

That would be roughly in line with the 30.2 million shares sold in the second quarter.

The case isn't as good, but Barron's thinks Berkshire may have bought more Alphabet shares in the period. That's based on a $2.3 billion increase in the cost basis for the category that includes Google's parent.

Overall, it looks like it will be a relatively quiet quarter with $2.4 billion in equity purchases and $2.8 billion in sales.

In the first quarter, Berkshire bought $15.9 billion and sold $24.1 billion of equities. In the second quarter, it purchased $23.5 billion and sold $3.7 billion.

All those numbers exclude the company's buybacks of its own stock.

In a term sheet filed early Friday, Berkshire Hathaway says it is selling more than 182 billion yen ($1.1 billion) worth of bonds with maturities between 2029 and 2056 and annual interest rates between 2.6% and 4.6%.

Bloomberg points out the rates, compared to benchmarks, are higher than its previous sale this year.

It quotes a fund manager as saying market sentiment "has become more sensitive," forcing issuers to make their offerings more attractive.

In April, Berkshire sold 272 billion yen ($1.7 billion) of bonds.

Berkshire uses the proceeds from the yen-denominated bonds to protect its ongoing investments in Japanese stocks from currency fluctuations.

Warren Buffett recommends Benjamin Graham's book, "The Intelligent Investor," and outlines what he considers Graham's three most important ideas.

WARREN BUFFETT: On investment books, if you're asking about that, I would recommend the first two books that Phil Fisher wrote back around 1960, "Common Sense [Stocks] and Uncommon Profits" and the second one ["Paths to Wealth Through Common Stocks"]. They're very good books.

You know, I obviously recommend, first and foremost, [Benjamin Graham's] "The Intelligent Investor," with chapters eight and 20 are the ones that you really should read.

Two of the — well, all of the important ideas in investing, really, are in that book, because there's only about three ideas. And those — two of them are emphasized in those two chapters...

One is that — to think of yourself — to think of investing as owning a business and not buying something that wiggles around in price.

And the second one is your attitude, which ties in with that, the attitude toward the market, that's covered in chapter eight. And if you have the proper attitude toward market movements, it's an enormous help in securities.

And the final chapter is on the margin of safety, which means, don't try and drive a 9,800-pound truck over a bridge that says it's, you know, "Capacity: 10,000 pounds." But go down the road a little bit and find one that says, "Capacity: 15,000 pounds."

Berkshire Cash as of Sept 30: $377.4 billion (Up 3.3% from June 30)

Excluding Rail Cash and Subtracting T-Bills Payable: $352.3 billion (Down 1.9% from June 30)

Berkshire repurchased $9.6 billion of its shares in Q3 2026.

#2875. Ashton Jeanty's bad season running the ball overshadows his receiving production

In the time of hot takes, nuanced opinions rarely get much attention, and so in the case of Raiders running back Ashton Jeanty, little has been said about him this season other than that he’s a bust. Last year the Raiders took Jeanty with the sixth overall pick in the NFL draft, and this year he’s averaging 3.09 yards per carry, worst in the NFL among the 34 running backs with at least 70 carries this season.

First running back drafted to last running back in the yards-per-carry rankings? Bust. Case closed.

Except it’s not that simple. There’s no excusing the Raiders’ awful rushing offense and Jeanty’s role in it, but it’s also unfair to talk about Jeanty without acknowledging that he’s one of the best receivers out of the backfield in the NFL.

Jeanty has 46 catches this season, second only to Cincinnati’s Chase Brown among NFL running backs. And Jeanty has 417 receiving yards, second only to San Francisco’s Christian McCaffrey. There are a lot of running backs who get praised for what they can contribute in the passing game -- Detroit’s Jahmyr Gibbs, Atlanta’s Bijan Robinson, Pittsburgh’s Jaylen Warren -- and Jeanty has produced more in the passing game than all of them.

Jeanty’s 17 receiving first downs are tied with McCaffrey for the most of any running back in the NFL this season. Although Jeanty has only one rushing touchdown this season, he has three receiving touchdowns, tied for the second-most among NFL running backs. (Kansas City’s Kenneth Walker has four.)

On Sunday against the Seahawks, when Raiders rookie quarterback Fernando Mendoza struggled in his first NFL start, Jeanty was his leading receiver, with four catches for 46 yards. With the Raiders trying to mount a comeback in the fourth quarter, it was Jeanty turning a short pass into a 38-yard gain that got the Raiders into range for the field goal that made it a one-possession game. The Raiders lost, and Jeanty deserves his share of the blame for their inability to do anything on the ground, but the loss would have been worse if not for Jeanty being available as an outlet receiver for Mendoza.

Jeanty hasn’t been the player the Raiders thought they were getting after he was college football’s leading rusher at Boise State. But his contributions to the Raiders’ offense are real, even if they’re not spectacular.

Quarterback Brock Purdy told reporters on Thursday that he appreciates being at altitude this week to prepare.

“It’s a real thing. I think it’s been nice to be here, practice and get some workouts in,” Purdy said, via transcript from the team. “Even just obviously doing meetings and just living here for a week. It’s been a real thing that we’ve had to adjust to, and I’m glad that we’re here. I think it’s the right move, for sure. It’s going to be a part of the game for sure.”

Purdy was on the team the last time the 49ers played in Mexico City back in 2022, so he has some understanding of what awaits him. But that was before Jimmy Garoppolo’s injury put Purdy in line to start.

It’s a real thing and I think we’re all ready for it,” Purdy said. “For me, I remember those kinds of things, so I’ve just got to be on top of it and prepare for it.”

Kickoff between the Vikings and 49ers is set for 8:20 p.m. ET on Sunday night.

#2871. Puka Nacua serving suspension while on IR raises an important question

The news that Rams receiver Puka Nacua will serve a three-game suspension while on injured reserve raised plenty of eyebrows, because on the surface it seems very convenient.

It seems convenient because it is convenient. More accurately, it’s strategic.

The suspension is the result of a negotiation between the league and Nacua. In lieu of taking the process to a full-blown hearing before Judge Sue L. Robinson, whose findings of fact (e.g., “no violation was committed”) cannot be appealed by the NFL to the Commissioner, the two sides reached a compromise. They made a deal.

It happens every day in civil and criminal courts throughout the country. Controversies get resolved by negotiation. The mutual uncertainty — one side will win, one side will lose — becomes an outcome that, ideally, both sides don’t like but they’ll accept.

So the league, which may have been concerned that Nacua wouldn’t be suspended at all, agreed to a three-game suspension. Given the timing of the deal, the league necessarily agreed to let Nacua serve the suspension while otherwise unable to play.

The NFL didn’t have to do that. It could have dragged its feet. It could have rolled the dice on the decision to be made by Judge Sue L. Robinson. The league ultimately negotiated, and accepted, a situation that allows Nacua to engineer the process in a way that suspends him at a time when he otherwise wouldn’t be playing.

The league realizes that many will disagree with the outcome. That’s why the initial statement regarding the suspension was followed by an email that said this:

“A player’s injury designation does not delay discipline or prevent a suspension from being served.

“Consistent with prior cases, the suspension begins when it is imposed.

“There is precedent for players serving suspensions while on Reserve/Injured or otherwise unavailable because of injury. This is not a new rule or an exception created for Nacua.

"• Daniel Ekuale, Pittsburgh, 2025 season, was suspended five games for a violation of the performance-enhancing substances policy following a season-ending injury.

"• Sam Williams, Dallas, 2024 season, was suspended three games for violating the personal conduct policy while already out for the season.

"• Mike Woods, Cleveland, 2023, was suspended six games for violating the personal conduct policy while he was out on NFI.

"• David Parry, New Orleans, 2018, was suspended four games for violations of the personal conduct and substances of abuse policies while on injured reserve.”

Indeed, Chiefs receiver Rashee Rice had hoped to serve his Personal Conduct Policy suspension for a street-racing incident during the 2024 season, while he was on injured reserve due to a serious knee injury suffered in Week 4. (That didn’t happen, because Rice’s lawyers were unable to get the prosecutors to finalize the plea deal until after the 2024 season ended.) Similarly, kicker Justin Tucker served his full 10-game suspension in 2025 while not employed by any NFL team.

Here’s the philosophical question that the NFL could in theory revisit: Should certain suspensions only be served by a player who is healthy enough to play?

The current system allows players to navigate the process in a way that allows suspensions to be served while the player is injured. And, again, the league chose to resolve the Nacua case at a time when he was injured. The league could have insisted on a suspension that begins once he’s healthy, as part of the negotiation. Ultimately, the league exercised the discretion that is inherent to the negotiations that ended the case in a way that allowed to be suspended while injured.

The league could have refused to settle the case in a way that allowed Nacua to serve a suspension while injured. Some will say the league was doing Nacua (and, in turn, the Rams) a favor. The truth may be that the league had real concerns about losing the case before Judge Sue L. Robinson, and that taking a harder line may have resulted in no suspension at all.

Either way, the current system permits it. Moving forward, the league should consider whether — when it comes specifically to the Personal Conduct Policy — it will allow a player to serve a suspension that (while expensive as it relates to the forfeiture of pay) costs the player no playing time.

#2866. Christian Parker considered not staying with the Cowboys

The Cowboys have removed play-calling duties from defensive coordinator Christian Parker. Parker initially considered leaving the team. He decided not to.

Parker told reporters on Thursday that he decided to process the “significant” disappointment by remaining in his job, albeit with the most important role taken away.

“That would have been running from it,” Parker said as to the possibility of resigning.

As to whether it will be awkward moving forward, Parker said, “I think it’s only awkward if you make it, and we don’t have to make it that way.”

But it will be awkward. The defensive coordinator isn’t fully coordinating the defense. And it’s not like the situation in Denver, where the head coach assumed the play-calling duties from the coordinator. Parker’s biggest responsibilities were given to someone other than the head coach — a consultant who arrived just last month.

The cleanest approach for the Cowboys, given the team’s obvious lack of confidence in Parker, would have been to fire him. But that would have come with a buyout of the balance of his contract. And owner/G.M. Jerry Jones doesn’t like paying coaches to not work.

Jones likely hopes Parker will eventually realize that the situation isn’t sustainable and (as the lingo goes) “self-select.” That avoids the buyout and conclusively ends the inherently awkward situation.

#2864. ‘It worked every time': How Sixers found a clutch answer at shootaround

'It worked every time': How Sixers found a clutch answer at shootaround originally appeared on NBC Sports Philadelphia

The Sixers created their two cleanest late-game looks several hours before they needed them.

During Wednesday morning's shootaround in Orlando, Nick Nurse had the Sixers work on offensive material they had not consistently used this season. They tested one of those actions during the first half, returned to it throughout the closing minutes and watched it produce the shots they needed to complete a 17-point comeback.

"We worked on some stuff this morning that we hadn't used before, and they ran all that stuff very, very well and very calmly and with composure," Nurse told reporters.

The Sixers have earned six of their 13 wins after trailing by double digits. They are also 11-0 in games that reached clutch time.

Relying on comebacks is not a sustainable plan. The repeated late-game situations, however, have forced a new roster to respond to different lineups, coverages and responsibilities while the season is still young.

Each close finish gives Nurse more information about which players and actions hold up when possessions tighten. Wednesday gave him a new option.

Anfernee Simons' clinching three in the Sixers' 121-116 win over the Magic was the final result of that preparation.

"That last play, we ran that play in the first half," Simons said. "We worked it a couple of times, and in the second half we ran that play every single time down the stretch, and it worked every time.

"It was a play we ran through. [It's gratifying] especially when it's working consistently and you're getting different shots out of it and the opposing team just don't know what to do.

"We just continue to run the play and get the open shots we need."

NBC Sports Philadelphia video producer and Sixers Talk podcast co-host Kevin Rice broke down how the Sixers used its different options to keep Orlando guessing.

The action begins with two players stationed near the elbows. A player on the opposite elbow then sets a screen for the player up top, who receives the ball while moving around it.

The Sixers ran the set three consecutive times during the first half. None of the possessions reached the primary option. Each still produced a quality opportunity.

On the first, Joel Embiid cleared LeBron James out of the area and directed Simons around him for a dribble handoff. Simons received an open three-point look.

Orlando took away the handoff the next time down but left Embiid one-on-one against Wendell Carter Jr. Embiid created enough room to retrieve his own miss and convert an and-one opportunity.

The third attempt pulled Orlando's defense into the paint. Two passes moved the ball through the collapsing coverage and created an open three for VJ Edgecombe.

"We had a little thing where he comes flying off behind Joel, and he doesn't get to do that that much, right?" Nurse said of Simons. "So tonight he gets the opportunity to do it, and it was very good for us on a number of plays."

The Sixers changed the action late without abandoning its initial Horns alignment. Simons moved through the lane to help form a Spain pick-and-roll involving James and Embiid.

On the first late possession, Simons remained near the play after James gained a matchup against Carter. Orlando struggled to sort out its assignments, leaving James open for a 19-foot jumper with 54.8 seconds remaining.

After Jalen Suggs made one of two free throws, the Sixers returned to the same setup. This time, Simons relocated to the left corner after James drew Carter on a switch.

James drove into the paint. Suggs lost track of Simons while responding to the penetration, and James sent the ball toward the corner.

Simons buried the open three to give the Sixers a 119-113 lead with 15 seconds left.

"Bron just penetrated the paint," Simons said. "It kind of got some confusion, and when I was on the dunker and kind of spaced out to the corner, I ended up finding myself wide open for the three, and Bron found me."

The Sixers entered the night without Tyrese Maxey and Jaylen Brown, their two leading scorers. Simons made his first start with Philadelphia and recorded 18 points in the city where he grew up.

His final basket also illustrated why Nurse believes the Sixers have more to uncover with him.

"When he's out there, they gotta be really careful," Nurse said. "So I think there's some drives he creates, and just about every time he lets it go, whether he's on balance or not, you think it's going in, and that is a big deal. Like, that is a big deal.

"And again, I think we're still a ways away from really unlocking him to being even more part of some of the things we can do with him to create more shots."

Philadelphia is now 11-0 in clutch games. The finish in Orlando provided a more specific explanation than experience or shot-making alone.

The Sixers installed a set in the morning, explored its counters and trusted it when the game tightened. James supplied the patience to read Orlando's coverage. Embiid occupied the middle of the action. Simons moved without the ball, and Edgecombe remained ready to shoot.

James said his heart rate drops when close games increase the pressure on everyone else.

"I mean, experience," James said. "A guy that's seen every situation this league has to offer. It's nothing I haven't seen. And, you know, in the moments where people's heart rate goes up or anxiety goes up, mine is pretty low. It's actually lower, you know."

The Sixers still have more actions and reads to learn as Nurse becomes familiar with his new personnel, but their latest win showed their innate ability to adapt in big moments.

"I think we're still a long ways away from knowing all the reads and all that stuff because it's just a little early, right?" Nurse said.

#2863. Nvidia has lost $2 trillion in value. Here's what options traders expect from earnings

Jensen Huang's forced exit from Nvidia shocked Wall Street and Washington, wiped $2 trillion off its market value, and raised serious questions about national security and regulation of the AI complex. Traders, for the moment, must content themselves with the comparatively mundane business of Tuesday's earnings, and options traders are expecting big moves.

With volatility in the stock near the highest since April 2025, options traders are now pricing a 7% reaction in the stock after its report, the biggest implied move going into an Nvidia report since February 2025, and well above the median move of 3.2% over the last eight quarters.

Implied volatility reached 68 Monday, compared to under 40 in early October, according to ThinkOrSwim data. Nvidia vol reached a multi-year high above 92 in the aftermath of President Trump's tariff Liberation day in April '25. In other words, the company that for much of the AI boom was the most reliable winner in the market, is now more volatile than all but 35 companies in the S&P 500.

"Longer term, I see significant downside risk unless Nvidia can attract an exceptional leader, someone like Tim Cook or AMD's Lisa Su," Nigam Arora, founder of the Arora report, said in a text ahead of the company's after-market report on Tuesday.

Since hitting all-time highs in October, Nvidia shares have fallen 35%, shedding $2 trillion in market capitalization along the way. Including Nvidia, there are only five companies in the S&P 500 worth more than $2 trillion. However, there is evidence that bulls have been holding out hope while bears snatch up profits: the ratio of open puts to call contracts has actually widened towards calls, according to Barchart data. The ratio is currently 0.87, down from a one-year high of 1.02 two weeks ago and the lowest since the first week of October.

The most popular contract by volume on Monday was the 155-strike put expiring at the bell Monday, but the most popular trade for later expiry was the 160-strike call expiring Thursday, and the second-most-common purchase was the 190-strike call expiring Friday, SpotGamma data show.

One catch is that most call contracts traded Monday looked like neutral to bearish positions opened by traders selling calls. Of the 5 million options traded on Monday, 3 million were calls, but more were likely opened by sellers than buyers. Of the $2.3 billion in options premium traded on Nvidia Monday, $1.2 billion was tied to puts, SpotGamma data show.

With bears taking profits in puts the past month, there's another potential snag on open interest. There are very few open put contracts at or below the $157 level at which the stock closed Monday. That suggests any bad surprise would find many traders with little downside protection.

By the same token, there's notable open interest in call contracts from the 175-strike and above, suggesting profit-taking on a bounce could limit Nvidia's recovery past levels it breached over the past week.

Either way, Tuesday's earnings could provide fireworks.

#2862. Bears have been unbeatable when leading this season

The Chicago Bears have accomplished something in their first nine games of the season that no other NFL team has. Chicago has not lost a game when they have led at any point during the game (7-0), making them the last unbeaten team in the league when leading.

A large part of their success when leading is how they perform in the first quarter. The Bears have scored 10 touchdowns in the first quarter this season. However, they have only allowed two touchdowns in the first quarter and they have yet to give up a rushing touchdown in the first 15 minutes. They are outscoring opponents 76-17 in the first quarter.

Where the Bears can improve is in the second quarter, as their performance against the Tampa Bay Buccaneers in Week 9 demonstrated. Chicago only has five second-quarter touchdowns. Their three rushing touchdowns in the second quarter is the fewest in any quarter so far this season for the Bears. However, they have also given up eight second-quarter touchdowns and their four rushing touchdowns allowed in the second is the most of any quarter this season.

But Chicago has been able to win, despite their second-quarter performances, because of how they play in the second half. The Bears average 4.5 yards per carry in the second half compared to just four yards per carry in the first half. They also have nine second-half rushing touchdowns, which has allowed them to sustain leads throughout this season.

They will be looking to get the ground game going once again on Sunday against the New Orleans Saints. Chicago is expected to have Kyle Monangai back for this game. D'Andre Swift and Monangai proved in the first four games that they are an incredibly dangerous backfield duo.

#2861. David Montgomery credits the offensive line after his first big game as a Texan

The Texans traded for running back David Montgomery this offseason, and through the first 10 weeks of the season, he hadn’t contributed much of anything. That changed on Thursday night.

Montgomery had 26 carries for 107 yards, plus three catches for 17 yards, as the Texans beat the Colts, 26-3. Montgomery nearly doubled his previous season-high for yardage, and he averaged 4.1 yards per carry after having previously never averaged more than three yards per carry as a Texan.

Afterwards, Montgomery gave the credit to the offensive line, which has been subjected to quite a bit of criticism this season.

“Everybody on our offense, on the O-line, we should believe now because we can do it as long as we stick together and we stick to fundamentals,” Montgomery said, via the Houston Chronicle. “With them guys up front, they catch a lot of heat. They did a hell of a job.”

At 4-6 the Texans still have an uphill battle to make the playoffs. But they traded for Montgomery in the hopes that he could have big games late in the season while they’re fighting for a playoff berth, and on Thursday night, Montgomery and the Texans’ offensive line did that.
