#2860. How LeBron James dissected Pacers defense down the stretch

How LeBron James dissected Pacers defense down the stretch originally appeared on NBC Sports Philadelphia

As he overtook Jason Kidd for the third-most assists in NBA history, LeBron James piloted the Sixers' offense down the stretch against the Indiana Pacers on Sunday.

With Joel Embiid sitting out and Tyrese Maxey getting a breather on the bench, James took over in the fourth quarter. He did so by spamming the same action over and over again.

The set consisted of James getting a ball screen from a guard – either VJ Edgecombe or Anfernee Simons – at the top of the key. After setting or ghosting the screen, the guard would short roll towards the basket.

When the Pacers blitzed or hedged the screen, James found the guard. The Sixers could then attack a 4-on-3 or 3-on-2 situation.

Twice, Edgecombe and Simons scored off the roll.

When the Pacers helped to the roller, that opened up the shooters in each corner.

James directed traffic on offense to ensure that the Sixers spaced the floor properly. Here, he waved Dean Wade out of the dunker spot and into the corner. Edgecombe promptly hit him for an open three off the short roll.

After all of the Sixers' success, the Pacers eventually switched the ball screen. That left the 6-foot-4, 173-pound Quenton Jackson on James, who has five inches and 77 pounds on him.

Jackson and James had already gone head to head on Sunday.

In the first quarter, James set a ball screen for Edgecombe which the Pacers switched. Edgecombe fed James in the post and the Pacers doubled him to support Jackson. James punished them for it.

They didn't double James after the switch in the fourth quarter. This time, James backed Jackson down, backed him down again and backed him down some more before scoring inside.

James' strength abusing matchups against smaller defenders after switches has been a safety valve for the Sixers' offense. Guards struggle to stop him from getting to the basket. When defenses send help, he picks them apart with his otherworldly passing ability.

That combination forced the Pacers to pick their poison. From the 9:55 mark in the fourth until the Pacers switched to a zone defense (after James bullied Jackson) with just over 6:00 left, the Sixers scored 13 points out of this action across seven possessions. That's a stellar 1.86 points per possession.

James, who's scoring a career-low 15.1 points per game so far this season, might not be the elite bucket-getter he was for most of his career. But he's still brilliant at conducting highly efficient offense that pressures a defense's pain points.

#2858. The Protect College Sports Act proponents are getting desperate

As the Protect College Sports Act languishes in the lame-duck session of the U.S. House of Representatives, the folks who hope to cure the “chaos” in college sports with an antitrust exemption that will give the NCAA and its members a license to violate the rights of players are getting desperate.

The best evidence of this comes from the fact that New York Yankees president Randy Levine, who serves as the vice-chair of President Trump’s Saving College Sports Roundtable, has called out Stephen A. Smith and me for our public opposition to the PCSA.

We’re being challenged by Levine because we’ve been vocal about calling the PCSA what it is — a bill that addresses the so-called “chaos” in college sports by reducing the rights that the players have won in recent years by successfully attacking a system that was littered with antitrust violations. The powers-that-be want Congress to wave a magic wand that will allow antitrust violations to occur without consequence. (I won’t speak for Stephen A. as to why he opposes the PCSA; I respect the fact that he’s willing to take a stand that clearly conflicts with the position of his employer.)

Levine made his case against Stephen A. (and Michael J.) in an interview with Charles Gasparino of the New York Post.

“One thing that has really annoyed me is the bogus argument put out by agents and their pundits such as Stephen A. Smith of ESPN and Mike Florio of NBC,” Levine said, adding that we “continually misrepresent that student athletes have had and will have no voice, and that is untrue.”

The problem isn’t they’ll have “no voice” later. It’s that they have “no voice” now. The process is being run not by the players but by the NCAA and the schools. The players aren’t claiming that there’s “chaos"; they benefit from the collapse of decades of antitrust violations flowing from the simple fact that the various schools huddled under the umbrella of the NCAA, which issued strict rules limiting what players can receive in exchange for playing college football.

Also, the characterization of me as one of “their pundits” means that Levine believes my opinions aren’t the product of my own authentic beliefs but that I’m somehow in the pockets of the agents representing college football players. While that is just plain false, the accusation isn’t surprising. We’ve caught wind of at least one media member who was offered a significant payment for a social-media post supporting the Protect College Sports Act. (I don’t know who specifically made the offer, but I know it was made.)

Levine called me in September, on the same day that Senator Ted Cruz (R-Texas) showed up on ESPN’s College GameDay to promote the PCSA. Levine began by basically calling me stupid for not realizing that collective bargaining is an impossibility for college sports.

Meanwhile, his November 10, 2026, letter to the entire U.S. House of Representatives explains on page 2 that “there will be nothing to prevent collective bargaining” if the PCSA passes. So if it’s stupid for me to think collective bargaining is possible, why would he feel compelled to sell the House on the idea that the PCSA would allow for collective bargaining?

Near the bottom of the article, Levine says this: “I’ve reached out to both of them (Smith and Florio) to debate me on this issue and heard crickets.”

I didn’t recall a specific invitation to debate Levine on the issue. I re-read the text message he sent on September 12, after I posted the article regarding our conversation. (It was actually very friendly.) “Always willing to debate and as I said to you in the beginning of the call I just wanted you to have the facts,” he wrote. “Was not trying to change your opinion.”

I didn’t interpret that as an invitation to have a public debate. I thought he meant he’s willing to continue to discuss the situation privately. I’m willing to do either, or both.

One last point. Gasparino’s article concludes by saying, “Reps for Smith and Florio had no immediate comment.”

Here’s my immediate comment: I have reps?

I’m easy to find, and I haven’t received any request from Gasparino about my position on the PCSA. Which I hope will not exit the House — and which I hope will disappear.

You broke it, you fix it. That should be the message to college sports. Congress has far better things to do, starting with figuring out how to avert an AI-triggered apocalypse.

#2857. AMD dives on short seller report of alleged China exposure

Shares of chipmaker Advanced Micro Devices close down 7.72% to $489.19 on Thursday after investment firm Hunterbrook Media issued a report alleging that the company "has its own China problem."

Hunterbrook disclosed that it had taken a short position in AMD.

The report claims that "after the U.S. blacklisted Chinese server maker IEIT Systems, the company kept getting newer AMD firmware, which enables AMD chips to run in servers."

CNBC did not independently verify this information.

Hunterbrook also said that new export rules in Washington could "tighten restrictions on AMD's foreign business," alleging that AMD "bills over 20% of its revenue to customers in China and Hong Kong," and "depends on a Chinese partner to finish most of its chips."

The allegations come amid investigations of rival chipmaker Nvidia over its sales to companies in Asia with ties to China. AMD has been viewed as a beneficiary of the uncertainty surrounding Nvidia.

"The claims misrepresent AMD's business practices and relationships," an AMD spokesperson said in a statement. "AMD complies with applicable US export control laws and regulations and maintains rigorous processes to ensure compliance across our global operations. We remain focused on serving our customers and upholding the highest standards of integrity."

Lisa Su, CEO of AMD, told investors on the company's recent earnings call that China is an "important" market and that the semiconductor giant wants to be fully compliant with U.S. export controls.

AMD continued to slide after hours, trading down 0.45% to $487 shortly after 4 p.m.

#2856. 'That chapter is behind me' - Ismaila Sarr puts Liverpool transfer saga behind him as Crystal Palace star eyes fresh start under Thomas Frank

Crystal Palace talisman Ismaila Sarr has addressed the summer transfer saga that nearly saw him swap Selhurst Park for Anfield. The Senegal international remains a vital part of the Eagles' project and is now focused on rediscovering his best form under the guidance of newly appointed manager Thomas Frank.

Reflecting on Liverpool's interest towards the end of the summer transfer window, Sarr admitted that the prospect of joining such a prestigious institution was significant. Palace ultimately turned down the Reds' approach for the Senegal international, who enjoyed an outstanding campaign last season, scoring 21 goals in 45 appearances across all competitions as the Eagles lifted the Conference League trophy.

Liverpool eventually decided to retain Cody Gakpo despite interest from Manchester City. In an exclusive interview with BBC Sport, the 28-year-old, who has been nominated for the African Footballer of the Year award, explained his mindset during that period. 'Liverpool is one of the biggest clubs in world football and, when a club like that shows an interest in you, of course it's something you take seriously,' Sarr stated.

'I don't think any player would be indifferent to that kind of opportunity. It was an interesting possibility, and naturally, I considered what it could mean for my career,' Sarr added. However, the winger was quick to emphasise that his commitment to the Eagles remains absolute. 'Today, that chapter is behind me. I've always believed that you can't move forward if you're constantly looking in the rear-view mirror. I'm a Crystal Palace player, I'm proud of what we've achieved together, and my focus is on getting back on the pitch and helping the team.'

The timing of Sarr's return to fitness coincides with a major shift in the dugout at Selhurst Park. Crystal Palace have endured a difficult start to the campaign, sitting bottom of the Premier League table with just seven points from their opening 10 matches. Following a disappointing spell under Pierre Sage, the club moved quickly to appoint former Brentford and Tottenham boss Frank. Sarr, who was limited to just two substitute appearances during Sage's brief tenure, views the managerial change as a vital turning point for a squad that has struggled to replicate the heights of their previous European success.

Sarr is optimistic that Frank's tactical approach will provide the spark needed to climb the Premier League table. 'Of course, it's a difficult moment for everyone at the club,' Sarr admitted when discussing the team's current form. 'After everything we achieved last season, nobody expected us to be in this position. But I know the quality of this squad. We have very good players, a strong group and the ability to turn things around. The arrival of Thomas Frank is an opportunity for a fresh start, new energy and a different approach.'

While Sarr is currently working his way back from a groin injury, his long-term future at the club is already being discussed. Despite having a contract that runs until 2029, Palace officials are reportedly in talks to offer the Senegalese star improved terms to reflect his status as one of the league's elite attackers. Sarr confirmed that negotiations are taking place but preferred to keep the specifics away from the public eye for the time being.

'Yes, there have been discussions with the club. For now, I prefer to let those conversations happen privately and concentrate on football,' he said. The forward is eager to repay the faith shown in him by the supporters and the board, especially after a season in which he netted 21 goals across all competitions. 'We all have to take responsibility, work together and fight for every point. I'm confident we can overcome this difficult period and achieve our objectives this season,' Sarr concluded.

Sarr's individual stock has never been higher, evidenced by his inclusion on the 10-man shortlist for the African Player of the Year award. The ceremony, set for December 7 in Lagos, follows a remarkable year where he was named the Conference League Player of the Season. His nine goals in that competition were instrumental in securing Crystal Palace's first-ever major European trophy, a feat that also earned him both the Fans' and Players' Player of the Year awards at the club.

His dominance has extended to the international stage as well, with Sarr playing a key role for Senegal at the 2026 World Cup, where he scored four goals. The forward had also featured in January's controversial Africa Cup of Nations final against Morocco, whose victory was initially overturned in March after Senegal's players walked off the pitch without the referee's permission. Senegal have since appealed to the Court of Arbitration for Sport (CAS) in an attempt to reclaim the title, but Sarr remains proud of his team's performance, insisting they 'became the African champions on the pitch'.

#2854. The 76ers are fun again, so it's the return of Roob's 76ers Stats!

The 76ers are fun again, so it's the return of Roob's 76ers Stats! originally appeared on NBC Sports Philadelphia

This 76ers team is a heck of a lot of fun to watch and fun basketball means fun stats!

At 13-3, the 76ers are exactly a month into the 5th-best start in franchise history and best since they were 14-2 in 2000-01. Which turned out to be a pretty good year. They were 15-1 in 1949-50 and 1966-67 and 14-2 in 1981-82.

To help everybody get through this interminable two-day gap between 76ers games, here's our first 2026-27 installment of Roob's 76ers Stats, with a tip of the hat to Stathead.

→ The 76ers have been out-rebounded by 34 through 16 games (719-685) and their 13-3 record matches the best in NBA history by a team that's been out-rebounded after 16 games. Three other teams have been 13-3 despite getting out-rebounded, most recently the 2013-14 Miami Heat. The others are the 1979-80 Bucks and 2012-2013 Spurs. The 76ers are an NBA-best 6-3 this year when they've been out-rebounded (which means they're 7-0 when they have a rebounding edge).

→ In the win over Orlando Thursday, V.J. Edgecombe had 24 points, eight rebounds, six assists and two steals, and Joel Embiid had 26 points, six rebounds, seven assists and two steals. It was the first time two 76ers had at least 24 points, six rebounds and assists and two steals in a game in 53 years. In a win over the Celtics at the Spectrum in 1973, Fred Carter had 31 points, six rebounds, six assists and seven steals and George McGinniss had 28 points, 16 rebounds, eight assists and three steals.

→ Kentavious Caldwell-Pope, a career 37 percent shooter from 3, is shooting 44 percent so far. Anfernee Simons, a career 38 percent shooter from 3, is shooting 41 percent. Jabari Walker, a career 33 percent 3-point shooter, is at 42 percent. Dean Wade, a career 37 percent shooter, is at 41 percent. Jaylen Brown, a career 36 percent shooter from range, is at 39 percent. The 76ers are shooting just 36 percent as a team from the stripe because James and Embiid have been so bad, but everybody else is off to career-best starts from 3-point range.

→ The 76ers are 7-1 on the road, equalling their best road record ever after eight games. They were also 7-1 in 1980-81, 1981-82 and 1982-83. The 1981 team had the best road start in franchise history, going 12-1 before losing its second road game.

→ With 15.7 points, 6.8 assists and 6.8 rebounds per game, LeBron James is only the fifth 76er ever to average at least 15 ½ points and 6 ½ assists and rebounds through 16 games. The others are some familiar names: Wilt Chamberlain in 1967, Charles Barkley in 1987, Ben Simmons in 2018 and James Harden in 2023.

→ The 76ers already have more wins this year after trailing by 10 points through the first quarter than all but two full seasons since 1950. They had four in 1985-86 and again in 2022-23. The Hawks also have three wins this year when trailing by double digits after the first quarter, but they're 3-4 and the 76ers are 3-2. So they actually have a winning record when they're down by double digits going into the second quarter.

→ With Jaylen Brown (22.6, 6.6), Joel Embiid (20.0, 8.3), LeBron James (15.7, 6.8) and V.J. Edgecombe (13.6, 6.5), the 76ers are the only NBA team with four players averaging 6 ½ rebounds and 13 points. The last time they had four players in a season who averaged 13 points and 6 ½ rebounds was 1961-62 when they were the Syracuse Nationals, with Dave Gambee (16.7, 7.9), Hal Greer (22.8, 7.4), Red Kerr (16.3, 14.7), Dolph Schayes (14.7, 7.8) and Lee Shaffer (16.9, 6.8).

→ Edgecombe's 6.5 rebounds per game are 2nd-most among shooting guards behind only the Grizzlies' Cedric Coward, who is at 8.5. Edgecombe's 6.5 is also 2nd-highest ever by a 76ers shooting guard behind only Greer's 7.4 during that 1961-62 season. Edgecombe averaged 5.6 rebounds as a rookie and his 5.8 career average is highest by a shooting guard who spent more than one year with the 76ers. Greer is second at 5.3. Among all NBA players who've played at least 90 games, Edgecombe's 5.8 average is 11th-highest all-time among shooting guards (although Stathead includes some swing guard-forwards in their data).

→ Joel Embiid's 14.6 shooting percentage from 3 is worst in NBA history after 16 games (minimum 40 attempts). Embiid is 6-for-41 from the stripe this year. The previous 3-point shooting low 16 games into a season belonged to Cedric Ceballos of the Lakers in 1995-96 and the 76ers' Evan Turner in 2013-14. Both were 6-for-43 for 16.3 percent.

→ LeBron James is at 22.2 percent from 3 on 10-for-45. The Sixers are the first NBA team in 12 years with two players under 23 percent after 16 games with a minimum of 40 attempts. In 2014 Tony Wroten was 10-for-50 (10 percent) and Turner was 6-for-43 (16.3 percent).

#2852. Jets improved their special teams coverage by having fullback Andrew Beck handle kickoffs

Only one kickoff has been returned for a touchdown in the NFL this season, and it was allowed by the Jets when kicker Austin McNamara not only failed to make the tackle, but got in the way of his teammates as they tried to run down Raiders return man Dylan Laube. That got the Jets thinking they needed to improve their kickoff coverage.

The solution was taking kickoff duties away from both McNamara, who is the Jets’ punter, and Jason Sanders, who is the Jets’ field goal kicker. Instead, it’s now fullback Andrew Beck, who has been a good special teams player for years in the NFL, who is handling kickoffs. Beck made a tackle last week on what could have been a much longer kickoff return without him, and he said he loves being able to both kick and make the tackle afterward.

“It was the whole reason that I’m out there, and I think it proves that it’s beneficial to have me doing it,” Beck told Newsday. “Instead of just to get some cool pictures, I’m actually out there doing it for a reason.”

Jets coach Aaron Glenn said when players have a skill set like Beck, with the ability to both kick and tackle, teams need to take advantage of that.

“He’s a unique player that has done a little bit of everything throughout his career,” Glenn said. “We want to utilize that as much as we can.”

Beck kicked in high school but not in college, and he didn’t think he’d be kicking again in the NFL. Now that he has the job, however, he hopes it increases his value to the team, and bolsters his career.

“I think I’ve shown that I can be successful in a couple different positions,” he said. “Hopefully the coaches feel comfortable putting me there.”

It’s almost unheard of for a player whose primary job isn’t punter or kicker to handle kickoffs, although with the dynamic kickoff rule promoting returns, it makes sense for a player who can make tackles to be out there if he’s capable of kicking the ball into the landing zone reliably. Saints safety Justin Reid has also kicked off, although he’s only done it once this season. If the Jets have success with Beck, it might be something that other teams try — and it might make players who have the unique skill set of being both good kickers and good tacklers more valuable.

#2850. Shane Steichen on Daniel Jones remaining the starter: "We'll evaluate everything"

As the Colts continue to struggle offensively, they continue to cling to quarterback Daniel Jones.

Asked by reporters after the 26-3 loss to the Texans whether Jones will remain the starter, coach Shane Steichen said, “We’ll evaluate everything.”

The No. 2 quarterback is 2023 top-five pick Anthony Richardson. On Thursday night, he got a garbage-time drive in the fourth quarter. The three-and-out, which started at the Indianapolis 39, consisted of Richardson running for two yards and throwing two incomplete passes.

It was Richardson’s second appearance of the season; in Week 8, he got a couple of mop-up drives when the Colts trailed the Jaguars, 42-7.

At some point, Richardson deserves a real chance to play. How much worse can the Colts’ offense be?

The Colts were outgained by the Texans, 354 yards to 167. They converted one of 11 third-down tries. Jones completed 12 of 27 passes, for 144 yards, no touchdowns, and an interception.

There’s a business reason that points to the Colts eventually getting Jones off the field. His contract has a $10.51 million injury guarantee for 2027. It vests in March. They’ll inevitably bench Jones to keep him from getting an injury that would keep the Colts from releasing him before the injury guarantee converts to a full guarantee.

That’s what the Giants did to Jones two years ago. He then requested, and received, his release.

The same thing could be happening, sooner rather than later, for Jones in Indianapolis. Which means that he could be available to another team.

That possibility highlights the Colts’ decision to not trade him for whatever they could get. And maybe they tried.

Maybe, in the end, his availability in trade is what helped the Vikings keep the Raiders’ asking price for Kirk Cousins to a third-round pick.

And maybe, in the end, the Vikings will have a chance to add Jones to the depth chart while showing Carson Wentz the door.

#2849. Observations after Sixers come back for strong shorthanded victory

Observations after Sixers come back for strong shorthanded victory originally appeared on NBC Sports Philadelphia

The Sixers stretched their winning streak to four games and snagged a strong road win Wednesday night over the Magic.

They earned a 121-116 victory in Orlando to improve to 13-3 on the season. The Magic dropped to 6-8.

Joel Embiid had 26 points, seven assists, six rebounds, three blocks and two steals.

VJ Edgecombe posted a season-high 24 points, eight boards and six assists.

LeBron James scored 22 points and Anfernee Simons added 18.

Magic star Franz Wagner had 27 points, 11 assists and eight rebounds.

The Sixers had a long injury list in Orlando. Tyrese Maxey (right foot soreness), Jaylen Brown (left hamstring strain) and Dean Wade (left hamstring soreness/tightness) were among their players out. Sixers head coach Nick Nurse told reporters in Orlando that Brown will be re-evaluated when the team returns to Philadelphia.

Orlando was without star forward Paolo Banchero (right high ankle sprain).

The Sixers will start a three-game homestand when they face the Bucks on Saturday afternoon. Here are observations on their win over the Magic:

The Sixers' undermanned starting lineup was Edgecombe, Simons, Kentavious Caldwell-Pope, James and Embiid.

After scoring 37 points in the Sixers' win over the Nets, Embiid had a subpar start. He began 1 for 6 from the floor and committed a turnover that led to an Anthony Black dunk.

The Sixers' lineup looked exactly like a unit that hadn't built any chemistry yet. The team was not sharp early on either end of the floor.

Desmond Bane drained a long three-pointer to give Orlando a 27-12 advantage. Orlando made 11 of its first 14 field goals and led by as many as 17 points in the first quarter.

Labaron Philon Jr. and Justin Edwards joined the Sixers' rotation. So did two-way contract players Tacko Fall and Caleb Love.

Philon impressed in the first quarter, converting two floaters and a corner three. The rookie passed his previous NBA scoring high of five points and finished the night with 10 on 4-for-5 shooting.

Fall played 10 minutes and posted two points, three rebounds and three blocks. He slammed in a dunk and rejected two shots early in the second quarter. Credit to Fall for giving the Sixers a good backup center performance and doing his job as a rim-protecting ultra-big man.

Love entered in the second quarter and was ready to fire, hitting a catch-and-shoot three-pointer on his first touch.

The Sixers' zone defense also helped them eat into the Magic's lead. The zone seemed to disrupt Orlando's offensive rhythm and it was active enough to prevent the Magic from consistently creating high-quality looks.

Edgecombe's buzzer-beating mid-range jumper cut the Sixers' deficit to 69-64 after two quarters.

The Sixers pulled within a single point on multiple occasions in the third quarter, including on a Philon three late in the period. They trailed by five points after the third.

Fall continued to play well early in the fourth quarter. He deterred the Magic's drivers from getting all the way to the rim as the very large back line of the Sixers' zone defense and made eye-catching hustle plays on both sides of the ball.

The Sixers outscored Orlando by seven points on the night with Fall on the court. Soon after Embiid subbed back in, he evened the game at 104-all with two free throws. Jabari Walker swished a three to finally push the Sixers in front.

The Magic regained a late lead with a Jalen Suggs three. James then drove and kicked the ball out to Edgecombe, who nailed one of his customary clutch jumpers to put the Sixers back on top.

No matter the night-to-night personnel, the Sixers have been fantastic at the tail end of close games. They're now 11-0 in clutch situations, which the NBA defines as games within a five-point differential in the final five minutes.

#2848. NFL's most surprising units, and one massive disappointment: Where Packers, Steelers and Titans (yes, really) are on the rise

The NFL season is always chock-full of surprises and disappointments. Despite a half-year of ranking and projecting and predicting how the NFL season will go from fans, media, and league personnel alike, the bullseye on the dartboard that we call a typical NFL season is nigh impossible to hit. And pulling a Luke Littler on those predictions is essentially unfathomable.

Now that we're past the halfway point in the season (enjoy those sloppy island games while you can!), I wanted to give a few of the most pleasant surprises from this NFL season, with a few disappointments that have left us with a sour taste in our mouths, a week before Thanksgiving.

With apologies to units like the Vikings defense, Chiefs offense, or others at the top of the boards, I narrowed this down to units where the expectations weren't that high or fell way below their projected status. So I excluded the Cowboys' defense despite it falling short of its already reasonably low expectations.

Jonathan Gannon seemed like a shrug of the hire for plenty of people after his tenure in Arizona collapsed under a mountain of injuries, inconsistency and a brutally competitive division. While the Packers had an interesting safety trio and young talent on their defensive front, Micah Parsons was lined up to miss the first half of the season after his ACL injury in the 2025 season. It was hard to feel confident they would have enough to overcome their potential lack of needle-movers at cornerback and unproven talent in their front seven.

Ten weeks into this 2026 season, and the Packers' defense has been not only surprisingly competent under Gannon, but outright good. The Packers' defense ranks sixth in FTN's defensive DVOA stat and ninth in EPA per play allowed. While their overall efficiency is more middle-of-the-pack and their run defense is definitely still a weakness, ranking 26th in rushing success rate allowed and 30th in EPA per rush (but 16th in DVOA, which adjusts for opponents), there is still plenty of good going on with this defense. And that sets the table nicely for Parsons' return that's currently projected for Thanksgiving eve.

Why has the defense leveled up? The young pass rushers have all had standout moments this season, with Lukas Van Ness making a huge leap that is forcing offenses to account for him in both the run game and pass protection. Van Ness has a 16.7% pressure rate, just above names like Aidan Hutchinson and Danielle Hunter, along with six sacks and nine stuffs in the run game. Rookie Dani Dennis-Sutton has not only been playable, but an impactful player at that. Dennis-Sutton has 30 pressures this year and his 18.2% pressure rate currently ranks eighth among all edge defenders with 100 or more pass rushes this season. Brenton Cox Jr. has had standout moments, too, and even Barryn Sorrell has held his own.

Both safeties, Xavier McKinney and Evan Williams, are playing at an All-Pro caliber level, with Williams truly emerging as a star by making impact plays in coverage and filling against the run.

Gannon has also been aggressive as a play designer and play-caller, too. Just look at the Williams interception in the play above, with Williams flying from the line of scrimmage to the back end to make the Troy Polamalu-esque play. The Packers blitz on 42.3% of late-down dropbacks, the fifth-highest rate in the NFL, per TruMedia. Gannon is constantly testing offensive lines and quarterbacks with pressure packages that are wildly different than the fairly vanilla scheme he ran in Philadelphia, which is a reminder that Gannon has a background under Mike Zimmer.

This defense has flaws, with a run unit that's still a bit leaky against offenses that are willing to pound it on them, and that's before getting to the issues with the Packers' own offensive line and run game. But this defense is still markedly better than many projected going into the season, with a 250-pound-sized reinforcement soon arriving to add gasoline to the fire that Gannon is throwing at offenses.

So, the Titans aren't very good. I don't think that's a big surprise in Robert Saleh's first year. The Titans' offensive line has been one of the underwhelming units in Tennessee over the past few seasons, and that's putting it nicely, despite a substantial amount of resources allocated to that unit in the draft and free agency and long-time offensive line coach Bill Callahan previously overlooking the group.

But, through 10 weeks, the Titans' offensive line has played like one of the most cohesive fronts in the league, using a sound scheme that has had actual results to go along with it.

Check out Austin Schlottmann (51), a new face at the pivot spot, pulling there!

Former first-rounder JC Latham has taken a big step forward this season under Carmen Bricillo, the Titans first-year offensive line coach who followed Brian Daboll to Nashville after their time together with the Giants. Latham has been a weapon in the run game, truly caving in the defense at the point of attack like a bouncer removing a rowdy patron and carving out clear lanes for Tony Pollard on the ground. Latham is just one example of the improvement the Titans front has had this year. Their run game has taken a step forward, going from a 35.1% success rate on running back carries in 2025, to a 38.3% rate this year. Explosives on designed runs, however, have jumped from 4.9% to 9.2% this season. They've gone from 28th in both categories in 2025 to 14th in success rate and fifth in explosive designed runs so far this season. Some of that is Pollard, sure, but explosives come in the run game when blockers are getting a hat on a hat on their assignments, something the Titans do, at the very least, an above-par rate.

Pass protection has improved this season, too. Dan Moore looks more solid than horrific, his pressure rates allowed dropping to more manageable levels. Moore was attributed with allowing 13.5 one-on-one sacks in 2025. Yes, 13.5. It's down to just 2.5 this year, which has been notable.

That's a combination of protections that are well thought-out and quarterback Cam Ward becoming a more decisive player as his second year has progressed. Ward was one of the most sacked players in the NFL last season and the Titans lost -109 total EPA to sacks alone last season. That's nearly a full touchdown of expected points lost to just sacks per game. This season, the Titans are seventh in sack rate (which is sacks per pass attempt) after ranking 27th last year and have lost just -23 EPA to sacks this season, or -2.6 EPA per game. This section is all about pleasantness, and I have to say that the Titans' offense has turned into one of those over the past few weeks or so. Pollard has been a joy to watch amid trade rumors. Ward has stacked together positive performances and looks closer to solidifying himself as the Titans quarterback of the future than relinquishing it, and how he's cut down on the damage taken on sacks is a clear indicator of that. His throwing creativity and underrated pocket-navigation ability is only adding to his appeal. What has helped Ward's feet calm down this year, which has only helped improve his consistency, is the competence he's receiving up front. And Ward's timing helps their jobs as well. It's as Mac and Dennis from "It's Always Sunny in Philadelphia" would say: a self-sustaining economy.

One disclaimer before I wrap up this part is how this unit might finish with Peter Skoronski heading to injured reserve, especially right before a matchup with Cowboys star Quinnen Williams. But so far the Titans' offensive linemen have all played to their best abilities under Bricillo and Daboll, so it'll be interesting to see how they navigate another new face along the interior.

Aaron Rodgers still likes to have his moments of playing sheriff and attempting to beat modern defenses with quick passes and a few too many go balls on the outside to DK Metcalf, but the Steelers have successfully started to lean into their burly offensive line and jumbo-sized skill position players. After swapping out the right side of their offensive line with guard Brock Hoffman and first-round selection Max Iheanachor at tackle in Week 3, the Steelers' ground attack started to overwhelm opponents. Particularly when using a duo run scheme.

The Steelers rank ninth in rushing success rate on running back runs, first in EPA per run play, and fourth in rushing DVOA. 10.6% of their designed runs have gone for an explosive gain (12 or more yards), the highest mark in the league. Jaylen Warren's 719 rushing yards ranks seventh in the NFL (with only two touchdowns because of Rodgers' tendency to opt for passing options in the red zone) and Rico Dowdle has done a nice job spelling him. Tight end Darnell Washington, aka "The Mountain That Rides," is a weapon in the run game and the Steelers' receivers are all strong and willing to scrap with defenders, and all have good size and strength to create a real advantage. Rookie Germie Bernard is going to be a favorite of coaches, quarterbacks and fans for a long time with his intelligence and sheer soundness in every aspect of playing the game, too.

I still have frustrations about how often the Steelers will get away from the run game at times, but when they embrace it it's a really enjoyable unit, led by star center Zach Frazier, who's playing like an All-Pro this season, and an emerging left side of Troy Fautanu and Mason McCormick (before he went to injured reserve with a dislocated ankle).

The last time a Todd Bowles-led Buccaneers defense was in the top five in defensive DVOA was all the way back during the Buccaneers' Super Bowl run in 2020. The Buccaneers' defenses never bottomed out over that time period, consistently being tough to run on with Vita Vea in the middle and Bowles' knuckleball game plans still giving offensive coordinators and quarterbacks fits from time-to-time. But they never felt like an upper echelon unit as long-time players aged out or left in free agency and injuries hit as well.

So far in 2026, the Buccaneers' defense has been one of the needle-moving units so far this season. It's a group helping the Buccaneers stay afloat along with a flourishing run game that has mitigated Baker Mayfield's injury and overall sub-par quarterback play. Pick a metric, and the Buccaneers are on top. Tampa Bay ranks first in DVOA and EPA per play (including first against the pass and run). They have yielded just 79.1 rushing yards per game this season which, yes, is the fewest among all NFL defenses. They stuff the run game and have generated the most takeaways in the NFL this season, with 21. Success rate wise, offenses are only succeeding on 30.1% of running back runs so far this year, the second-lowest mark in the league. The Buccaneers have never been as efficient or dynamic against the pass under Bowles tenure, either, all while blitzing at their highest rate since 2019.

New faces and young talent improving have helped spark this improvement. Rookie Josiah Trotter has fit this defense like a glove and Alex Anzalone has also improved the linebacker unit. Zyon McCollum and Jacob Parrish have been a strong pair as outside cornerbacks. The Buccaneers have an island of misfit toys for their pass rushers (complimentary), even with rookie first-round pick Rueben Bain Jr. off to a slow start. Second-year player David Walker has been really flashing when he's been on the field, so make sure to give him a gander when you check out the Bucs defense flying around.

Really, there's only one unit for this section. And this could be an entire article in itself. The formula for the Chargers' offense being a needle-moving unit this season seemed simple: Justin Herbert + Mike McDaniel's scheme + Jim Harbaugh overseeing the operation = profit.

And, well, instead of swimming in gold coins like Scrooge McDuck, the Chargers are instead having a season of Black Thursdays. The Chargers are dead last in points per drive (1.36) and the rate stats all paint a picture of a season from hell. The Chargers are bad on early downs (31st in first-down rate on early downs), bad on late downs (dead last in third-down conversion rate), situational football (29th in red zone efficiency), they turn the ball over on 3.4% of their plays (highest rate in the league), and to top it off Herbert has been hit or sacked on nearly a fifth of his dropbacks (18.3%, ranking 31st) this season. Which is actually something that feels more normal for the Chargers' offensive operation over the past few seasons. Whether it's efficiency or explosiveness, the Chargers have been outright poor this season. They rank 31st in overall success rate, only above the Eagles' offense having its own season from hell, and are dead last in passing success rate. The Chargers have only racked up an explosive gain on 8.3% of their plays this season, which ranks 31st. All this makes for a team that ranks dead last in EPA per play.

I could keep going. It's all bad, with nothing really to hang their hat on other than getting RB Omarion Hampton going a little bit recently, including a 105-yard performance in a Week 10 win over the Ravens.

Mistakes hit this unit and position group with such regularity, it feels like a small miracle when the Chargers are even able to execute a play properly. Receivers are often redirected on routes or just end up with improper spacing in the passing game, sometimes with multiple players ending up in the same spot like they're about to play a game of red rover. Multiple blockers are too often going to the wrong assignment or to no assignment at all. For an offense that uses formational variety and tons of motion before the snap, Chargers players seem to be stretched too thin mentally, which has led to mistakes compounding any talent disadvantages they have (and boy do they have those).

And even when those things do go right, Herbert is making weirdly too aggressive and incorrect decisions — he ranks last among qualifying quarterbacks in turnover rate — or a Chargers pass-catcher just drops the ball, sometimes even giving the football a volleyball set to a defender (the Chargers have 15 drops this year, by the way, which ranks 26th). Herbert's own spiraling this year has been a week-to-week car crash that is hard to look away from. His big arm and longer throwing motion haven't mixed well with McDaniel's preferred quick-hitters off play action, and the lack of spacing has compounded the forays he has tried down the field. For an offense that seeks explosives, something McDaniel was able to seemingly generate by the dozen in Miami, it feels very tight, static, and just slow. And with nothing to alleviate the lack of spacing and separation in the passing game, and a run game that ranks bottom 10 in success rate and explosives, a pressing Herbert has spiraled the Chargers' offense into something like Charybdis.

#2847. Hawkeye Football: Updated Game Line, Weather Forecast for Iowa vs. Illinois

We're back after what can only be described as a disappointing midweek development with the playoff rankings. Iowa has two games remaining to make a final playoff push, starting tomorrow in Champaign-Urbana.

For the Saturday before Thanksgiving, the weather in Champaign looks absolutely majestic. If you've been to a game at Illinois before, you know it can get cold and windy. That will not be the case tomorrow, at least not with the temperature, as temperatures at kickoff will be right at 70 degrees and sunny. There will be wind from the south around 12 miles per hour, though that forecast looks better than it did early in the week. Memorial Stadium can be a wind tunnel so that's something to watch. Otherwise the weather looks great.

Have you seen Illinois's injury report? Geez. And here we thought Iowa's was a bit of a mess going into the Northwestern game two weeks ago.

However…did you all see Tyler Barnes's tweets during the week after those sham rankings came out? He's effectively Kirk's mouthpiece on that and his tweets were the Iowa version of going off. Kirk is publicly saying that the rankings don't affect him. I believe that…to a point. We know full well that when something gets him irritated, he doesn't let it go. Ever. The comments from Shawn Eickhorst still ring in his ears and that was a dozen years ago now.

He knows he's got a good team this year and they're being disrespected. He knows, deep down, they have to put a number up on someone. With Illinois's injury issues on offense, I have a hard time seeing them get enough points on the board to beat Iowa, and I think this team is ready to go. They're hungry. They're about to share another data point with Oregon and I think Iowa gets a tally in their favor with a 27-10 win in the same building Oregon lost in a month ago. If they can get the score up into the 30s, they should. You know the drill with the Hawks – keep your guard up. But Iowa gets a win here, comfortably, to set up a huge game with Nebraska next week.

#2843. Anthropic was supposed to save the AI trade. What went wrong?

Anthropic's historic IPO — the largest in U.S. history — delivered a big win for the company and its investors. But it failed at another task: reviving animal spirits in the AI trade.

The Nasdaq Composite closed down 2.25% amid a broader market pullback with the S&P 500 declining 2% to 7,175.

Investors had been looking forward to the Anthropic IPO as a "clearing event" — reviving the tech sector after months of choppy trading, the Nvidia espionage scandal, and growing concerns that AI chip sales to Asia are likely to slump as Washington cracks down on potential violations of export controls.

Some of those worries resurfaced Thursday as a short seller, Hunterbrook Media, alleged in a report that firmware sold by chipmaker AMD had turned up in U.S.-blacklisted companies. AMD stock closed down more than 7%. The chipmaker told CNBC it complies with U.S. law.

Adding to the worries, the House Foreign Affairs Committee released documents showing that a subagency of the Commerce Department approved the sale of more than 879,000 AI chips to Chinese companies. That also raised the prospect of an Asia sales slowdown under stricter export controls.

Many investors and analysts continue to express confidence in the AI trade, noting that Anthropic's successful market debut — the stock closed up 17% from its IPO price and raised more than $81 billion — bodes well for capital spending across tech.

But investors acknowledged that this is a high-tension moment and said it's time to be more strategic about the trade.

Dan Niles, founder of Niles Investment Management, described himself as a "circumspect bull," making the comparison to market conditions in 2022 when the big indices tanked in the back half of the year.

"You could have still made money that year. You just needed to have the right combination of longs and shorts," he said.

Part of the challenge, Niles said, has been a price war between the two cutting-edge frontier labs, Anthropic and OpenAI. That has posed a threat to the overall revenue and profit potential of AI and the capex going into it. Fierce competition on price is producing collective losses between the two top companies, he notes.

"The combined revenue growth for OpenAI plus Anthropic went from growing 75% sequentially in Q2 to 48% in Q3," he told CNBC. "The combined losses went from $7.9 billion to about $8.4 billion from Q2 to Q3."

While uncertainty is high now, some resolution may be on the horizon if a greater degree of commercial and regulatory clarity emerges over the next few months.

Trading experts also told CNBC that the tech sector's troubles Thursday were a more serious matter than Anthropic simply siphoning capital from other parts of the market.

"There's a reset of AI in general," said JJ Kinahan, senior vice president at Cboe Global Markets.

Investors for years have profited in tech by buying the dip, he said, but "there's been an attitude change that [you] can't buy things blindly and they'll all go up." Nvidia's ongoing declines have shaken confidence in the buy-the-dip strategy, he said.

Anthropic's next few trading days will be a key gauge of the market's strength. Friday will be a "wild card," Kinahan said. It could be a "final flush out" for the market, or a bounce that leaves the market in "no man's land" while volatility remains elevated.

A better gauge may be Monday, when options on Anthropic start trading, providing insight into whether retail investors will back the company in force — providing more of a vote of confidence in the AI trade.

"If people think we're going up [on Anthropic] they'll buy stock, but retail particularly will just buy calls," he said. "There's a bigger test coming Monday."

For now, investors continue to sell AI-related stocks.

Neoclouds, the generally highly indebted AI-dedicated computing providers at the outer edges of the AI trade, were down substantially Thursday, with CoreWeave closing down more than 6%.

Hyperscalers such as Alphabet and Amazon.com, viewed as relatively defensive AI bets, also finished lower.

One bright spot: Cybersecurity stocks continued to rally, with Palo Alto Networks and CrowdStrike both closing up. AI is finding security holes across software infrastructure, creating a scramble to patch the vulnerabilities before hackers can exploit them.

— CNBC's Daren Fonda contributed reporting.

#2841. Victor Wembanyama, NBA's dunk king, turns to the 'shunk' due to painful blister on hand

Victor Wembanyama has found a new way to shoot in order to give his hands a rest and prevent future blisters.

It's called the "shunk," as San Antonio Spurs color commentator Sean Elliott put it. It's basically the 7-foot-4 Wembanyama "dunking" the ball through the basket like a jump shot.

Wembanyama showed off the "shunk" during the Spurs' 124-112 win over the Los Angeles Clippers on Thursday. The two-time All-Star posted 32 points, 11 rebounds and 1 "shunk."

"I'm not going to lie," Wembanyama said afterward via ESPN. "Actually, I've had this blister forever, and it's killing me. And every time I dunk, it kills me. I've been dragging this blister for I don't know how long because of dunking. So, I did not [quit dunking], but if I can avoid it, I would."

Through 16 games, Wembanyama leads all players with 72 dunks — Thursday's "shunk" was counted as a dunk by the NBA. The next closest is Giannis Antetokounmpo of the Miami Heat, who has 49. The Spurs star is on pace for 369 dunks this season, which would pass Rudy Gobert's record of 306 he amassed during the 2018-19 season.

Was the "shunk" the shortest jump shot Wembanyama has ever attempted?

#2838. Colts release of Daniel Jones makes trade deadline decisions look so much worse

Colts release of Daniel Jones makes trade deadline decisions look so much worse originally appeared on The Sporting News. Add The Sporting News as a Preferred Source by clicking here.

The Indianapolis Colts have played in 11 games this season, and the team is 3-8. Following a brutal 26-3 loss to the Houston Texans in Week 11, this Colts team is obviously one of the worst teams in the league.

One day later, the Colts released quarterback Daniel Jones. It's a massive decision, and one that helps paint the team's trade deadline in a different light.

As NFL insider Jordan Schultz highlighted, the Colts had a chance to trade Jones at this year's trade deadline. By not doing so, along with a few other moves the Colts didn't make, their trade deadline looks a lot worse now than it did before.

"Sources: The Colts opted not to trade QB Daniel Jones despite receiving interest, believing they had already paid too much in signing bonus money," Schultz reports.

The Colts didn't want to trade Jones at the November 10 NFL trade deadline, and now 10 days later, they've cut him from their roster.

Indianapolis might've been able to get something, even if it was just a late Day 3 pick, back for Jones had they traded him. Instead, they've cut him and won't get anything in return.

That's a brutal turn of events for the Colts, and it's clearly making the team's trade deadline look worse. But there are two other key non-trades this team made that look worse after their Week 11 loss.

MORE: Jim Harbaugh's record by year: How 1-8 Chargers compare to worst seasons in NFL, college

Jordan Shchultz reported on NFL trade deadline day that running back Jonathan Taylor wasn't being traded, even though they had "multiple offers of a 2nd-round pick and more" for Taylor.

The decision not to trade Taylor for that strong return looks even worse now with how much Taylor has struggled this past week. He ran for 21 yards on 11 attempts against the Texans, and it is a sign of a missed opportunity at the deadline for the Colts.

Fortunately, the Colts do have Taylor under contract beyond 2026, and he's still a very talented player, so not trading him isn't a total disaster. The same cannot be said for defensive tackle Grover Stewart.

MORE: One play, two devastating injuries: How Aidan Hutchinson's Drake Maye sack shakes up AFC, NFC playoff races

Schultz reported on deadline day that Stewart wasn't traded by the Colts, despite the fact that they reportedly had a strong offer on the table.

"Sources: The Colts had an offer for DT Grover Stewart last week involving a 4th-round pick and another late-round pick, but waited on it and it was ultimately pulled," Schultz reports.

The Colts could've traded Stewart for a fourth-round pick and more, but waited too long to accept the offer on the table. That was obviously a huge missed opportunity, as Stewart is a free agent after the 2026 season.

Whatever compensatory pick they could have gotten for Stewart likely would not be better than the fourth-rounder from whichever interested team tried to acquire the defensive tackle.

Passing on that trade was a mistake, as they wound up keeping Stewart despite a deal being in the team's best interests.

MORE: Grading NFL trade deadline debuts, from Kirk Cousins' Vikings return to Josh Downs' impressive day

The decision not to trade away Taylor can be defended, but it was still arguably a bad decision not to get the guaranteed value of a second-rounder and more this season.

Not trading Stewart is a much bigger blunder for the Colts, as the reported trade that was on the table was obviously one they should've accepted.

But the Colts' release of Jones might be the most egregious mistake. They had a chance to trade Jones, getting out of his contract and getting draft capital in return. Instead, they turned down offers and kept him.

Just 10 days later, the Colts released Jones, which has made an already questionable trade deadline look a whole lot worse.

While they got decent value for the players they dealt, there was a lot of draft capital left on the table that the Colts could've had at this year's NFL trade deadline.

#2836. Cathie Wood’s Ark Invest was a big buyer of Anthropic as she tells CNBC she’s ‘quite optimistic’

Cathie Wood was a major buyer of Anthropic following its debut Thursday on the Nasdaq, adding shares in all her major funds.

Wood's firm, which already owned private shares of the artificial intelligence company in its Ark Venture Fund leading up to the initial public offering, bought $90.8 million worth of Anthropic in six funds, according to a trade notification from the firm.

"We've owned Anthropic since early '23," Wood told CNBC's "Squawk Box" on Friday. "It's our best performing equity by far in ARKVX, our venture fund. ... Our confidence has grown with time as it really took over the enterprise" AI business.

Anthropic shares jumped 17% in their first day, but closed well off the highs of the session and were headed for sizable losses Friday. The Nasdaq, which was expected to get a boost from a major IPO reviving enthusiasm about the technology trade, fell by 2% on Thursday.

"We're climbing a wall of worry," said Wood. "Everybody is worried about AI hype and the political situation after the election, but I think we'll look back and say, OK this is more like the '80s and '90s, climbing a wall of worry you know with bricks much like what we have today: inflation, interest rates, wars, new technologies and so forth so quite optimistic here."

The Anthropic stakes are relatively small across the funds, but Wood typically will buy shares in companies she likes steadily during long stretches. Wood said she is "averaging down" with her purchases of Anthropic.

In her $6.1 billion Ark Innovation ETF, Anthropic is a 0.3% stake. The biggest holdings in that fund are Tesla and SpaceX, representing more than 21% of the fund together.

Ark Innovation, which retail investors piled into during the pandemic as it gained about 150% in 2020, tumbled the subsequent next two years, losing 67% in 2022 alone. Wood's following was dented after the big losses.

The fund had a comeback stretch as the bull market revved back up from 2023 to 2025, but has fallen on hard times again this year, down more than 10% in 2026. The S&P 500 is up 4.8% this year.

Wood dismissed comparisons of today's environment to the dot-com bubble.

"Back then if an Anthropic had gone public, it probably would have gone up threefold the first day," she said. "That's how crazy things were."

#2833. Why Daniel Jones is the QB Replacement the Patriots Need After Drake Maye Injury

Why Daniel Jones is the QB Replacement the Patriots Need After Drake Maye Injury originally appeared on NESN. Add NESN as a Preferred Source by clicking here.

The Patriots are in the market for a hurler after Drake Maye was ruled out most, if not all, of the 2026 season with a fracture in his right fibula. On Friday, a high-profile option presented itself in the most auspicious of circumstances.

The Colts released Daniel Jones on Friday, one day after he completed 12-of-27 passes for 144 yards, zero touchdowns and one interception in a loss to the Texans. Jones, 29, inked a two-year, $88 million extension with Indianapolis ahead of the season. His toils, marked by a league-high 13 interception tally, has made him the subject of scorn around much of the league.

Jones is hardly an eye-catching QB1 option at his point in his NFL career. Still, New England's threadbare QB room could push the team to attempt to gain his signature as the Patriots attempt to mount a charge on the AFC playoff picture without their best player.

With that, here's why New England should attempt to sign Jones.

MORE: Patriots Projected to Select New WR For Drake Maye in Latest ESPN 2027 Mock Draft

First, a caveat: Jones' time as an NFL starter appears to be over. He has posted below-average numbers across the board this season, falling well below the league average in yards per attempt, net yards per attempt, air yards per attempt, completion percentage, touchdown percentage, interception rate and passer rating this season, per Pro Football Reference. His Achilles injury, when coupled with his previous torn ACL, looks to have zapped most of his mobility.

While hardly a bastion of offensive alchemy, the Colts offense was the joint-second-worst in the league by EPA per offensive play under Jones' stewardship, according to PerThirtySix. He has shown little to suggest that he's capable of spearheading an offense for longer than a few games before unraveling.

Now, with that out of the way, here's another truth: Jones is the best option available in a poor crop of potential Maye replacements. While Jones has not looked an accomplished passer since before his Achilles tear, most of the options presentable to New England constitute career backups, including DeVito.

Including Jones, there are about a dozen quarterbacks on the market for NFL jobs, per Spotrac. Some, like Andy Dalton, are signed to practice squads. Others, like Jimmy Garoppolo, are currently unattached, although they're believed to be content with stepping away from the sport for good.

For all of Jones' faults, he's available and in football shape. He was the league's worst starter during his stint in Indianapolis this season. But he was still a starter, helping the Colts win games against the Texans, Commanders and Dolphins.

Josh McDaniels tinkered with his system ahead of Maye's arrival, making it more user-friendly. The tenets of his structure, which revolves around short- and intermediate passing, broadly remains the same, however. Jones struggles to push the ball down the field — of quarterbacks to attempt at least 20 passes, Jones posted the 11th-lowest intended air yards average this season, per Legion Report. That's a stark contrast from Maye, one of the sport's best deep-ball throwers.

Still, McDaniels looked well-equipped to adjust his system to fit Jones. Known as one of the sport's better offensive minds, the 50-year-old likely would prefer having a player who started nearly 100 games under center as opposed to DeVito.

Jones is not the quarterback he showed flashes of being at different points in his career. Still, if New England wants to take a flier on a cheap alternative to Maye, there are ample options less appealing than Jones.

#2832. As Anthropic shares tank, investors scramble to hedge. It could be very expensive

As Anthropic shares tumble toward their IPO price of $83, investors are looking for ways to hedge. Unfortunately, they may have to wait until Monday, when options on the AI giant are expected to list.

If Anthropic breaks its IPO price, it will represent a 27% decline from Thursday's intraday high of $113. The wild price swings offer clues on how expensive it might be to buy protection on the new tech giant once options begin trading.A 20% intraday move points to an implied volatility somewhere between 100 and 150 for Anthropic options, according to estimates from single-stock market-makers on the Cboe trading floor in Chicago.

Options traders are also looking at how SpaceX traded in the days following its offering for clues on how options might price.

Implied volatility in SpaceX started at 116, but that didn't deter options traders big and small from piling into the contracts tracking the stock, with about 1.7 million contracts traded on the first day for a total options premium of about $2.5 billion. Far out-of-the-money call contracts were some of the most popular trades as the stock soared 40% in its first three sessions.One key difference between now and then? Anthropic is trading against the backdrop of S&P 500 volatility close to 30, compared to 17.3 when SpaceX debuted. "I expect ANT options to be super active," said Tom Sosnoff, co-founder of the trading platforms ThinkOrSwim and TastyTrade. "I expect heavy options volume."One thing Anthropic and SpaceX have in common is heavy retail involvement in both IPOs, with retail brokerages receiving a 20% allocation from SpaceX and reports pointing to between a 15-20% allocation from Anthropic on Thursday. "Round one of the Anthropic IPO easily goes to the individual investor," Sosnoff added. "Retail participants bailed early at the best prices of the day while institutional investors paid up to top off their underwhelming allocations. I believe retail was aggressively reloading in the mid-90s."Further signs of retail demand will become evident when options likely begin trading on Monday. One thing to watch: the spread between out-of-the-money call and put contracts, which was heavily skewed toward calls in the case of SpaceX. "I would be careful comparing Anthropic to SpaceX," said Dennis Davitt, CIO and Co-Founder of Millbank Dartmoor Portsmouth. "From a market-structure perspective, the two are dramatically different. The availability and cost of borrowing will be different, the companies are at very different stages of maturity, and SpaceX has allowed employees to monetize portions of their holdings over time. All of those factors matter when pricing options."

#2830. Jon Horst has done enough damage

As the buzzer sounded against the Lakers last week to hand the Bucks their fourth straight drubbing, a range of feelings came over me: sadness, helplessness, anxiety, and, quite frankly, anger. It wasn't because the team was losing; I fully expected that. The first year of any rebuild always includes heaps of losing. No, it's *waves arms wildly* everything else that's going on—the bigger picture.

You see, what keeps supporters of a rebuilding team going is the future, but when you look at what the post-Giannis Bucks have—or, more importantly, what they don't have—there's certainly not heaps to get excited about. We know the draft is basically off the table until 2031, when Milwaukee regains its pick rights. The Bucks did get some potentially juicy Miami picks in the Giannis deal, which they could use to trade back into the upcoming drafts, but that remains theoretical; the only other avenue to get back in would be through spinning some or all of Tyler Herro, Jaime Jaquez Jr., and potentially even Ryan Rollins, but none of those guys will yield anything too flashy.

Among the players the Bucks have in-house as plausible future building blocks, Brayden Burries looks, at the very least, like a long-time starter, so that's comforting. Nate Ament is growing by the day—figuratively and maybe even literally—but it is still unclear whether he can transition from "intriguing talent" to "full-time winning player." I am getting more and more confident that Kasparas Jakucionis will become something, while Kel'el Ware, Ousmane Dieng, and Bogoljub Markovic remain much more of a question mark, and at this point, you'd probably bet against all three of them becoming long-term contributors. Finally, there's Ryan Rollins, who, at 24, may or may not be part of the future, but is someone I remain high on. That's… kinda it? Seven guys, most with a wide range of outcomes, and no blue-chip prospect as you enter the next four drafts without control over your first-round pick. Not ideal.

But it doesn't end there. Beyond the young core not being anywhere near as robust as you'd hope, Horst also made some cataclysmic free-agent signings that will further impede the rebuild. Exhibit A: Myles Turner, who signed to a four-year, $108m deal with Milwaukee last summer as a key part of Horst's last-ditch attempt to construct a winner around Giannis. Of course, to make this happen, the Bucks GM had to waive Damian Lillard and stretch the remaining $113m on his contract, putting $20m in dead cap space on Milwaukee's books for the next five seasons. Don't forget that Horst also had to send two second-round picks to Charlotte so they would absorb Pat Connaughton's contract, freeing up the requisite cap space to sign Turner.

LAS VEGAS, NV – JULY 11: Jon Horst and Doc Rivers of the Milwaukee Bucks Introduce Myles Turner during a Press Conference at the 2025 NBA Summer League game on July 11, 2025 at the Thomas & Mack Center in Las Vegas, Nevada. NOTE TO USER: User expressly acknowledges and agrees that, by downloading and or using this photograph, User is consenting to the terms and conditions of the Getty Images License Agreement. Mandatory Copyright Notice: Copyright 2025 NBAE (Photo by Ryan Stetz/NBAE via Getty Images) | NBAE via Getty Images

Suffice to say, getting Turner in the door was hard work! Surely Horst can trade him for something decent, given the extreme lengths he went to sign him? Nope. The 12-year veteran has been so diabolical that Milwaukee would struggle to even get neutral value for him at this point, with Brew Hoop founder Frank Madden recently calling Turner "easily the Bucks' worst ever free agent signing." Bravo. And hand up, after the decision was made that Giannis would not be traded, I was a fan of the Turner signing. I saw him play a key role on a team that had just gone to the NBA Finals and thought he would be Brook Lopez on steroids. Clearly, I was wrong. In my defence, I'm also not paid millions to have the purported ability to make these decisions.

And then there's the granddaddy of them all: circumventing the salary cap to sign Gary Trent Jr. No, you're not going delirious; you heard that right. Circumventing. The salary cap. To sign Gary Trent Jr. Predictably, just hours after Horst inked Trent to a definitely-not-shady $64m contract following a career-worst season, the NBA announced it would investigate the deal for possible cap circumvention and, according to Brian Windhorst, "everyone in the league expects a guilty finding." To add insult to injury, The Athletic reports that the penalty could be just as severe—if not more severe—than what the LA Clippers received.

Honestly, I'm not sure I'm ready to consider the degree to which any level of severe penalty could hamstring a small-market team like Milwaukee, which already traded most of its picks away. It would be franchise-altering for sure, and some would say franchise-dooming. All of this to secure the services of Gary… Trent… Junior. I keep repeating it because it almost doesn't sound real. Like, to be clear, GMs should always play by the rules, but to circumvent the cap for what? A ninth man on a good team? That's next-level foolish. At least the Clippers got their money's worth! Horst saw Trent averaging 14 and two in Toronto with non-existent defence and thought, "I simply must have this guy, and I'm willing to cheat to get his signature." What was he thinking?

So there. The Bucks are in a deep hole, and based on what we're seeing, it'll be a while before they can climb out. Of course, the main reason for that is that the org continually mortgaged the future for the present throughout the Giannis era, which, to be clear, I was largely on board with! I was in on mortgaging the future for Jrue Holiday. I was in on mortgaging the future once more for Damian Lillard (even if, in hindsight, way more consideration should have gone to whether Lillard was the right fit next to a superstar who lacked the exact intangibles needed to maximise him).

But you don't get carte blanche to operate that way forever. I know I've already bagged on about this, so I'll keep it brief, but Horst emptied the clip for Dame—that was (or should have been) his final bullet. Once the moment came when any reasonable person looked at Milwaukee and deduced that their title window had very likely closed, it was incumbent on the Bucks GM to capitalise on the one golden ticket he had left to salvage the future of this franchise after years of mortgaging it: trading Giannis from a position of strength. Unfortunately, that moment came when Lillard tore his Achilles less than two years after he arrived, much sooner than anyone had hoped.

MILWAUKEE, WISCONSIN – APRIL 27: Damian Lillard #0 of the Milwaukee Bucks is helped off the court after an apparent injury during the first quarter in Game Four of the Eastern Conference First Round NBA Playoffs against the Indiana Pacers at Fiserv Forum on April 27, 2025 in Milwaukee, Wisconsin. NOTE TO USER: User expressly acknowledges and agrees that, by downloading and or using this photograph, User is consenting to the terms and conditions of the Getty Images License Agreement. (Photo by Stacy Revere/Getty Images) | Getty Images

Still, the harsh reality wasn't going away, even if Horst wanted to ignore it and double down, which was exactly what he did. The product we're watching now—along with many of the restrictions that will hamstring the org's ability to improve it in the future—is largely a direct result of that decision, which forced Horst into negotiating from a position of weakness when he did pull the trigger on a Giannis trade.

And look, I understand and respect the opposing point of view: that it was always worth keeping your franchise GOAT for one final run while at least keeping the door ajar for Horst to pull another rabbit out of his hat that would convince the two-time MVP to re-sign. I get it. I would just say the NBA moves incredibly fast, and if you do not capitalise on opportunities when they are presented, you will get left behind—that's exactly what I fear has happened to Milwaukee by playing out the string with Giannis. In any event, and regardless of the decision to keep Giannis for that final season, enough evidence has piled up to show Horst lacks the poise and decision-making chops to do this job.

We've talked ad-nauseum about the nuances of Horst's career in the big chair. He's certainly had some major wins, and not all the losses people blame on him were actually his fault. Of course, he deserves immense credit for building the nucleus that ended a 50-year title drought in Milwaukee. Immense. But you can't dine out on that forever while ignoring the litany of blunders since then: the coaching hires, draft misses, contract misevaluations, and worst of all, the bewildering willingness to play Russian roulette in pursuit of signing wholly inconsequential players. Heck, the cap circumvention is a fireable offence on its own, let alone all the other stuff!

The truth is that Horst's decisions since the title weren't those of a level-headed operator; they were the moves of a riverboat gambler desperately chasing losses with bigger, bolder bets, the stakes rising with each slipup. Loss after loss after loss, culminating in what I am comfortable labelling one of the worst and most inexplicable gambles in NBA history. A decision, mind you, that fans will be forced to bear the brunt of as their team gets pushed even further down the already-deep hole Horst put it in.

Look around. The most successful teams share one thing: competent management. It's no coincidence that the Thunder, time after time, hit on the exact same picks the Bucks routinely miss on; it's no coincidence that the Celtics consistently take other teams' trash and turn it into their treasure. Equally, it's no coincidence that the Kings and Pelicans have spent decades as the laughingstock of the league. Competent management matters, and if the Bucks are serious about being taken seriously, the first step seems quite obvious.

#2829. Darian Mensah not a finalist for Johnny Unitas Golden Arm Award, and it’s seemingly for a silly reason

I don't often opine on the consideration for or decisions regarding individual awards around here, particularly with articles of their own. But I saw something today that I wanted to use my platform to make very clear.

Miami Hurricanes QB Darian Mensah is a top Heisman Trophy finalist. He is the best quarterback in the nation. He leads FBS in completion percentage (76.3%) and QB rating (186.8). He's thrown for 3076 yards with 29 touchdowns and just two picks, which rank near the top of their respective categories. He's been elite in every sense of the word.

That said, he was absent from the list of five finalists for the Johnny Unitas Golden Arm Award. And Texas AB Arch Manning (LOL) was one of them.

As far as why he was excluded, the award lists among its criteria that no player shall have played for more than two programs, subject to "committee review in exceptional circumstances."

Well, that's funny. Cam Ward was a finalist two years ago, and he played for more than two programs. Yeah, I don't care if one was FCS. It doesn't matter. He played for more than two programs, which makes this whole thing seem stupid and arbitrary. It appears as though the award powers-that-be have a "holier than thou" attitude when it comes to "commitment & loyalty" of a player.

My problem is two-fold. First, Miami has proven that the transfer portal is a good thing, particularly at quarterback, and that players can find the best versions of themselves as players and teammates at a third school. Ward proved it. Carson Beck showed it last year in his second stop. And Mensah is an elite leader, quarterback and teammate at stop number three.

That's the transfer portal world that we live in nowadays, and it's the portal world at its finest in Coral Gables. Putting in a transfer limitation requirement while promoting commitment/loyalty/positive impact elsewhere creates an inference that these players do not possess it, and frankly, that's the wrong message to send.

But the message I want to make sure that voters for the Heisman Trophy, Maxwell Award, and other individual awards realize is: MENSAH DID NOT QUALIFY. I write this because sometimes you will see some players win the Unitas or Maxwell Award and go on to win the Heisman Trophy. NOT WINNING THE UNITAS AWARD DOES NOT MEAN MENSAH IS/WAS NOT THE BEST QB THIS YEAR. IT MEANS HE DID NOT MEET THE COMMITTEE'S PRETTY LITTLE CRITERIA REGARDING SUPPOSED LOYALTY THAT HAD NOTHING TO DO WITH HOW GREAT HE DID ON THE FIELD AND AS A TEAMMATE AND LEADER.

With that being said, #HeisMensah hype will continue on Saturday morning in Coral Gables, where ESPN College Gameday is taking place.

#2826. Prediction markets outlook for stocks in 2026 dims amid AI sell-off

As stocks continue their tech-driven sell-off, prediction markets traders are seeing a bleaker outlook for the remainder of the year.

On platform Kalshi, speculators place just 6% odds that the S&P 500 will cross 8,000 in 2026. On Oct. 13, those odds were as high as 72%.

Contracts ask traders if the S&P 500 will go above various levels in 2026, and they're resolved using data from S&P Dow Jones Indices.

On Friday, the Nasdaq Composite turned negative for the year as investors continued to dump tech stocks, primarily those with exposure to the artificial intelligence build-out. The Dow Jones Industrial Average and the S&P 500 fell into correction territory, too.

The S&P 500 has slashed its year-to-date gain to just over 2%.

The Stock Trader's Almanac in a Thursday note broadly agreed that the hopes for a December rally are fading, with the caveat that a "Santa rally" could still appear at the end of the year.

"The market is under pressure and more downside can't be ruled out," the note said.

The Stock Trader's Almanac sees 2026 following the year-end pattern of 2018, when the Federal Reserve was in a tightening cycle in a midterm year. The Fed is widely expected to hike rates at its December meeting.

In that scenario, the Stock Trader's Almanac thinks more churn is to come. "The low is more likely in mid- to late December than this week."
